#5957. Thomas Massie files articles of impeachment against Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth

Rep. Thomas Massie (R-KY), seen here on September 16, 2026 at the U.S. Capitol, filed articles of impeachment against Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth on Monday. Roberto Schmidt/Getty Images hide caption

Rep. Thomas Massie has reintroduced articles of impeachment against Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, setting up a vote in the House of Representatives in the next two days.

The Kentucky Republican's resolution accuses Hegseth of high crimes and misdemeanors and lists eight specific charges against him.

Pete Hegseth greets British Defense Secretary Wes Streeting at the Pentagon on Oct. 15, 2026. Hegseth says any effort to impeachment him will fail. Finn Gomez/Getty Images hide caption

Hegseth defended his record at the Pentagon in a video released last Thursday, dismissing Massie's effort as "sham articles of impeachment."

"Didn't work against President Trump and it won't work with me," Hegseth said.

Speaking to reporters during an impromptu press briefing on Monday, Trump expressed support for his embattled Defense Secretary.

"I'll stand by him," Trump said. "He won two wars. He was in charge. We all won."

Massie has long been a staunch critic of Hegseth. During the GOP primary, Hegseth campaigned on behalf of Ed Gallrein, who went on to defeat Massie.

In response to Hegseth calling him a "disgruntled defeated congressman," Massie told reporters on Monday: "Well, he's right about one thing — I'm a defeated congressman. I'm not disgruntled."

He added: "I'm being polite here. What is an impeachment? An impeachment is just firing someone, really. You don't go to prison, you don't go to jail, you don't pay a fine. When the Democrats get in charge, they are going to be very vicious."

Massie said the effort also underscores the need to litigate whether Congress has real war powers or not. The war powers resolution has largely been ignored by presidents of both parties.

"What's at heart here is the balance of power between the executive branch and Congress," he said.

Massie first sought to bring the impeachment resolution to the floor before the midterm elections, prompting Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La., to call an early recess to avoid the vote. That caused the resolution to expire, which is why Massie reintroduced the resolution Monday.

Presumptive next House Speaker Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., has already signaled support for an impeachment push against Hegseth.

Even Republican members who oppose impeaching Hegseth have said the Trump administration could do a better job making the case to Congress and the public about what the plan is for the Iran war going forward.

Rep. Warren Davidson, R-Ohio., indicated to NPR that impeachment would be hypocritical given that administrations going back to the Korean War have largely ignored Congress' war powers efforts.

"I think trying to scapegoat Pete Hegseth is just the status quo politics in Washington," he said. "I think it would be a mistake to impeach."

Davidson said lawmakers who want changes are better off trying to leverage the power of the purse to withhold money from the conflict.

There could be sufficient votes to impeach Hegseth in the House, with the support of all Democrats, Massie, and a handful of Republicans. However, it is unlikely to clear the higher Senate threshold for convicting and removing Hegseth from office — which requires two-thirds of the Senate.

NPR's Eric McDaniel contributed to this report.

#5949. France accuses Iran of cyber 'sabotage' in fridge attacks

A woman walks past empty refrigerators at an Intermarché supermarket in Névez, western France, on Oct. 12, following a cyberattack affecting the French supermarket chains Intermarché and Cooperative U that caused their refrigerators to defrost. FRED TANNEAU/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

France is accusing Iran of having launched a series of destructive cyberattacks against its critical infrastructure in October.

According to the French National Cybersecurity Agency, ANSSI, Iranian military hackers took over refrigeration controllers at around 700 different retail sites in France, from supermarkets to butcher shops, turning up the temperature in the middle of the night and spoiling millions of dollars' worth of food.

They also targeted several small hydroelectric power plants, resulting in "moderate effects," wrote ANSSI in its press release.

The hacking campaign was "unprecedented in both its scale and its level of aggression," said French Foreign Minister Jean-Noël Barrot. He said it "marks the first large-scale cyberattack aimed at sabotage on our national territory, and it constitutes a major attack on the nation's interests and the security of the French people."

The Paris Prosecutor's Office has opened its own investigation into the Iranian cyber unit, Shahid Kaveh, which the French government is linking to Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps. "The Paris Public Prosecutor's Office is requesting the issuance of international arrest warrants," the office said in a statement.

The attacks on French industrial controllers are part of a larger trend. In 2026 alone, hackers have targeted refrigeration controllers from Europe to Israel and the United States.

Grocery stores in European countries including Sweden and Ireland were hit in similar attacks last week.

In a separate incident in France, employees at a major French blood bank had to act fast to save blood samples from heating up when their refrigeration system was hit last week, prompting international calls for the health sector to protect its lifesaving products from spoiling.

In Israel in the spring, suspected Iranian hackers going by the name of "Cyber Isnaad Front" claimed responsibility for sabotaging an industrial food processing plant, including infecting its corporate computers with malware, taking over office printers to print out threatening messages and hacking office cameras for use in a propaganda video released in early October. That video included a taunt in French referencing ongoing education-related protests, and came just before the attacks on French supermarket fridges. Cyber incident response firm Profero attributed the Israel attack to a wing of Iran's Revolutionary Guard Corps.

Over the summer, commissaries on U.S. military bases also went through something similar, when food spoiled after the temperature shot up inside refrigerators and freezers overnight.

The French government is the first to formally assign blame for the wave of destructive attacks.

They were able to identify the hackers through a series of digital clues, including digital infrastructure used by the hackers, connection to a propaganda outlet, and "traces" of the use of Farsi language in the attack, according to ANSSI.

While NPR and others have reported that U.S. officials suspect Iran may have been behind the attacks on U.S. military bases in August, the Pentagon has not made any official statement, even to conclude that the disruptions were in fact the result of a cyberattack.

"Consistent with our policy and to preserve the integrity of the process, the DOD [Office of Inspector General] Criminal Investigative Service does not discuss ongoing investigations," Mollie Halpern, a spokesperson for the Department of War Office of the Inspector General wrote in an email to NPR.

If Iranian hackers are behind the attacks across Europe, the U.S. and Israel, cybersecurity experts say that would fit with what they know about Iranian hackers' strategy and goals.

"Iran's cyberattacks against refrigeration systems likely represents the IRGC attempting to reassert Iranian power abroad through both physically and psychologically disruptive operations," Alex Orleans, the head of threat intelligence at cybersecurity firm Sublime Security, told NPR. "Such activities continue to provide tangible and cost-effect results given how Iran's national resources remain tied down in the war."

"Also, Shahid Kaveh, the IRGC cyber unit that the French specifically attributed the disruptive attacks to, is repeating a pattern here," Orleans says. He referenced a series of cyberattacks on American water treatment plants in August, which most experts believe were linked to Iran, as precedent. The U.S. government has still not officially attributed those attacks.

"This unit has once again refined its tradecraft through attacks on Israeli targets before shifting their targeting to the same systems in the West," Orleans says.

For those tracking Shahid Kaveh and the other Iranian cyber units that operate alongside it, these attacks fit squarely into their modus operandi.

"Shahid Kaveh group is an offensive cyber unit responsible for some of the greatest hits of Iranian offensive cyber [operations]," says Ari Ben Am, an adjunct fellow at the Foundation for the Defense of Democracies' Center on Cyber and Technology Innovation.

One group tied to Shahid Kaveh is the Cyber Av3ngers, he explains, which the U.S. government linked to cyberattacks on Israeli-made systems in U.S. water treatment facilities in the winter between 2023 and 2024. The French government also cited other cyberattacks carried out on behalf of the IRGC against French interests, including against the satirical French magazine Charlie Hebdo and another against digital kiosks used at the Paris Olympics in 2024.

Cybersecurity experts say the attacks on refrigeration controllers have highlighted a yearslong difficulty with securing operational technology, the machines that control physical processes in the real world — from water pumps to cooling systems.

These machines are often outdated and riddled with vulnerabilities, and are very difficult to patch because operators can't afford to shut them down and disrupt critical services including healthcare and transportation. Additionally, the machines are often left exposed to the open internet — often at least in part due to contractual requirements with vendors that want to maintain those systems remotely. That combination of factors makes it a complicated problem to solve.

Iran has yet to respond to the accusations. However, Monday's accusation by the French government may lead other affected countries to take action as well.

During Monday's G7 meeting in Paris, Secretary of State Marco Rubio was asked by journalists whether the U.S. shared information with the French to help with their attribution of the Iranian hacks. Rubio said he couldn't comment, but noted that the "French are quite capable."

"What kind of sickos are these people?" he asked. "They attack refrigerators. France is not even involved in this, really," referring to the war between the U.S. and Iran, which began in February. "[These Iranians] do terrible things around the world … the news today doesn't surprise us," Rubio concluded.

#5945. California voters defeat billionaire tax proposal that divided Democrats

California voters rejected Proposition 40, the billionaire tax, which had divided Democrats with opponents saying it would drive the wealthy from the state. Jae C. Hong/AP hide caption

Midterms coverage: Live results | Stories around the countryStay connected: NPR Politics Podcast | NPR Politics newsletter | NPR app

California voters have rejected a proposal to charge billionaires a one-time 5% tax on most of their wealth, according to a race call by The Associated Press.

Proponents said the tax could raise $100 billion for healthcare. Led by a union that represents healthcare workers, the SEIU-UHW, advocates of the tax said the Republican-controlled Congress cut healthcare programs to give tax breaks to the wealthy, so it was time for the wealthy in California to fill the gap. The state has more than 200 billionaires, according to estimates, with some putting the number at a few hundred.

Opponents argued that the proposal, known as Prop 40, would just drive the wealthy out of California, costing the state revenue and harming its economy.

But the measure divided Democrats in a state where they heavily outnumber Republicans.

The state's Democratic Party favored Prop 40, as did Rep. Ro Khanna, D-Calif., and Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., who went to the state to campaign for it. But Democratic Gov. Gavin Newsom opposed the tax, saying it would be better to have a national tax on the wealthy. The Democratic candidate for governor, Xavier Becerra, also opposed it.

A leading teachers union also opposed the proposal, which would have set aside just a small portion of the resulting revenue for schools. And Google co-founder Sergey Brin led other wealthy Californians in a surge of campaign spending opposing the measure. At least five times as much was spent opposing the measure as favoring it.

Brin also drove a campaign that put two other proposals on the ballot to block the billionaire tax from taking effect, had it been approved by voters.

#5938. Tampa Community Turns Out for Sierra Club’s ‘Power & Perspectives: The Energy Dialogue’

TAMPA, Fla. — On Thursday, November 5, Sierra Club Florida and partner organizations hosted “Power & Perspectives: The Energy Dialogue,” a community town hall addressing TECO bill increases and the need for local utility accountability. Over 25 people attended the event featuring a panel of Sierra Club, Food & Water Watch, and Hillsborough Affordable Energy Coalition representatives, as well as a member of Tampa’s City Council and the city’s Sustainability & Resilience Officer.

TECO has recently boasted rate decreases, however, monthly bills for residential or small business customers using 1,000 kWh per month are still over $10 higher than in December 2024. While families contend with higher bills, the CEO of TECO made $2.5 million last year. The CEO of Emera, TECO’s parent company, made $9.5 million.

“Transparency shouldn’t be optional when families are forced to choose between keeping the lights on and buying groceries,” said Walter Smith II, Sierra Club’s Tampa Bay Organizing Representative. “We invited our neighbors, TECO reps, and environmental justice experts into the same room because the community deserves answers about their energy burdens and the various impacts of fossil fuels on our environment and health. This town hall is a critical first step toward demanding a just, affordable, and clean energy future for everyone in Tampa Bay.”

“Tampa residents have been struggling under the weight of skyrocketing electricity bills, all while Tampa Electric walks away with record profits year after year and passes the costs of fuel, storm recovery, and even its brand new Midtown headquarters onto ratepayers,” said Isabella Moeller, Florida Organizer for Food & Water. “But our community has had enough. TECO is a for-profit company beholden to shareholders only, that’s why we’re making a wide call to our local elected officials to hold TECO and its regulators accountable to the people they serve. Tampa City Council has an opportunity to pass an affordable energy resolution that commits the city to opposing TECO’s unjust rate hikes and supporting clean, affordable energy solutions. We must build on this momentum and ensure that affordable energy is a priority for every elected official in Tampa.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5935. The Democratic Party gained power across the country in the midterms. Now what?

Newly elected Democratic U.S. Senators walk toward an orientation meeting inside the U.S. Capitol on Nov. 09, 2026 in Washington, DC. From left to right are Sen.-elect Juliana Stratton (D-IL), Sen.-elect Annie Andrews (D-SC), Sen.-elect Peggy Flanagan (D-MN), Sen.-elect James Talarico (D-TX), Sen.-elect Josh Turek (D-IA), and Sen.-elect Troy Jackson (D-ME). Win McNamee/Getty Images hide caption

Two years ago, voters sent Democrats a message when they ousted them from power in Washington. At first glance, last week's midterm results sent a new one.

Democrats will enter the next Congress with one of the largest majorities in a generation, flipping dozens of House districts and at least seven Senate seats in mainly Trump-won communities. The party now controls a majority of governorships, and gained control of several additional state legislative chambers.

"I think one of the big surprises for me on Tuesday night was just how deep and wide our election results were," Democratic National Committee Chairman Ken Martin said in an interview with NPR. "This was the biggest election cycle for Democrats in the history of our party."

Martin, who has faced criticism from some inside the party throughout his term as chairman, said that the stunning Democratic victories across the country were the result of his party's focus on issues like affordability and ending the war in Iran.

He said the midterms were bigger than politics and bigger than party, and acknowledged the results were just as much about serving as a "massive repudiation of Donald Trump and the MAGA Trumpers and the Republican Party." "I think it's an admonition for Democrats, for sure: Now that we're in power, we have to deliver," he said. "But it was a complete message to Republicans. You had the ability to help us and you did nothing."

With an expanded Congressional majority and a lame duck president, one of the frontlines for figuring out if the Democratic party's power will hold will be in the South, Martin said.

"Six of the 10 fastest growing parts of the country right now are below the Mason-Dixon line, and 40% of the U.S. population is going to live in the South after the next Census," he said. "As we go into 2032 and there's a realignment with the reapportionment, states like Minnesota, Michigan, Wisconsin, Pennsylvania, New York, Rhode Island, Illinois and California are all going to lose electoral votes and congressional votes to the South."

Last week's midterms brought the region's power – especially with Black voters – more squarely into focus. Turbocharged Black voter turnout helped elect the country's first Black female governor in Georgia and may elect the first Black U.S. Senator from Mississippi since Reconstruction.

A slew of Republican-led states seeking to redraw House maps for partisan advantage backfired, as Democrats gained several seats across the region.

Georgia Democratic Sen. Raphael Warnock said in an interview with NPR that Democratic victories in 2026 were a "proof of concept" that the South is central to the future of American politics.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries (D-NY) speaks during a press conference on Nov. 4, 2026 in Washington, DC. Jeffries is expected to be the next Speaker of the House, the first Black speaker in history. Kevin Dietsch/Getty Images hide caption

"You saw a multiracial coalition of voters stand up on Tuesday night beyond anything I had even hoped for, and they offered a silent rebuke of where we are now," he told NPR. "It's now up to us to provide the leadership, even as we listen to people, and point the way forward into how we build an economy that works for everyday working families."

Warnock said Democrats' direction in the next two years should aim to serve as a cure for the last decade of Trump-dominated politics that has been defined more as top versus bottom rather than left versus right.

"Ordinary people have had their hopes dashed time and time again, cycle after cycle, across Republican and Democratic administrations," he said. "They've seen the widening chasm between the haves and the have nots."

He called the midterms a "moral moment" that will help determine what kind of nation America wants to be.

In the early days after the 2026 elections, a look ahead to the 2028 presidential race finds both parties with the potential to see crowded fields of candidates in several new lanes reacting to signals sent by the electorate.

That includes Warnock, who will be up for reelection in 2028 and is among names floated as a presidential contender, along with former Transportation Secretary Pete Buttigieg, California Rep. Ro Khanna, Illinois Gov. JB Pritzker, Kentucky Gov. Andy Beshear, former Vice President Kamala Harris and others who have spent significant time in the South campaigning for other Democrats.

As new members of Congress descend on Washington for their orientations, competing visions for Democratic governance are on display with postmortem event panels called "What Worked" and "What Just Happened?" and event names like "What's Next" as party strategists argue online about which wing of the party was the most successful.

2026 victories also scramble the traditional map of swing states, Martin said, suggesting that Democrats would start off competitive in places like Florida and Texas and as favorites to win the White House.

"Texas is no longer a red state. It is a blue state. They didn't even go from red to purple to blue, which is a normal sort of progression," he said. "If Texas is now a blue state, and you look at Georgia and you look at other parts of the South, how do the Republicans get to 270 votes?"

#5933. Solar Industry Shows Hope After US Election, Focuses On Key Message

Let’s be honest — even with humongous, epic control of Congress like we haven’t seen in several decades, Democrats can’t really do anything legislatively unless Donald Trump wants to, and there’s a super slim chance Trump would do anything to support clean energy. He is extremely anti clean energy and pro fossil fuels. It would have to be some wild compromise for Trump to do anything supporting solar power, and you can completely forget about any support ever for wind power. Nonetheless, it’s better to have Democrats in power in the Senate and the House with the intent of supporting solar rather than fossil-bought politicians trying to hamper it and push more pollution on all of us.

Also, hey, maybe Democrats could find a way to sneak some solar support into some much bigger legislation somewhere?

Anyway, this is how the CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA), Tim Pawlenty, has responded to the election results: “Energy affordability was a key issue in this election, reflecting the real pressures facing American households and businesses. The solar and storage industry looks forward to working with newly elected federal and state leaders to bring more affordable, reliable power online and deliver the energy savings Americans are demanding.”

That statement also gives a hint of where there is huge potential for the industry. About three quarters of the country now live in a state that will be controlled by a Democratic governor! A lot of things can be done at the state level to support solar power.

One important thing is to keep the messaging consistent and clear: solar power is about making electricity costs more affordable, preventing long-term rising costs that come from fossil fuels. SEIA is clearly quite good at such messaging, but that needs to remain a central focus across the industry.

#5929. NRDC Assessment of BAAJA, the 2026 Permitting & Transmission Bill

The Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026 (BAAJA) is the result of years of negotiations on permitting reform. The product is a bill that combines critically important transmission and grid reforms with significant and concerning changes to federal environmental laws, permitting standards, and judicial remedies.

The transmission provisions in the bill would produce substantial and durable benefits, updating an aging power grid by allowing new investments. These reforms, which NRDC has long supported, are an essential component of accelerating the electrification and decarbonization of the power sector and achieving greenhouse gas reduction targets. On their own, these changes are much needed and overdue; they would also help any future clean energy incentives and standards succeed in cutting emissions. But transmission is an enabling technology rather than a substitute for policies that directly drive clean energy investment and reduce pollution.

However, there are a number of concerning provisions that raise substantial issues and reinforce the position of incumbent energy interests. The “project certainty” language is designed to ensure projects cannot be arbitrarily canceled. As drafted, it could benefit fossil fuel, mining, data center, and other industrial projects more than clean energy. It could also limit agencies’ ability to address newly discovered harms, constrain future pollution regulation, and create large taxpayer-funded corporate handouts.

The bill would also weaken key elements of core environmental laws, including the Clean Water Act (CWA), the Endangered Species Act, the National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA), and the National Historic Preservation Act (NHPA). The bill would narrow environmental review across those statutes, diminish state and Tribal authority, constrain public participation, and restrict courts’ ability to stop projects proceeding under unlawful permits. These changes erode fundamental protections for communities.

The backdrop, of course, is the Trump administration’s nonstop attacks on clean energy projects, tax incentives for clean energy, and climate standards. NRDC’s analysis finds in aggregate that the Trump administration’s actions could double carbon emissions from the power sector over the next decade while raising consumers’ electric utility bills by $30 billion per year by 2035. Lawmakers should push for tangible evidence that this has changed before voting on the legislation.

While NRDC is encouraged by the transmission provisions, other elements of the bill are deeply flawed. We will be seeking improvements in the coming weeks and will take a position on the bill before a floor vote.

Below is an assessment of the most positive and most negative elements of the bill. This is not intended to be comprehensive.

An aging power grid desperately needs updating, and with more extreme weather and surging demand from data centers, getting new electricity connected to the grid quickly is essential to keeping energy costs down and maintaining resilience. In one fell swoop, this legislation would address these issues, producing the necessary conditions to deploy more clean energy.

This legislation would provide a dramatic near-term boost to the nation’s grid by increasing both the amount of power that existing transmission lines can carry and the supply of electricity available to meet growing demand. It establishes procedures that could cut the time to build a major new transmission line to five to six years from 10 years—or sometimes significantly longer—today.

The Midwest’s multi-value projects: How agreeing on who pays can unlock transmission

In 2011, the Midwest grid operator, MISO, approved 17 high-voltage lines costing more than $5 billion and spread the cost across the region based on who benefits. Within about a decade, 16 of the 17 were in service, helping to connect wind power across the Midwest.

Between regions, where no such agreement exists, almost no major lines were approved in the decade after FERC’s 2011 planning rule. The bill tries to bring the Midwest’s approach to lines that cross between regions.

In the short term, it would require utilities to upgrade existing transmission lines and deploy advanced transmission technologies whenever their benefits exceed their costs. This technology can increase the capacity and efficiency of existing transmission lines, reduce congestion, and improve reliability, often at a cost that is 5 to 10 times cheaper than building new transmission. If deployed aggressively in the short term, this could free up more than 100 gigawatts of transmission capacity—enough to power 75 million homes.

Importantly, the bill goes beyond simply requiring utilities to consider these technologies: It directs the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) to establish timelines for deployment, makes it easier for utilities to recover their costs, and establishes penalties for utilities that fail to deploy cost-effective technologies.

From PJM’s queue freeze to Southwest Power Pool’s fix

In 2022, PJM, which runs the grid for 13 states and Washington, D.C., stopped taking new requests to connect to the grid while it worked through a backlog of more than 300 gigawatts, 95 percent of it renewables and storage. It didn’t reopen until April 2026, so projects that applied after 2022 are only now starting their studies.

In March 2026, FERC approved a new approach from Southwest Power Pool, which runs the grid across much of the central United States. It plans transmission and new power plants together over 20 years, charges developers a standard up-front fee to connect, and studies projects once a year with a single decision point. The bill would require every region to adopt a process like this within two years and to speed up studies with better data and automation.

The bill makes major interstate power lines easier to build by strengthening federal authority over large interstate transmission lines. Currently, a state can block or indefinitely delay a project that would provide broad regional or national benefits. If a state rejects an eligible project or fails to act within one year, the bill requires FERC to issue a permit for a covered transmission line when a developer seeks federal review and the project satisfies specific criteria. FERC must then spread the costs fairly among all consumers who benefit from the projects.

The bill also requires neighboring grid regions to plan interregional transmission jointly, assess the benefits of potential projects, and establish methods for allocating costs. Despite a FERC order issued in 2011 that was meant to spur these cross-regional power lines, currently, virtually no major lines have been built between regions because there is no durable process for planning them or determining who should pay. The bill addresses this long-standing problem and codifies important elements of FERC’s updated regional planning framework from 2024, known as Order 1920.

The bill will also reduce the long wait times for energy projects to connect to the grid. Approximately 2,000 gigawatts of proposed generation and storage are waiting to connect to the grid, and 85 percent of that capacity is solar, wind, or storage. Projects often wait years for studies and may receive unexpectedly high upgrade costs late in the process. The bill encourages more coordinated planning, provides developers earlier information about costs, and improves data and automation.

Taken together, these transmission and interconnection reforms are a once-in-a-generation opportunity that would produce significant and durable benefits. The reforms would also facilitate the infrastructure needed so that future clean energy incentives and standards could succeed.

The data center provisions could provide protection for existing utility customers and support grid investments by requiring data centers to pay for their impact to the grid. It would direct FERC to develop pricing rules designed to prevent transmission costs created by data centers (generally 20 megawatts or more) from being shifted to other ratepayers and ensure data centers provide financial assurance to avoid costs shifting in the future. FERC would also have to revise transmission pricing policies and require standardized analysis of upgrades and cost-reduction options. These provisions address the transmission portion of the broader effects of rapid data center growth. The bill does not address local air pollution, water consumption, noise, land use, reliance on on-site fossil fuel generation, transparency, or public participation.

With geothermal energy poised to be a major player in the clean energy future, the bill provides policy reforms that could increase permitting efficiency and lower some of the barriers to geothermal development. But the geothermal provisions also come with their drawbacks, including the expansion of Section 390’s oil and gas activity categorical exclusion to geothermal development, which allows for significant drilling and production activities to take place on public lands without environmental review.

In an effort to respond to the Trump administration’s relentless attacks on wind and solar projects, this legislation includes “project certainty” language to protect developers from unreasonable attacks. Unfortunately, these provisions could have problematic unintended consequences, including locking in the Trump administration’s preferential treatment of oil, gas, data centers, and mining projects. This part of the legislation could also lead to a slew of industry lawsuits that result in taxpayers paying billions of dollars to private industry.

These “project certainty” provisions would largely prevent federal agencies from canceling, suspending, or changing federal approvals once issued. The provision’s reach is not limited to energy projects and is not limited to specific agencies or types of permits. The legislation sets out a small handful of circumstances in which agencies can modify an issued permit. Notably, while these circumstances include national security or defense concerns, they do not include environmental harm—even if newly discovered, severe, or widespread. Its broad language could even lead to industry arguments and legal claims that operating facilities are protected from subsequently adopted pollution requirements under the Clean Air Act and other statutes. State permits to implement future U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) standards for power plant carbon emissions, for example, could face legal challenges based on this provision.

And if a project is canceled or amended, a company can go to court to get its permit reinstated—as well as require the government to pay for its litigation expenses and the cost of project delays. The government could also be forced to use taxpayer money to make an additional payment of 25–50 percent of total project costs incurred to the company. For a single large energy project, this could easily amount to hundreds of millions, or even billions, of dollars.

A separate provision would allow energy-project developers to sue agencies for alleged “disparate treatment.” The approach uses a five-year comparison period that could embed the second Trump administration’s energy preferences in the legal baseline, and thus advantage oil, gas, and coal projects. And it prevents agencies acting in good faith from fully defending themselves in court. Successful claims could result in payments equal to 50–100 percent of projected construction costs plus actual damages and legal costs, creating a serious risk that the provision could be gamed and produce large taxpayer-funded payouts.

The bill makes sweeping changes to our ability to enforce a number of environmental laws. For example, a court that finds violations of the CWA’s “dredge and fill” permit system will be unable to invalidate the permit except in narrow circumstances; a court can never invalidate a permit based on a violation of the NEPA and the NHPA, no matter how severe it finds a legal violation to be. Though a deficient review can be returned to an agency, construction is allowed to continue while the agency corrects its mistakes. The result could be that by the time the agency completes its review, the project may already be largely or entirely built and the damage difficult or impossible to reverse.

Right now, if a court finds that an agency violated NEPA, it can stop the project from moving forward until the agency corrects the environmental review documents. For example, a federal judge put an oil and gas lease in Alaska’s ecologically rich Cook Inlet on hold until the responsible agency corrected its defective environmental review. That reasonable result may not have been possible had this bill been the law.

The bill also excludes activities from NEPA review, expands exemptions for others, narrows the analysis of alternatives, and potentially limits who can bring a case. Collectively, these changes substantially reduce the practical value of NEPA as a means to improve environmental outcomes. Tribes, local organizations, and affected communities may be particularly disadvantaged if they are cut out of the process or cannot stop harmful projects before they cause damage.

The bill would dramatically curtail the rights and powers of Tribes enshrined in Section 106 of the NHPA, effectively limiting Tribes’ ability to protect their lands and culturally significant places from proposed projects with a federal connection. The bill limits Tribal participation in decisions concerning federally connected projects and what Tribal interests are protected, which, under current law, includes not only artifacts but also sacred places, cultural landscapes, waterways, wildlife, and living ecosystems. Tribal Nations and Indigenous partners have made clear that these changes significantly undermine Tribal sovereignty and the federal government’s nation-to-nation responsibilities.

NHPA, specifically Section 106, has played a critical role in ensuring that permitting processes must integrate Tribal Nation feedback, helping to improve the environmental and cultural resource outcomes for projects, including the Pebble Mine and the Arches and Canyonlands National Parks, due to oil and gas drilling proposals. Proposed changes to Section 106 would place additional areas in further jeopardy—including mining proposals that would impact the Brooks Range and Gates of the Arctic National Park and Preserve, along with drilling proposals in the Chaco region, the Western Arctic, and the Arctic National Wildlife Refuge, respectively.

The bill weakens several CWA programs, which will make it easier for activities that pollute or destroy water resources to move forward without adequate safeguards. It limits the ability of states and Tribes to ensure the health of streams, lakes, wetlands, and groundwater when the federal government authorizes activities that impact them. It takes a fast-tracked permitting process meant for activities that have minimal impacts (like boat ramps) and applies it to major projects such as pipelines and highways.

The bill makes significant changes to the Clean Water Act program that gives states and Tribes the ability to demand water quality–based improvements to federal permits. For gas pipelines and transmission projects, reviewing authorities are limited to considering direct discharges, (e.g., just the effects of a concrete structure for a pipeline) rather than broader construction-related effects such as erosion, sediment, contaminated runoff, groundwater pollution, and damage to wetlands and streams, or ongoing impacts from the operation of the project.

For other projects, the bill places a substantially higher burden on states seeking to require protections. Currently, a state can identify a water quality concern, request reasonable changes, and allow a project to proceed with stronger safeguards. A state would now be required to demonstrate harm by clear and convincing evidence (versus reasonable evidence) and show that its proposed condition is the least burdensome means of addressing the water quality concern. In practice, these new hurdles to water quality conditions will be very difficult to overcome, which will likely result in fewer state conditions on permits needed to protect water quality. In addition, the bill would restrict states and Tribes from taking the actions they can today to protect habitat, groundwater (including drinking water supplies), and many small streams and critical wetlands.

How the Clean Water Act change could impact states

If the special provisions for gas pipeline projects had been the law, Pennsylvania likely would not have been able to require the Atlantic Sunrise Pipeline Project to comply with state erosion and sediment laws, and West Virginia likely would not have been able to require controls and compliance monitoring that enabled the state to enforce against the Mountain Valley Pipeline’s violations of runoff requirements.

By shifting and raising the burden of proof, this requirement would jeopardize precautionary state actions, such as a Tennessee decision this spring, when it refused to sign off on an Army Corps permit for a landfill because the project developer had failed to show that pollution from the site would not harm local waters. This kind of state engagement improves projects and does not end them.

The bill also weakens the Clean Water Act’s general permitting program (administered by the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers) for dredging and filling waters. The bill directs the Corps, when creating general permits, to look only at the harm that results from filling in waters. No other water impacts like water pollution or groundwater depletion could be considered. Additionally, right now, most general permits can only be used for impacts of one-half acre or less; this bill would quadruple this threshold, which could allow larger data centers or other major projects to qualify. It also extends the duration of general permits, reducing opportunities for public input on requirements for projects to once a decade. Finally, it requires the Corps to maintain fast-track permits for large “linear” projects that can have major environmental harms, like roadways and pipelines.

The bill weakens other Clean Water Act programs as well. It would curb the EPA’s ability to stop projects that dump dredged or fill material that “have an unacceptable adverse effect on municipal water supplies, shellfish beds and fishery areas (including spawning and breeding areas), wildlife, or recreational areas,” which the agency has invoked rarely, but done so to protect exceptional habitats threatened by highly damaging projects. It would slow the EPA’s development of scientific advice to states about the levels of pollutants that can cause harm to aquatic life or people, and it would delay updates to discharge requirements for industrial polluters and sewage plants; together, these two changes would constrain the ability to control “forever chemicals.”

Language relating to the “best available science” standard could be interpreted to restrict precautionary action when scientific information is uncertain. This is particularly concerning when an imperiled population of a species is difficult or expensive to study and waiting for definitive proof could result in irreversible loss.

The bill also allows states to assume responsibilities currently carried out by the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service. Many states lack the capacity, expertise, or in some cases, the commitment necessary to perform this responsibility effectively, and if a state is not executing its responsibility, there is not a requirement for the federal government to reassume it.

The bill would exempt many wells drilled on private lands that tap into federally owned oil and gas reserves from federal permitting, opening at least 57 million subsurface acres of federal resources to exploitation without federal permits. If passed into law, this provision strips thousands of private landowners across the West of important protections like required notice of industry’s plans to develop on their property, increases the risk that landowners get stuck with orphaned wells and their associated environmental and financial costs, and creates new risks of fraud and abuse as it relates to royalty reporting.

The bill allows mining companies to claim an unlimited number of five-acre mill sites on public lands to be used for things like tailing storage—wastewater and rock often containing heavy metals and other dangerous residues—and waste dumps. And it allows mining companies to occupy public lands for any other use relevant to a mining project, regardless of its proximity to the actual mine, meaning roads, pipelines, powerlines, and any other activity formerly requiring a federal permit and environmental review would be allowed so long as the activity is included in the mine’s operation plan.

The modeling completed to date focuses on greenhouse gas and economic impacts. Available analyses from NRDC and other groups generally project modest to significant greenhouse gas benefits, especially over the long term, but the results vary substantially depending on the provisions modeled, policy baseline, fuel and technology costs, and implementation assumptions.

NRDC’s initial results show modest power sector benefits.

The benefits of additional transmission will generally be greater in a policy environment that drives clean energy deployment. In a baseline with limited support for new clean energy resources, transmission may improve reliability and reduce costs without producing sizable emissions reductions.

The oil-and-gas effects remain uncertain. NRDC has not quantified the bill’s effects on oil-and-gas development. Because the existing baseline already assumes high levels of domestic production growth (oil and gas production have both doubled in the last 20 years), faster permitting may not produce a large increase in emissions compared with business as usual. Nevertheless, the bill could accelerate individual projects, improve their economics, and make approved infrastructure more difficult to regulate or modify later. The risk of locking in that trajectory merits consideration, even if near-term emissions changes appear modest.

Data-center growth creates a particularly important unknown. The grid provisions may indeed enable data centers to finance upgrades and connect to an expanded transmission system. But transmission takes years to plan and construct. In the interim, faster permitting and weaker environmental safeguards could encourage additional on-site or nearby gas generation, particularly in regions with access to existing pipelines or gas fields.

Greenhouse gas emissions are only one measure of the bill’s effects. The modeling does not quantify potential consequences for drinking water, local air pollution, public health, endangered species and wildlife, or other metrics. It also cannot account for the practical importance of judicial remedies in ensuring that agencies and developers comply with environmental law.

NRDC is seeking significant improvements to the bill in five areas:

NRDC will continue to advance technical corrections, including changes needed to address drafting errors and unintended consequences. But technical corrections cannot substitute for changes to some of these structural provisions, for which we will continue to advocate.

NRDC is the nation's most effective environmental action group, combining the grassroots power of 1.3 million members and online activists with the courtroom clout and expertise of more than 350 lawyers, scientists, and other professionals.

#5926. Is Trump About To Reignite His Big EV Push?

There are so many ironies with the Trump presidency. I remember writing about many of them in his first term. Now, though, we have some additional ones. For example, despite his apparent hate for clean technologies (wind power, solar power, electric vehicles, energy efficient appliances), Trump has pushed a ton of people to buy electric vehicles, including people who really wouldn’t consider doing so even with normal EV incentives in place.

Of course, we all know what this is about. This was a key reason for the absolutely massive blue tsunami that took over the country this week. Trump’s war on Iran, which many Americans can’t explain in the slightest, drove gasoline prices up enormously and pushed people to go to the polls and vote Democrat.

Trump and his team also knew this. They knew that the war on Iran was raising gas prices and that high gas prices are one of the worst things you can have when running for office. So, even though Trump didn’t leave himself any easy and simple way to get out of the war on Iran, he quieted down or paused his most significant actions against the country for the midterm elections and gas prices are not as high as they were a few months ago. They are just above $4/gallon on average, whereas the rose above $4.50/gallon earlier this year. Just in the week leading into election day, the national gas price average dropped by 12 cents a gallon. Of course, it was too little too late, and Republican politicians’ lack of concern for families and their high gas costs didn’t fare well in the election. This was probably the leading single factor for the swing to the left.

Now that the election is over and the war is not close to any resolution as far as we can tell, Trump may be inclined to heat it up again. If he has been holding back, he may go full force again into trying to force Iran to give in, and this could lead to a bigger closure of the Strait of Hormuz … which would likely lead to spiking gas prices again. Naturally, even at $4/gallon, Trump is making a lot of Americans (and humans more broadly) think about buying electric vehicles, and some of them are going to go ahead and making the switch. What if Trump goes further, as many now expect he will, and oil supply is disrupted much worse and oil prices spike again? He could become one of the greatest EV salesmen in history. We’ll see what happens….

#5925. House Republicans to elect a new leader as they look to rebuild from midterm defeat

Rep. Jim Jordan, R-Ohio, left, and Rep. Steve Scalise, R-La., confer during a news conference at the Capitol on June 8, 2022. The two GOP leaders are competing to replace Mike Johnson as the top House Republican. J. Scott Applewhite/AP hide caption

Just one week after losing the majority in the midterm elections, House Republicans will meet behind closed doors on Tuesday to pick a minority leader for the next Congress.

The choice to succeed Mike Johnson, R-La., as the top House Republican will be between current House Majority Leader Steve Scalise, R-La., and Ohio Republican Jim Jordan, a co-founder of the ultra-conservative House Freedom Caucus.

The election — which will be conducted by secret ballot — represents a high-stakes decision for the party that will help determine which way it goes as it looks to regroup from a staggering defeat that saw Democrats flip more than 40 GOP seats en route to winning back the majority.

"I think what develops here will become a larger, external conversation about the direction of the Republican Party," said former House Speaker Pro Tempore Patrick McHenry, who held the gavel in an acting role during the tumultuous GOP speakership fight in 2023.

Republicans say they are ready to turn the page on their losses and start rebuilding to regain voters' trust. But first, they will have to decide between two candidates who would bring very different styles of leadership.

In Scalise, 61, House Republicans would be choosing a form of continuity — the No. 2 House Republican since 2023, and someone who has served in broader GOP leadership roles for more than 10 years. He's seen as more moderate than Jordan, and in a letter to colleagues announcing his candidacy, the close Trump ally said his approach to the job would be "conservative and consensus-driven."

"My number one goal as Republican Leader will be to win back the majority and I expect you to hold me accountable to that mission," Scalise wrote. "To succeed, we will need to be effective in both the oversight and legislative fights ahead, but we will also need to lay out a clear and optimistic vision for America."

"We have big tasks ahead, and tested leadership is needed now more than ever," he added.

In Jordan, 62, Republicans would be choosing a veteran of both the House Oversight and House Judiciary committees — two panels poised to play a central role in the new Congress as Democrats plan for a long list of investigations into President Trump and his administration.

A former college wrestling coach known for his pugilistic style, Jordan has pitched an aggressive posture to his colleagues, writing that "offense, not defense, wins elections."

"For too long, we have operated without a clear plan to survive the hour or the day in front of us. That will not cut it against an emboldened and energetic Democratic party," Jordan said in his letter to colleagues, taking a swipe at the House leadership team led by Johnson and Scalise.

"I am battle-tested on the oversight front and I know the Democrats' playbook," he added.

The contest is a rematch from 2023. Back then, Jordan and Scalise were vying to succeed Kevin McCarthy. After multiple candidates failed to secure enough votes, Republicans ultimately voted for Johnson.

Leslie Shedd, a Republican strategist, said the candidates will need to break through a process that can be like "family-on-family violence."

"It's really important … to lay out a very clear, substantive vision of how they plan to lead this conference out of what is now a desert," she said.

John Leganski, who was an aide to McCarthy, the former House Speaker, estimates more than 70% of the incoming House GOP conference was elected after Trump won his first term. As a result, they were largely made in his image or won because of it.

That means, Trump could wield a major influence over the direction of the House Republican conference as a result. So far, he's staying out of the race, though in 2023 he did endorse Jordan.

Leganski said that the size of Democrats' victory last week will limit leverage for Republicans under the eventual House Minority Leader when the new Congress is seated in January.

"I think whoever wins your job is to … number one, try to win back to the House," he said. "And number two, make life as difficult for the Democrats as possible, largely by voting no."

NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews. Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.

#5921. GM Steps On Tesla’s EV Toes With EV1 Celebration

In a move that shocked and surprised nobody, last week Tesla CEO Elon Musk pulled the plug on his much-hyped Cybertruck. Production will end sometime next year. It may be small consolation, but at least the Cybertruck sold more units than another notorious flop in modern automotive history, General Motors’s ill-fated EV1 sedan. Was EV1 really a flop, though? In sales, yes, but GM wants everyone to know that the EV1 beat Tesla to the electrification punch in key technology milestones.

GM actually sold few, if any, EV1 units, preferring to lease the car with a focus on drivers in California. Production began in 1996 but that didn’t last long. In 2003 GM pulled the plug, recalled the vehicles, and sent them to the crusher, having produced barely more than 1,000 units overall.

Only a scant few escaped the junkyard. One of them was rescued by US filmmaker Alex Gibney, leading to his advisory role in the searing 2006 EV1 documentary “Who Killed the Electric Car?” (btw, Gibney ‘s newly released “Musk” documentary is available on streaming now).

This year marks the 30th anniversary of EV1 production, and GM is using the occasion to celebrate. Rather than shoveling all memory of the EV1 debacle into the memory hole, GM has embarked on sort of under-the-radar public relations campaign, seeking to position the car as a necessary, albeit short lived, step along the vehicle electrification journey.

In not so many words, GM also draws attention to the fact that its work on EV1 predates the launch of Tesla Motors, which came on the scene in 2003. The automaker’s Senior Writer and Editor at GM News, Chris Perkins, ran down the particulars in March, noting that EV1 was the first EV to deploy heat pumps for climate control, energy efficiency, and battery temperature management.

Perkins also takes note of the car’s regenerative braking system. “Engineers developed a system for the car that translated a driver’s brake-pedal input into an electronic signal that blended regenerative and friction braking,” Perkins wrote.

“It’s a precursor to the braking systems used in GM’s modern EVs, which also add One-Pedal Driving1 and paddle-actuated Regen-On-Demand2 capability,” he added.

EV1 also eschewed mechanical actuators in favor of fully electronic (aka by-wire) controls for the accelerator pedal, brake pedal, parking brake, and gear selector, with the power steering featuring electro-hydraulic control.

Additionally, Perkins elaborated on the regenerative braking angle in a follow-up article in August, by way of demonstrating a through line between the 1990’s technology and the automaker’s current EV lineup.

“Conceptually, the EV1’s braking system operates exactly like the braking systems in GM’s modern EVs,” Perkins wrote. “Press the brake pedal, and the car’s computers decide how much friction and regenerative braking to actuate. Should the electronic system fail, it’s connected to a mechanical backup, just as in the EV1.”

As part of the PR campaign, GM is also putting its 1994 concept sedan on display, the 1994 Impact (pictured above). “As one of the first modern, purpose-built electric vehicles from a major automaker, the Impact was a critical learning tool that shaped the final production version of the EV1,” GM emphasizes.

If you blink, you’ll miss it. The Impact is on display at the GM-sponsored Test Track at EPCOT, the Disney theme park in Bay Lake, Florida, until mid-December.

As for why all this fuss, why now, that’s a good question. Over the past several years, GM has put a jolly spin on its inability to catch up to Tesla in sales, let alone surpass it. As recently as last year, GM vigorously touted itself as #2 in EV sales in the US, second only to Tesla as a badge of honor.

Still, by August of 2025 the competition was beginning to take on a different tone, with GM pitching low-cost LFP (lithium-iron-phosphate) batteries as a near-term pathway to meeting, then beating Tesla.

The 2026 EV1 celebration elaborates upon the technology angle, underscoring GM’s claim on the pre-Tesla, 1990’s roots of innovation in the vehicle electrification field.

The new emphasis on technology also comes at a pivotal moment for EV sales in the US. The US market nosedived last fall after US President Trump and his Republican allies in Congress prematurely eliminated federal tax credits for EVs. However, activity continued to stir in some quarters, and Trump himself made the case for EVs more attractive when he decided to bomb Iran in February.

Nine months later, the price of gas remains stubbornly high, sparking renewed hopes that EV sales will rebound more quickly than first anticipated. In addition, a fresh round of innovation is on the horizon, with GM banking on its forthcoming LMR (lithium manganese-rich) battery formula to push costs down without sacrificing performance.

While Tesla is not the only competition in town, beating the startup at its own game would be a feather in GM’s cap, and Elon Musk made the effort a little easier when he canceled the Cybertruck, leaving Tesla without a foothold in the hot market for pickup trucks in the US. The automaker still has sedans, SUVs, the new Semi Class 8 truck, and the “flying” Roadster in its pocket, but the demise of the Cybertruck has left the pickup field wide open.

Meanwhile, Ford is on track to produce its new Fathom electric pickup next year starting at under $30,000, far less than the Cybertruck’s most recent starting price of $77,000 plus change. The Jeff Bezos-backed startup Slate Auto is also aiming at the affordability market with a stripped-down electric pickup starting at a base price of $26,000 and change, before accounting for add-ons.

On its part, GM is aiming for the middle ground with the 2027 Silverado electric pickup, starting at around $56,000 and reaching close to Cybertruck range at around $72,000.

Meanwhile, Rivian is also aiming upscale at close to $80,000 and up for its RIT pickup. The startup is on the move, having somehow managed to squeeze a loan guarantee of up to $4.5 billion out of the Trump administration. The funds are earmarked to expand Rivian’s production capacity with a new factory in Georgia.

Readers, what do you think? Does GM — or any other automaker — have a snowball’s chance in hell to catch up to Tesla in the US market? Drop a note in the discussion thread…

#5918. Why did California's billionaire tax fail to surf the blue wave?

Ultrasound Technologist Mayra Castaneda speaks in front of healthcare workers holding placards in support of the Billionaire Tax during a media briefing in Los Angeles on April 27, 2026. Healthcare workers and allies outlined the next steps in their effort to get California's Billionaire Tax on the ballot for the November election, with their efforts already exceeding 1,500,000 signatures collected from across the state. The initiative would levy a one-time 5% tax on California billionaires. (Photo by Frederic J. BROWN / AFP via Getty Images) FREDERIC J. BROWN/AFP via Getty Images/AFP hide caption

Late Monday, The Associated Press determined that California voters had rejected Prop 40, the proposed billionaire wealth tax I wrote about last week. Given the big blue wave we saw during the midterms, why did this tax on billionaires fail to surf to victory?

To make sense of the results, I went back to the two economists I spoke with last week: UC Berkeley's Gabriel Zucman, a leading champion of wealth taxes, and Stanford's Joshua Rauh, a prominent critic of Prop 40.

Last week, they disagreed on what this wealth tax on billionaires would do to California's economy and tax revenue. Now comes the sequel, in which they disagree on what the results say about the politics of wealth taxes.

Their debate could foreshadow a bigger one in the next Democratic presidential primary: should the party embrace a wealth tax?

Zucman blames the corrosive influence of money for Prop 40's failure.

"I am not surprised," Zucman says. Opponents of Prop 40 spent almost $400 million fighting it, including spending on competing ballot measures, making it one of the most expensive ballot initiative fights in history.

"The money spent by the billionaires was critical," Zucman says. "Anyone who lives in California has been carpet-bombed by a deluge of ads. One can easily see how this could be pivotal in such a closely contested ballot."

But didn't Democrats win plenty of races this cycle despite huge sums spent against them? In Texas, for example, Elon Musk alone poured more than $150 million into defeating Democrat James Talarico. And Talarico ended up winning his Senate race by a comfortable margin. Why would money have been so decisive with Prop 40 and not in other races around the nation?

For one, Zucman says, the spending gap was much bigger in the fight over Prop 40. "The billionaires spent more than $300 million on ads versus negligible sums on the other side," Zucman says. "On TV and in their mailboxes, voters were exposed only to the anti-tax arguments. This was very different from the other midterm races: in those races, when spending was high (e.g., Talarico vs. Paxton in Texas) it was high on both sides, even if unevenly. The net difference in spending was considerably larger for Prop 40."

It's also possible that money can matter more in ballot initiatives than in races between candidates. Analysts have complained for decades that California's ballot initiative system is too easily manipulated by special interest groups. The nitty-gritty details of specific policies can get complicated, and many voters don't take the time to get educated on them. That can give well-funded advertising campaigns more power to shape their views.

In candidate races, meanwhile, voters can at least fall back on name recognition or party labels, which give them an easy mental shortcut — what social scientists call a "heuristic" — when making decisions at the ballot box. Even when they know little about the candidates, they usually know which party they prefer.

But Prop 40 didn't come with the same clear partisan signal. In fact, prominent Democrats, including California Gov. Gavin Newsom, came out against it. Newsom said he favored a national minimum tax on billionaires instead.

"Popular opinion on wealth taxes is clearly not as strong as proponents thought," Rauh says.

Rauh questions whether money and advertising can really explain Prop 40's poor showing. He compared support for the tax with support for Kamala Harris in the 2024 presidential election. In some of "the most progressive Bay Area counties," he says, Harris won overwhelming support while Prop 40 won much less. This gap between support for Harris and support for Prop 40 was bigger there than it was in other Democratic-leaning counties across the state.

If advertising was the main explanation for this tax's poor showing, he argues, why was the gap so much bigger in the Bay Area? Sure, he says, advertisers may have done more targeting there, but he thinks the uneven pattern across the state casts doubt on the idea that advertising was the decisive factor.

"[T]he counties with the largest defections are actually the ones whose residents are most likely to know a billionaire, to work for one, or to have watched one move to Texas or Florida," Rauh writes in a recent post. "The simple reading is that proximity to the people being taxed, and to the businesses they fund, made voters more skeptical of the tax relative to their baseline politics."

For Rauh, that skepticism points to a broader conclusion: "The electorate even in progressive California has shown that it isn't ready for a wealth tax," he says.

More moderate Democratic voters, especially in affluent areas like the Bay Area, may simply dislike this novel way of taxing rich people in America and the consequences it could have. After all, this policy idea has divided Democrats before. During the 2020 presidential primary, Elizabeth Warren and Bernie Sanders both championed wealth taxes, but rivals like Sen. Cory Booker and Michael Bloomberg opposed them. Joe Biden said he didn't oppose a wealth tax on the campaign trail, but early in his presidency, he declined to pursue Sen. Warren's wealth tax proposal.

Or maybe voters weren't rejecting the idea of a wealth tax per se. Maybe they were rejecting the particulars of this wealth tax.

For one thing, Prop 40 was a one-time tax, and it earmarked its projected revenue in ways that drew opposition from traditional Democratic allies. It allocated 90% of the revenue to health care, leaving only 10% for food assistance and education-related programs.

That helped put some typically left-leaning organizations in the opposition camp. For example, the California Teachers Association, a powerful teachers union. It argued that Prop 40 shortchanged public schools and did "not provide the sustainable and long-lasting funding that our schools and communities deserve."

Meanwhile, Prop 40 would have taxed billionaires at the state level. Supporters of a national approach argue that a wealth tax would be more effective at the national level because it's harder to leave America than it is to move to another state.

When Newsom came out against Prop 40 back in June, he wrote, "Wealth is movable, and it shops for the state with the lowest taxes. The fight belongs at the federal level, where this broken system was created in the first place."

Despite Prop 40's defeat, Zucman believes the wealth tax movement is far from over.

"With the blue wave nationally, the billionaire tax is going to be front and center in the new Congress," Zucman says. "Many of the newly elected Democratic senators have been campaigning on curbing the power and influence of billionaires. One can reasonably expect to see significant progress in the next two years."

Rauh also expects to see more wealth tax proposals in the coming Congress. But with President Trump in the White House, he sees little chance of one becoming law anytime soon.

"It will be the 2028 election that will really demonstrate the extent to which the public wants wealth taxation, as those proposals will likely feature prominently in political debate," Rauh says.

But even if wealth tax supporters win over voters, Rauh stresses they would encounter another big hurdle: the courts. He argues a federal wealth tax would constitute "a direct tax," a kind of tax that is subject to crippling constitutional restrictions.

As I explained in this 2019 Planet Money newsletter, the U.S. Constitution requires that "direct taxes" go through a complicated rigamarole in order to be constitutional. Specifically, the share of a direct tax's total revenue collected from each state has to match its share of the national population. That could mean wildly different tax rates depending on where people live.

This weird rule's history is tangled up with slavery. In one early Supreme Court case, one justice described it as basically a concession to Southern states seeking to limit federal taxation of their land and enslaved people.

The 16th Amendment exempted income taxes from this requirement. But that amendment doesn't mention wealth taxes.

Legal scholars disagree over whether this rule would doom a federal wealth tax. Is a wealth tax even a "direct tax"? That early Supreme Court case involved a tax on horse-drawn carriages that George Washington signed into law in 1794. The Court upheld it, a precedent wealth tax supporters point to today. For more on that story, listen to the Planet Money episode I did, "The Carriage Tax."

Stay tuned for more wealth tax coverage in the Planet Money newsletter in the future. We guess this movement isn't going away.

#5917. Cold Seawater Is Supplying District Heat in Esbjerg, Denmark

Esbjerg, Denmark, is using large electric heat pumps to extract heat from the North Sea and supply its district-heating network. During a two-hour winter test, seawater entered the system at 3.3°C and left at 1.2°C. The two heat pumps consumed 19.9 MW of electricity and supplied 55.2 MW of heat at a district-heating water temperature of 90°C. The difference, 35.3 MW, came from the seawater. The project team’s May 2025 operating report recorded a coefficient of performance, or COP, of 2.8. The figures describe that test, not annual average performance.

A heat pump transfers thermal energy from a colder source to a warmer destination. Its refrigerant absorbs heat at a temperature below that of the seawater, and an electrically driven compressor raises the temperature so the heat can enter the district network. Esbjerg uses carbon dioxide as its refrigerant in a high-pressure cycle. For each unit of electricity used by the compressors during the test, the plant delivered 2.8 units of heat. Resistance heating would have required 2.8 times as much electricity for the same heat output under those conditions.

The installation has a nominal heating capacity of 70 MW and began delivering heat in late 2024, as part of Esbjerg’s replacement of its retired coal-fired combined heat and power plant. The operating report describes controls for variable conditions and protection against freezing as seawater temperatures fall. The recorded COP of 2.8 was lower than the more favourable performance figures used in the original project expectations, and full guarantee testing had not been completed when the results were published. The city’s heating supply also includes a wood-chip boiler and waste-incineration heat. Emissions reductions reported for the wider replacement programme cannot be attributed in full to the seawater heat pumps.

Electricity price and heating-network temperature have a direct effect on the cost. At an illustrative electricity price of €100/MWh, compressor power at COP 2.8 costs about €36 per MWh of useful heat. That excludes seawater pumping, equipment maintenance, capital recovery, financing and heat distribution. A network able to operate at lower water temperatures generally asks the compressor to make a smaller temperature increase, which can improve COP. Older networks supplying 90°C heat face a harder operating requirement than newer, lower-temperature networks.

Esbjerg builds on decades of seawater heating experience. Monaco began using seawater heat pumps in 1963, and Stockholm has used large seawater-sourced district-heating equipment since the 1980s. Creative Energy’s Horseshoe Bay development in West Vancouver takes a different approach. Submerged heat exchangers transfer heat through a closed loop, with no seawater intake or discharge to the marina. Denmark’s Aalborg project is developing 177 MW of seawater heat-pump capacity. These installations serve different loads and use different marine arrangements, so their costs cannot be assumed to match Esbjerg’s.

Marine heat systems require more than suitable water temperatures. Engineers must address corrosion, biological fouling, intake and discharge effects, pumping requirements, maintenance access and freezing protection. The environmental assessment has to consider the local marine ecosystem and the discharge of colder water. The distribution network can be the larger financial obstacle in areas without existing pipes or concentrated heat demand. Wastewater, industrial waste heat, ground-source systems or individual air-source heat pumps may provide lower-cost heat at some sites.

Hot-water storage can add value by allowing a utility to make some heat when electricity is inexpensive and deliver it later. The available flexibility depends on storage capacity, network temperatures and heat demand. Esbjerg’s test provides direct evidence that cold seawater can support high-temperature district heating. Coastal utilities can use that evidence when comparing local heat sources, network investments and the costs of replacing combustion-based heating.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines Esbjerg’s operating results, experience in Stockholm and Monaco, the Horseshoe Bay system near Vancouver, and the cost and environmental factors that determine where seawater heating is suitable.
