#5822. Why 17th-Century Parisian Streetlights Were The OG Flock Cameras

For the past couple years, Flock Safety and its vast network of spy cameras have been all over the news, and for numerous reasons. Whether you've heard about them because of the staggering number of Flock cameras dotting US highways, because of the CEO calling Flock detractors terrorists, or the massive number of cops that keep getting arrested for illegally using automatic license plate readers (ALPRS) to stalk people, one thing's for sure: You've definitely seen news about this state-sponsored private surveillance network (and its similarly dubiously-intentioned competitors, like Axon) for one reason or another. But you might be surprised to learn that you can trace a line back through history from modern surveillance tech like ALPRs back to the some of the first streetlights, which appeared in Paris in the late 1600s.

Like ALPRs, these public lights weren't well-loved by the public, especially by revolutionaries and the lower classes, who were particularly affected by the policing the lights enabled. The similarities don't end there, though. Just like people today love vandalizing Flock cameras and other ALPRs, the Parisians of the 17th and 18th centuries loved smashing the lanterns lighting up the city streets. Like today, such vandalism carried significant legal ramifications, but people still saw fit to express their discontent by destroying the lights, a behavior that only became more popular with the beginning of the French Revolution.

It seems like the French have always been creative and prolific protesters, and the ongoing 2026 French student protests we're seeing today are just a continuation of that legacy. So maybe it should come as no surprise that the French showed us how to push back against state and private surveillance alike more than 300 years ago.

Today, streetlights are abundant, and they've become a completely normalized part of society. You'll find them all over in urban areas, and they line many sections of highway, as well. They're so prevalent that you might only notice their omnipresent status when one goes out in the city, or on a section of road that's suddenly surprisingly dark. With them being such a mainstay of our everyday lives, it may seem difficult to imagine streetlights as surveillance technology, but that is exactly what they were used for when they first started cropping up in European cities a few hundred years ago.

The first street lights in Paris were installed during the reign of King Louis XIV while the night life of aristocrats was booming, but murder rates in the city meant partying all night wasn't safe for the wealthy. The King appointed a head of police who turned to public lighting to solve the issue, which kept aristocrats safe while also enabling increased scrutiny on poor people and vagrants.

While the lamps were initially intended to curtail murder rates, the ruling class quickly realized it could use them to police activities of all types, and not just crimes. The technology enabled the French government to crackdown on vagrancy, petty crime, and even political dissidence on a level never seen before.

If that sounds familiar, it's because ALPRs are enabling similar — but even more wide-reaching — policing behaviors today. Except ALPR technology isn't that accurate and its warrantless police surveillance has led to several false arrests and accusations. For a comprehensive look at the trajectory from public lighting to ALPRs making detailed profiles of your car's movements across the country, check out this detailed and thoroughly-sourced video from one of my favorite YouTubers, Design Theory.

→ Baca artikel asli

#5821. The Jackery HomePower 2000 Plus v2 Red Cross Edition — CleanTechnica Tested

Jackery was one of the earliest portable power station producers to bring these beautiful creations into the mainstream. They have a line of portable power stations and proprietary portable, foldable solar panels that continues to make them one of the top go-to options for anyone looking for power on the go.

Recently, they launched a Red Cross Edition of their popular HomePower 2000 Plus v2 that donates a portion of the profits to the Red Cross to support first responders. We covered these limited edition units when they first hit our radar during our visit to Jackery’s headquarters in Shenzhen, China, and now they are finally moving into production.

Jackery donates 10% of profits from this series to donate portable power stations to rescue organizations and emergency response teams, supporting disaster relief and recovery efforts. At the volumes Jackery is producing, that’s a meaningful step in the right direction to putting portable power stations into the hands of the first responders who need them most.

Disclaimer: Jackery provided the Red Cross edition of the 2000 Pro V2 portable power station to the author for the purposes of this review.

Charging up the unit is a simple matter thanks to its ability to pull a charge from multiple sources. Right out of the gate, you can charge from a normal 120V AC wall outlet at around 1,600 watts. This is typically the maximum you can safely charge from any wall outlet without overheating cables or outlets, and the Jackery HomePower 2000 Plus V2 makes full use of it. In our testing, we pulled 1,590W from the wall for a full charge from empty in an hour and a half.

You can also charge up the unit with solar panels, which with 800 watts of solar can recharge the battery from fully drained to overflowing in 3.2 hours. Jackery uses a proprietary solar connector, so keep that in mind before you buy your battery and/or panels.

You can also charge it up via your car’s 12V automotive outlet (requires optional car charging adapter for $29.90), which can push power out at about 100 watts. This varies slightly from car to car, but the fact of the matter is that you’re generally limited based on the factory wiring for what that outlet can handle.

Jackery knew about that, so they developed their own DC-to-DC charger for combustion vehicles. This optional accessory costs $239, but taps into the full power of your alternator and can pull up to 600 watts. This is a faster charge and can top up the battery in about 4 hours, but requires a bit of extra planning and work and assumes you’re running a combustion vehicle. It’s worth consulting with a professional if you’re going to go this route, as alternator wiring and max output varies greatly from car to car.

Electric vehicles typically don’t have a 12V outlet, opting for USB-C instead. Unfortunately, the Jackery HomePower 2000 Plus V2 Red Cross Edition is not able to charge up via USB-C at any power level. Fortunately, many electric cars come with integrated AC outlets, which can be used to charge the battery up in a pinch.

I tested this in my Cybertruck and achieved similar charging rates to what I was able to achieve at home, which tops the battery up in about an hour and a half. This is a great option to use while fast charging on a road trip because you can charge for about 30 minutes at a time to keep your portable power station charged up without having to run the inverter while driving, which drains the battery faster.

When it’s time to tap into all that stored power, the Jackery HomePower 2000 Plus v2 Red Cross Edition is ready. It can push out 2,400 watts of continuous power with surge capability of up to 4,800W. Given that this is a compact 2 kWh unit weighing just 21 lb, that’s a pretty solid level of power output that it can handle. Power output has continuously been improving with each successive generation and Jackery continues to be near the top of the pack with its numbers.

It’s obviously up to the task of powering lightweight items like drones, laptops, portable lights, charging radios, keeping the internet on, and even powering your fridge in an emergency. On the home front, it’s also a great option. Thanks to its LFP chemistry, Jackery expects you to get 6,000 charging cycles out of this battery, which equates to more than 16 years of daily cycling.

Even though it is such a compact unit, it should be able to keep essentials running at home — including your refrigerator, freezer, computer, lights, smartphone, Wi-Fi, etc. — for nearly 10 hours. That should get you through most outages, and, honestly, if you’re having longer outages than that, you probably want to look into a home battery system. Or you can tap into the HomePower 2000 Plus v2 Red Cross Edition’s ace up its sleeve….

The Jackery HomePower 2000 Plus V2 Red Cross edition can actually add more capacity. It has a special port on the left side of the unit that lets you plug in any of Jackery’s Battery Plus expansion batteries for up to 12kWh of total capacity. That’s a 6× increase over this unit’s installed capacity and lets you scale your storage capacity as you find new use cases for it.

The use case for this at home is really keeping the lights and and the refrigerator running during an emergency and using solar or a generator to charge up when it runs low. It can absorb up to 800 watts of solar from an attached array, meaning you can fully charge it in about an hour and a half depending on the sunlight you’re getting. Even in terrible conditions, it’s still going to charge up in just a couple of hours.

We put this beast to use running a portable refrigerator, lights, USB, charging our laptop and phones, and even charging a few portable batteries for us to take with us hiking. This unit kept our devices charged up for several days of fun in the desert. It was great knowing that after we were done charging our devices, the battery would top itself up again from the attached solar panels.

No matter how many devices we plugged in, including our usual kitchen torture test, its 2,400W of power output was more than enough to keep everything running. The surge power is pretty cool too because it allows the unit to absorb the spikes in consumption that come from a lot of devices when you first turn them on.

But we wanted more. So we pulled out some new winter heaters and really made the Jackery sweat. Each of these heating units can pull up to 1,500 watts each, which is about the max you can get from a standard 15A outlet. We plugged both into the Jackery and cranked them up to the max. I’m personally thrilled to have more energy-hungry appliances to torture — I mean test — portable power stations with.

Playing with all of their settings, we had them pulling almost 3,100 watts constantly before the Jackery finally decided to throw in the towel. It kept them running for several minutes over its own 2,400 watt constant power output rating, which was impressive, especially considering how compact this thing is.

It’s exciting to see Jackery continuing to push into new battery technologies, but in this case, this is the same battery they sell as a normal unit, just rebranded to highlight the great work being done at the Red Cross and to give some of the profits back in the form of product.

That’s worth celebrating, and it’s exciting to see Jackery leading the charge to enable first responders with technology without charging governments or consumers more money for the same product.

Or maybe you just like a bright red unit that’ll make it hard to lose or forget. Whether you’re heading out on a camping trip or heading back to the house after a long week out in nature, the bright red colorway of this unit is sure to stand out.

For more information about the Jackery HomePower 2000 Plus Red Cross Edition or to purchase one for yourself, head over to Jackery’s official website.

I'm a tech geek passionately in search of actionable ways to reduce the negative impact my life has on the planet, save money and reduce stress. Live intentionally, make conscious decisions, love more, act responsibly, play. The more you know, the less you need. As an activist investor, Kyle owns long term stock holdings in Tesla and Rivian.

#5820. Smarter Cooling, Stronger Reliability: Advancing Wind Turbine Gearbox Performance

As the wind energy industry matures, operators and service providers are increasingly focused on maximizing turbine availability while controlling long-term maintenance costs. Although major drivetrain components often receive the spotlight, one overlooked system can have an outsized impact on turbine reliability and performance: gearbox oil cooling.

Heat exchangers play a critical role in maintaining proper oil temperature and viscosity within wind turbine gearboxes. When cooling systems become clogged with airborne debris, dust, or organic material, oil temperatures rise, lubrication performance declines, and component stress increases. Over time, these conditions can contribute to reduced gearbox efficiency, elevated maintenance demands, and avoidable downtime.

“Wind operators today are balancing increasing performance expectations with the realities of aging fleets and rising maintenance costs. Technologies that improve cooling efficiency and reduce service exposure can have a meaningful impact on long-term turbine reliability and operational profitability,” according to Mike Erickson, HYDAC’s Market Manager for the Wind Industry.

Traditional “panel cut” heat exchanger designs have long been susceptible to clogging due to their tightly packed fin structures. In many wind environments — particularly agricultural, dusty, or high-pollen regions — debris accumulation can severely restrict airflow. Oil leaks can further compound the issue by trapping contaminants within the exchanger core, making cleaning more difficult and labor intensive.

This operational challenge has driven the need for more resilient cooling technologies designed specifically for real-world wind farm conditions.

HYDAC’s new “Square Wave” heat exchanger design represents a significant advancement in addressing these reliability concerns. Engineered to allow debris to pass more freely through the exchanger fins, the design minimizes clogging while improving cooling efficiency under demanding operating conditions.

The importance of effective cooling cannot be overstated. Wind turbine gearbox oil typically operates within an optimal temperature range of approximately 45°C to 65°C. Even modest temperature increases can significantly impact oil viscosity. According to field data, a 12°C rise in oil temperature can reduce viscosity by nearly 28 percent — affecting lubrication quality and accelerating wear on critical drivetrain components.

Field trials have demonstrated measurable operational benefits from the Square Wave design. During a one-year trial on GE 1.x turbines, the upgraded exchanger consistently reduced clogging and lowered maintenance requirements compared to traditional panel cut cores. Operators reported reduced up-tower cleaning time and fewer temperature-related cooling faults.

Field validation across multiple turbine platforms has further reinforced the operational advantages of the Square Wave design. In one case study involving legacy 1 MW-class turbines operating in the Pacific Northwest, a major renewable energy operator installed pilot sets of Square Wave heat exchangers as part of a cooling performance evaluation. Installation teams reported the upgraded exchangers integrated seamlessly into the existing platform with no retrofit complications.

After initial operating data demonstrated improved cooling consistency, the operator expanded the evaluation program with additional units. Long-term SCADA analysis later showed turbines equipped with Square Wave technology operate approximately 20 to 25 percent cooler than comparable turbines using conventional heat exchanger designs under similar operating conditions. The data also highlighted another important advantage: the Square Wave cores continued to maintain airflow and cooling efficiency in debris-heavy environments where traditional exchanger designs became increasingly restricted over time.

The successful deployment also generated interest from additional wind service organizations seeking retrofit-ready cooling upgrades that could improve turbine reliability while reducing maintenance burdens across aging fleets.

“What operators are increasingly recognizing is that small improvements in thermal management can create meaningful gains in turbine reliability, maintenance efficiency, and long-term asset performance,” said Erickson. “The field data we’re seeing continues to validate the value of designing cooling systems specifically for real-world wind operating environments.”

More recently, long-term SCADA data from Mitsubishi platforms further validated the technology’s performance. After two years of operation, turbines equipped with Square Wave heat exchangers operated approximately 19 to 25 percent cooler than reference turbines using standard exchanger designs under comparable operating conditions.

For wind asset owners and service providers, the implications are substantial: Improved thermal management supports healthier oil viscosity, more stable gearbox operation, and potentially longer component life. At the same time, reduced cleaning frequency and fewer cooling-related service events translate directly into lower operational expenditures and increased turbine availability.

As the industry continues to prioritize lifecycle optimization and energy production efficiency, innovative subsystem upgrades such as advanced heat exchanger technology are becoming increasingly valuable. Rather than waiting for costly failures or performance degradation, operators are seeking practical retrofit solutions that improve reliability without requiring major system redesigns.

“Effective thermal management is critical to protecting gearbox health and maintaining oil performance over time. By addressing one of the most common causes of cooling degradation — debris accumulation — operators can improve uptime while reducing unnecessary maintenance interventions,” said HYDAC’s Erickson.

For wind stakeholders evaluating ways to improve gearbox reliability, reduce maintenance exposure, and enhance turbine performance, cooling system optimization deserves greater attention. Proven retrofit technologies like advanced heat exchanger designs can offer a practical path toward improved uptime and operational efficiency without requiring major system overhauls.

As fleets age and performance expectations continue to rise, collaboration between operators, service providers, and technology partners will be essential to building more resilient wind assets for the future. Now is the time to evaluate whether existing cooling systems are supporting — or limiting — long-term turbine reliability goals.

“HYDAC’s goal is to help wind owners and service providers solve practical operational challenges in ways that create long-term value across the entire asset lifecycle. Innovations like the Square Wave heat exchanger are designed to improve reliability, simplify maintenance, and support greater energy production over time.”

Don’t let cooling system limitations impact turbine performance.

Proactive cooling optimization can help operators reduce maintenance exposure, protect critical drivetrain components, and maximize energy production across the asset lifecycle. To learn how HYDAC’s Square Wave heat exchanger technology is helping wind operators improve reliability and lower operating costs, connect with our wind energy specialists today or visit: https://www.hydac.com/en-us/industry-solutions/renewable-energy/wind-power/

#5819. Here Are 5 Of Today's Best Gas Sport Compacts, Ranked By Horsepower

Enthusiasts like to moan about how performance cars aren't as common or affordable as they used to be, but if you actually look at the market and account for inflation, we're sort of living through a golden age of sport compact cars right now. Toyota, Honda, Volkswagen, Subaru, and even Hyundai all make go-fast versions of their entry-level compacts. They're all scrappy, front- or all-wheel-drive machines that have hatches, trunks, and back seats, and most are still available with manual transmissions.

Some are easier to get ahold of than others, but for now, they all sticker at or under $50,000. They all make around 300 horsepower, an amount that we at Jalopnik have determined is the correct amount of horsepower. And we've lined up the best gas-powered sport compacts on the market right now (sorry, Ioniq 5 N Stans), ranking them on how many ponies their little engines put out.

To the continued disappointment of its fans, Subaru hasn't made a bona fide WRX STI since 2022, but the less powerful, current WRX still arguably lands in, or at least close enough to, the 300-horsepower ballpark. Subie's street-legal rally machine makes 271 hp out of its turbocharged 2.4-liter flat-four engine. You can get it with a six-speed manual, but also a continuously variable transmission (CVT) that the automaker euphemistically calls the Subaru Performance Transmission if you so choose.

This being a Subaru, symmetrical all-wheel drive is standard, and my brief time behind the wheel confirms that it is quite good on a loose surface. On-road, its inputs feel looser than those of its rivals, the powertrain can feel a tad agricultural, and as you'd probably assume from the numbers, it isn't quite as potent. On this list, the WRX may come last in terms of power, but if it's any consolation, it costs a good amount less than the top performers. A new base trim introduced for 2026 starts at just $33,690 (including a $1,195 destination charge).

Also keeping the affordable sport compact flame alive is the $36,595 Hyundai Elantra N. This car's turbocharged 2.0-liter four-cylinder makes 276 hp if you opt for the six-speed manual, and the power is the same most of the time with the available seven-speed dual-clutch automatic. The latter, however, comes with an "N Grin Shift" push-to-pass-style boost system that amps things up to 286 hp temporarily. That's still not enough to shift its ranking on this list, but an extra 10 hp is an extra 10 hp.

In practice, raw speed is one of the less notable aspects of the Elantra N experience. It sounds rude and burbly, its limited-slip differential, sticky tires, and huge brakes make it an absolute treat to carve up a backroad in, and despite being on the cheaper end of the spectrum, it has one of the nicer interiors in this class. Hyundai has already unveiled the new-gen Elantra, which means an N version of that is very likely around the corner, but this one is still a good deal while it lasts.

Speaking of corners, the Toyota GR Corolla is a car that absolutely loves 'em. Can't live without them, really. It's still a firecracker down the straights, though, pumping out 300 horsepower on the dot out of its 1.6-liter turbocharged three-cylinder — yes, three-cylinder. It's the only car on here that doesn't have four pistons. A six-speed manual is the standard gearbox, naturally, but Toyota added an available eight-speed auto starting from the 2025 model year.

Out of all the cars on this list, the GR Corolla feels the rowdiest to drive. The least refined, the least luxurious, and the least concerned about comfort, tech amenities, or cargo space. But that's arguably where its charm lies. That three-cylinder engine always sounds like it's about to explode, while GR-Four AWD and Torsen limited-slip diffs front and rear make it feel like the rally machine that it is, constantly. But if it's pure horsepower you're after, there are a couple of hot hatchbacks that trounce it. A 2026 Toyota GR Corolla with the manual transmission starts at $41,815.

One of the cars that trounces the GR Corolla (relatively speaking, of course) is the mighty Honda Civic Type R, which makes 315 hp out of its turbocharged 2.0-liter four-cylinder engine. Like the Elantra, power goes to the front wheels only, and the Type R has the distinction of being the only vehicle here to be offered exclusively with a manual transmission. Once you've driven it, though, that decision makes a whole lot more sense, because not only does this car have the best shifter in this class hands-down, it's one of the best shifters ever made, regardless of segment or age. Add in a weapon of a front axle, steering to die for, and a chassis that makes some of the other cars on this list feel floppy, and you have one of the best performance cars available today, period.

This car gets a mild update for 2027 with improved engine cooling, tweaked damping in +R mode, better rev-matching, and a brake pedal that's said to feel more direct. It starts at $49,990, although finding a dealer who will actually sell it to you for that number may take some effort.

As great as that Civic is, it isn't the most powerful gas-powered hot hatch available today. That honor goes to the Volkswagen Golf R, which makes 328 hp out of its turbocharged 2.0-liter, sending power to all four wheels. VW used to sell this with either a six-speed manual or a seven-speed dual-clutch auto, but as of the 2025 refresh, the Golf R is auto-only. Perhaps the same lean toward outright pace that gives it the most horsepower in its class was also responsible for it ditching the manual.

The Golf R is an undoubtedly speedy vehicle. Behind the wheel, it feels relatively composed and like a buttoned-down brand of hot hatchery, which pairs well with its very practical, luxury car-adjacent interior. On the flip side, that also means it likely won't be your first choice if you're looking for an unapologetic sense of theater or fun. But if you must have the one car in this class that'll win the most spec sheet comparisons, the VW Golf R is your guy. The 2027 Volkswagen Golf R starts at $51,320 (assuming the $1,275 destination charge carries over from 2026).

#5818. Ford Drops Hint About New Fathom EV Battery Life

The Ford Motor Company has been playing its forthcoming Fathom electric pickup truck close to the vest, releasing few details ahead of volume production except for the MSRP of $28,350. Still, some clues have dropped. The latest news involves a newly released real-life data analysis of five-year battery capacity for the Ford Mustang Mach-E. The findings raise expectations for the next generation of Ford EVs, namely, the Fathom, which is the first vehicle to roll down the tree-like assembly line of the company’s new “Universal EV” platform.

On one level, the comparison between the Mach-E and the Fathom EV battery is apples-to-oranges. The 2021 Mach-E deployed [correction: NCM (nickel-cobalt-manganese) batteries with Standard Range at 68 kWh usable capacity as launched, and Extended Range at 88 kWh usable capacity as launched]. Ford is equipping the Fathom with an LFP (lithium-iron-phosphate) EV battery. LFP is a relatively new, more economical formula than Li-ion. All else being equal the tradeoff is lower range, though some Ford watchers point out that drivers can order their Fathom with an optional extended-range battery.

The point is that the 2021 Mach-E is old news. The new news is the forthcoming Fathom, which Ford has been regularly teasing as the first product of its secret, not-so-secret “skunkworks” team for the past five years or so. The addition of a cost-cutting, internal inverter for bidirectional charging is the topic of the most recent news drop, indicating that Ford expects Fathom drivers to use their EV battery for stationary applications as well as drive time.

Drivers who routinely use the bidirectional feature will likely put their Fathom through more charging cycles than a typical EV, and prospective buyers will need some assurance that their zero emission pickup will be up to the task.

And, that’s where the 2021 Mach-E data comes in. Ford has just released the 5-year numbers for retained battery capacity over time, and they look pretty good. By releasing the data now, when the Fathom is nearing volume production, Ford has raised expectations that the new electric pickup truck will deliver similar, if not better, longevity.

Correction: The Mach-E data doesn’t just look pretty good, it looks very good. Ford deployed its telematics platform to assess more than 36,000 Mustang Mach E’s in the “class of 2021,” driven in the US and Canada over an average ownership period of 5.2 years as of August 2026, during which time drivers clocked a collective 2 billion miles.

The 2021 Mach-E was sold with both standard and extended range battery packs, and the data combines both variations.

Relative to the Fathom’s bidirectional charging feature, the key finding is that high-mileage vehicles scored well. “More than 2,000 owners have crossed 100,000 miles on their original battery pack while maintaining >91% average health,” Ford explains. In the 2021 cohort, 30 drivers also ran past 200,000 miles while retaining an average capacity of about 88%.

Ford, which graciously shared an embargoed press statement with CleanTechnica over the weekend, also emphasizes that 95% of the 2021 Mach-E’s landed between 87% and 97% health, indicating that a relatively small number of outliers brought the average down to 92% overall.

The Mach-E data will surprise nobody who has been paying attention to the latest news about EV battery lifespan. As it turns out, EV batteries last far longer than first anticipated. That’s significant enough when an EV battery is deployed for mobility purposes. Capacity retention takes on extra importance when the same battery is used to manage electricity use in homes and other buildings.

Ford provided some insights last month, when it posted a long form article describing the Fathom’s built-in inverter. An inverter is a device that converts the DC current from an EV battery into the AC current used in homes. A typical bidirectional charging system requires the property owner to install a wall-mounted inverter, adding another layer of expense. Ford has absorbed the cost of the inverter into the Fathom itself, a decision aimed at encouraging drivers to make use of bidirectional charging at home. The company has also been working with utilities to help motivate drivers to take advantage of the feature.

With bidirectional charging, EVs realize their full potential as rolling, zero emission power generating stations. Drivers can use bidirectional charging on an emergency basis, to keep the lights on at home in case of a grid power outage. They can also take advantage of utility incentives, which reward EV owners for deploying their stored ectricity during periods of peak demand or other stresses on the grid, benefiting all ratepayers in the service territory.

Some US utilities already offer discounted or even free rates for EV owners who charge up their battery during off-peak hours. Try that with your gasmobile!

Stay tuned to this channel for further details about the Fathom and its battery. In the meantime, some Ford dealers have been passing along information from the automaker’s HQ. Here’s a representative example from the DC-area dealer Koons Ford:

“Battery options beyond the standard-range configuration have not been announced yet. Ford has confirmed a long-range battery option is planned and that its upcharge will be significantly lower than what Lightning buyers experienced, but specific range figures and pricing tiers are not yet public. This page will be updated as Ford confirms additional specifications.”

Okay, so much for that. Tonkin Hillsboro Ford in Oregon is hardly more forthcoming:

“Ford Fathom pre-orders will begin in early 2027, with a starting MSRP of $28,350 for the standard-range battery. While some specifications are still to come, there’s already plenty to know about this new Ford electric truck.”

“Ford has not yet released projected EPA-estimated driving range figures for the Fathom,” Tonkin elaborates. “The vehicle is currently being introduced with a standard-range battery, but an official EPA range estimate isn’t available yet.”

“This is one area where shoppers will need to wait for additional information,” the dealer adds.

Hmmm…let’s turn to the automotive press, where EV Charging Stations was somewhat more forthcoming. “The 2027 Ford Fathom electric pickup truck will not exceed 300 miles of range in its base form, but it likely will with the optional larger battery,” EVCS reports.

“The Ford Fathom has a 400-volt LFP battery system and 48-volt auxiliary electrical architecture. The truck features a cell-to-pack battery system (no modules), with prismatic battery cells being structural elements of the vehicle,” EVCS elaborates, further speculating that the extended-range battery could involve a higher-performing LFP formula or a switchover to the familiar NMC (nickel-manganese-cobalt) concoction.

EVCS also calculates that the standard EV battery will yield a range of under 220 miles, with the extended range version coming in at 300 miles, as Ford has indicated.

Meanwhile, the Detroit Free Press has noticed that a fully loaded Fathom with all the bells and whistles still comes in around the $40,000 mark, well below the $54,000 average cost of new EVs.

We won’t have long to wait for the full details to come out. Last week, Torque News was among those reporting that the Fathom is well into the final validation stages leading to full volume production.

With sales expected to begin late next year, the Fathom will face some competition from other EV makers that are aiming at the electric pickup truck market. One pickup stakeholder that Ford won’t have to worry about, though is Tesla, which is planning to halt production of the Cybertruck next year.

Photo: Ford is drawing attention to the long lifespan of the 2021 Mustang Mach-E EV battery, setting high expectations for the forthcoming Fathom electric pickup truck (courtesy of Ford via CleanTechnica archive).

#5817. Solar and storage companies voice support for federal permitting reform

Hundreds of solar and storage companies have co-signed a letter addressed to Congress in support of the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026 (BAAJA) (S. 5653).

“You can’t lower energy costs if you can’t build the energy projects, and these companies know that this bipartisan legislation will help get America building again,” said Tim Pawlenty, CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA), in a press release.

BAAJA was introduced in the Senate on Sept. 30 by Sen. Shelly Moore Capito (R-WV), and co-sponsored by Sen. Mike Lee (R-UT), Sen. Sheldon Whitehouse (D-RI) and Sen. Martin Heinrich (D-NM). The legislation is focused on quickening permitting for a range of projects with federal oversight, many of them related to energy production. BAAJA specifically calls out solar photovoltaic systems, energy storage and on- and offshore wind, but also includes language for fossil fuel sources, such as natural gas, oil and coal.

If passed, the Senate bill would amend sections of the National Environmental Policy Act, Clean Water Act and Endangered Species Act of 1973 with the goal of shortening project permitting windows. According to the text, it would change and reduce which projects would need to be reviewed under those acts, such as water quality certifications; shorten the length of review periods for projects; and would not require permits for oil and gas drilling on land not owned by the federal government.

BAAJA would also grant the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission the ability to issue permits for some electricity transmission projects.

According to a SEIA analysis, if BAAJA was passed, solar energy output could increase by 167 GW between 2028 and 2035. The companies that signed this letter to Congress believe that federal permitting practices are outdated, and permitting reform is a solution to lowering energy costs.

“The message from more than 300 companies comes as voters made clear in the midterm elections that lowering energy costs is a top concern,” the group stated in a press release. “Permitting reform is urgently needed to meet rising demand and get more reliable, affordable solar and storage projects built.”

BAAJA is currently under review by the Committee on Energy and Natural Resources.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5816. Which Generation Of Hyundai Tucson Is Safest According To The IIHS

Car safety has come a long way since the days of yore. Ask the Insurance Institute for Highway Safety (IIHS). The organization's crash testing standards have evolved significantly over 30+ years as more new vehicles keep getting better at protecting their occupants. By now, we're pretty familiar with the IIHS Top Safety Pick awards. However, just because your second-gen 2011 Hyundai Tucson has the Top Safety Pick under its belt doesn't mean it's as safe as the third-gen 2021 model, which also happens to be a Top Safety Pick winner.

Although the IIHS rates the small SUV highly, understand that it also keeps updating the award's eligibility criteria as the years go by. For example, headlights were not even part of the Top Safety Pick criteria in 2010, nor were vehicle-to-vehicle or pedestrian crash-prevention features. And Top Safety Pick+ wasn't even a thing before 2013. In fact, looking at the many generations of the small SUV, it's fair to conclude that fourth-generation Tucsons (2022-2026) appear to be the safest, according to the IIHS. Not only have certain fourth-generation Tucsons gone through the organization's slew of updated testing standards, but they have consistently been part of the IIHS Top Safety Pick+ category between 2022 and 2026, unlike the third or prior generation models.

If you're looking for the safest model year within the fourth generation, the 2026 Tucson looks like the strongest candidate. While models built after March 2024 have fared really well in the updated tests, the eligibility criteria for Top Safety Pick+ have changed between 2024 and 2026, with the most recent update pushing automakers to improve their crash avoidance systems and protection for back seat passengers. Still, despite the tougher rules, the 2026 Tucson meets all the requirements for Top Safety Pick+.

Although the 2026 Tucson won the Top Safety Pick+ award, it's worth noting that the small SUV performed poorly in the IIHS's latest whiplash prevention test. This replaces the original head restraint test that was discontinued in 2022. The new test evaluates three important aspects: "head and spine support, the interaction of the head with the head restraint, and how well the seat handles the energy transfer during impact."

If it means anything, only four out of the first 18 small SUVs tested scored really well and earned a Good rating, according to the IIHS. Although the Tucson isn't part of the group, that's not to say no Hyundai is — the Ioniq 5 was among the SUVs that scored a Good rating, as are the Subaru Forester, Toyota RAV4, and Audi Q3. Joining the Tucson with a poor whiplash prevention score are the Ford Bronco Sport and Mazda CX-50.

According to the IIHS, the Tucson's head restraint pushed the dummy's chin down toward the chest. While most of the vehicles tested were 2025 models, the IIHS says these ratings can apply to certain 2026 models as well. Something similar happened with the aforementioned second-generation Tucson as well. Although all model years except 2014 and 2015 were awarded the Top Safety Pick, they performed poorly in the driver's side small overlap test. IIHS noted that the side curtain airbags didn't deploy, the seatbelts allowed excessive movement, and the dummy's head barely contacted the front airbag. Third-gen and later Tucsons, however, performed considerably better in the test.

Notably, the fourth-gen Tucson received Good ratings in every other area except its LATCH ease of use and headlights (for trims other than Limited), where it received Acceptable ratings.

#5815. EVs Displace 5 Times More Oil Globally Than They Did 5 Years Ago — Analysis

Five years on from Glasgow, EVs have proved the single most effective way to curb oil use, says T&E.

At COP26 in Glasgow, twenty-four countries and a group of leading car manufacturers committed to ending the era of fossil-fuel powered vehicles by 2040 “or earlier”. Five years and two energy crises later, oil savings from EVs globally are up fivefold, new T&E analysis shows. EVs are the single most effective way of cutting oil demand, improving energy security and reducing emissions, says T&E.

While battery electric vehicles (BEVs) represent just 4.2% of the global fleet, they now account for 25% of all new car sales worldwide – up from 6.6% five years ago. This surge has pushed oil displacement from 215,000 barrels a day in 2021 to 1.2 million barrels a day today, or roughly 1.2% of global consumption.

At the same time, electric buses now make up 5% of the global fleet, battery electric trucks are at 1% but in some countries exceed 50% of new sales. T&E estimates these vehicles avoid as much as 320,000 barrels a day. As road transport burns close to half of all the world’s oil, transitioning to EVs is crucial in reducing oil dependency. As the global car and truck fleet continues to grow, EVs are the only thing stopping runaway oil demand, warns T&E.

As electrification takes center stage at this year’s COP, current policies put battery electric cars on track to displace roughly 5.8 million barrels of oil a day by 2034 — matching the sustained disruption seen during the 2026 crisis. Accelerating EV adoption will be crucial to preventing future oil shocks, according to T&E.

Antony Froggatt, senior director at T&E, said: “Electric vehicles are the single most effective way of cutting oil demand. Right now the only winners in our oil addiction are fossil fuel giants and petrostates. Places with near 100% sales of EVs like Norway, Denmark and Hong Kong have effectively insulated themselves from future shocks. Five years after Glasgow, EVs stand as one of the clearest victories in global climate history. At this year’s COP, world leaders must set binding electrification targets. Not just for the climate, but for national security.”

BEVs on the road now avoid more than 200 Mt of CO2-equivalent a year. According to T&E, rapid transport electrification is a vital part of the wider energy transition by providing essential grid flexibility and storage. Just as past COPs accelerated the global boom in wind and solar, backing for electric vehicles at COP31 can deliver the same breakthrough, slashing oil demand and driving emissions down.

[1] Displaced oil is estimated from daily per vehicle savings: about 0.019 barrels per car (derived from Ember), 0.2 per truck (IEA) and 0.15 per bus (bottom-up from typical fuel use and mileage). The 2026 supply loss has been estimated at 5.8 million barrels a day. We use IEA fleet projections for 2030 and 2035 fleet sizes to calculate the offset year of the BEV car fleet. #5814. At $8,000, Could This 1977 Cadillac Seville Milan Get You To Go Short?

Custom-built by Millan Coach Builders of Simi Valley, California, today's Nice Price or No Dice Cadillac Milan is one of around 500 converted and truncated two-seaters. Let's see if its price will make short business of securing a sale.

For nearly a decade and a half in the 1980s and '90s, Chrysler's K-Car served as the basis for over three-quarters of the company's automotive output. During that span, the humble FWD platform spawned nearly fifty different models, encompassing family cars, wagons, sports cars, limos, and Chrysler's savior minivans.

We looked at an amazingly clean example of one of those models last Friday: a 1986 Chrysler LeBaron Town & Country with fewer than 4,000 miles. It was a total throwback, and in beautiful shape. Still, nice as it was, few of you wanted to step into that time machine, especially at its $18,900 asking price. Ultimately, the comments dunned the LeBaron as just a crappy K Car, with the voting going likewise in an 82% 'No Dice' loss.

In antiquity, Milan, Italy, then known as Mediolanum, served as the capital of the Western Roman Empire. Today it serves as the locus of both Italy's automotive and fashion industries. With all that history, it's surprising that today's 1977 Cadillac Seville Milan isn't named for the Lombardy capital.

Instead, both the car and the company, Milan Coach Builders, were named for the car's designer and company founder, Milan Daniels. Built in small numbers (reportedly fewer than 500), the Milan was not sanctioned by Cadillac or GM.

Private parties would bring their already-purchased Sevilles to Milan's shop, where the cars were cut in half, shrunk from a 114-inch wheelbase to 96 inches, and fitted with a convertible top. Lost in the process were the roof, rear doors, and the back seat. At the time, the idea was to create an American analog to the Mercedes SL, a model that the Milan's dimensions purposely aped. Optionally, Milan Coach Builders also offered a fiberglass hood with a central bump, and an accompanying Rolls-style Parthenon grille.

The result looks less like a Mercedes SL and more like Internet sensation and beloved role model for overcoming adversity, Seattle's Jimothy the Raccoon. That's not a bad association, and considering that upwards of 500 people sent their then-very-expensive Cadillacs to go under the knife, it's obvious these cars have some appeal.

The conversion was only offered on the first-generation Sevilles, and Milan Coach Builders shuttered in the early 1980s after that model ceased production. This one, in white over a white leather interior and charcoal canvas top, is claimed to be in decent condition.

According to the ad's description, it also has had a 6.2-liter Chevy LS3 V8 professionally installed, just 108 miles ago. Overall, the car reportedly has 30,906 miles on the clock. That engine, the seller assures us, provides the car with both a "massive power upgrade and turn key reliability." The seller also boasts that "The shortened wheelbase makes this incredibly nimble compared to a standard Seville," and says that "it is a blast to drive." Aside from the modern Chevy mill, this Milan seems to otherwise maintain its '70s groove.

Aesthetically, the seller comments that the car is in great condition, has been "meticulously cared for and shows beautifully." That's interesting, since the photos in the ad, which appear to have been taken with a pinhole camera by a distracted three-year-old, show several discrepancies. In one shot of the car with the top down—arguably its most flattering pose—a rub strip spans the passenger door, and a bumper gap filler sits at the rear. In another photo showing the car with the top up, both the strip and the filler are absent. That bumper gap filler (made of urethane) is also absent in the trunk shot, as are the trunk lock and Cadillac wreath.

It's hard to say which of those photos represents the car in its current state, although the seller claims the Caddy "shows beautifully," so perhaps the photos showing missing parts are from a previous state, and the car is now in its final, intact form. One thing we do know, from the ad, is that this Milan comes with a clean title.

It's always smart to hedge your bets, and the seller of this Milan has done just that by listing the car on both Craigslist and Facebook Marketplace. The Craigslist ad has been up for about three weeks, which isn't unreasonable for such a unique vehicle seeking an appreciative buyer. The Facebook Marketplace ad, however, has been up for 32 weeks, which shows what a backwater the Meta classifieds can sometimes be. The main discrepancy between the ads, and the likely reason that the seller managed no bites on Facebook, is that there the car is priced at $16,000, while on Craigslist it's asking half that: $8,000. We're going with the Craigslist price, so it's time to weigh in and vote on whether that's a good deal.

What's your take on this Milan and that $8,000 asking price? Does that feel fair given the car's unique history and presentation? Or, even at that lower-than-Facebook price, does this custom Caddy come up short?

San Francisco Bay Area, California, Craigslist, or go here if the ad disappears.

#5813. A review of ‘Solar Roots: The Pioneers of PV’

By Tor “Solar Fred” Valenza | November 10, 2026

Was today’s modern solar industry inspired by off-grid California-living, marijuana-growing, Grateful Dead-loving hippies? That’s the main question explored in “Solar Roots: The Pioneers of PV,” a new documentary that recently premiered in Grass Valley, California.

The entertaining documentary traces the history, colorful characters, off-grid homeowners, solar educators and the entrepreneurs who embraced solar photovoltaic technology for vastly different reasons.

Shot on a shoestring budget with volunteers, “Solar Roots” may not have the polished production of a Hollywood documentary, but it is an untold story about the people and early solar and technology that led to the modern solar industry.

Since 2009, when I first started in the solar industry, I had heard jokes and anecdotes about the marijuana-growing hippies who sparked the residential solar industry. Until “Solar Roots,” no one had ever set up a camera to talk to those early solar pioneers and document why rural America became a hub of off-grid solar innovation.

Enter Jeff Spies, a longtime solar veteran and the documentary’s executive producer. In 2016, Spies organized a weekend party for the off-grid solar pioneers of Northern California, where he started his career in the early 2000s. After hearing so many colorful stories about why these rural homesteaders bought $14/watt solar panels and started new solar installation and distribution businesses, Spies enlisted another solar industry veteran, Jason Vetterli, to travel to Northern California, Colorado and New Mexico to capture these unique and personal stories on video.

This isn’t the first documentary about the solar industry or its challenges, and to be clear, Vetterli and Spies’ narrative is not about creating “the solar biz.” Truly, it’s about a particular tribe of solar early adopters. Through interviews, we learn about the 1970s, ’80s and ’90s counterculture movement and the people who stumbled into solar businesses out of necessity and good timing.

Listening to these stories, you quickly understand that none of these solar pioneers intended to get into the solar business to make millions or solve climate change. Their real motivations were tied to maintaining their independence from cities while having enough electricity to keep the lights on and the music playing while raising a family. Beyond the home applications, solar became a new water-pumping solution that supported the pioneers’ cash-only “agricultural” businesses.

Vetterli sets the film’s tone and theme with the first scene. Instead of fading in to a utility solar farm or suburban residential roofs, the film opens with a young hippie using a wire to connect a single 55-watt ARCO solar panel to a car battery that powers an old cassette boombox. He then inserts a Grateful Dead tape and presses play.

The Grateful Dead-inspired musical score, composed by Midnight North, a band whose members include another solar veteran, TJ Kanczuzewski, appropriately plays under the rest of the film’s narrative and interviews.

After a brief solar technology history lesson, Spies interviews the solar pioneers about the roots of their solar PV fascination, how they came to realize that solar could grow and impact their lives and their off-grid movement.

This realization wouldn’t have happened without the back-to-the-land movement. Buying land was inexpensive but getting a utility to build a power line to DIY houses in rural California cost millions. While living without electricity, we learn about their lives before and after PV.

Before PV, the pioneers had to jerry-rig their pickup trucks’ batteries to listen to music and use dangerous kerosene lanterns for light. Gasoline generators were loud, costly, required maintenance and were contrary to the quiet, remote lifestyle they wanted. After PV (and lead-acid batteries), the solar pioneers could enjoy light, music, small DC refrigerators for cold beer and cold cuts, 12-volt TVs and other DC devices that were designed for camping.

After implementing solar PV into their own homesteads, the pioneers wanted to share their find with their neighbors. It was that desire to share the off-grid solar PV love that led to the early solar distributor businesses, publications and training centers — not a desire to make millions.

As businesses grew, cultures clashed. We hear the story of when corporate solar salespeople traveled to Humboldt County in three-piece suits to meet their best customers who are wearing T-shirts and jeans and carry the scent and cash of a “weedpreneur.”

While the majority of the film is about the people who wanted an off-grid lifestyle, Vetterli and Spies also interview the late 1990s PV code and policy pioneers. These solar people also wore suit, but they also saw grid-tied potential to make solar mainstream.

“Solar Roots” does a great job covering this off-grid to grid-tied transition, but the film only covers a fraction of the stories and people who inspired the multi-gigawatt and global solar industry that exists today, and that’s O.K. A single two-hour documentary could never cover all of the world’s solar pioneers, but we’re fortunate to have met these particular solar people. They may not have started the residential solar industry, but they certainly contributed to it.

Spies and Vetterli plan to release the entire documentary online in the coming months and have set up a mailing list at www.solar-roots.com for people to receive updates.

Tor “Solar Fred” Valenza is a former film and TV writer (Stargate SG-1, Dharma & Greg, The Dead Zone) and current chief solar public relations geek at UnThink Solar, a solar PR and communications firm.

→ Baca artikel asli

#5812. BAAJA Remains Fundamentally Flawed Despite Manager’s Amendment

Senate Must Allow for an Open Amendment Process

Washington, DC — In the continued rush to hold a vote during the lame duck session on the “Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026”, the senators crafting the bill released a manager’s amendment on Saturday. The Senate has scheduled a procedural vote to begin consideration of the draft legislation tomorrow.

On Friday, Sierra Club issued a statement that it could not support the permitting bill as currently written, outlining fundamental changes that must be made. The manager’s amendment partially addressed these concerns, but major substantive issues remain.

In response, Sierra Club Executive Director Loren Blackford released the following statement:

“The Sierra Club appreciates the improvements made by the manager’s amendment, but the draft legislation remains deeply flawed. The Senate must allow an open amendment process for additional changes to be made.

“Updating and expediting transmission and clean energy permitting is essential to delivering lower costs, reduced air, water, and climate pollution, protecting communities, and expanding the grid for the American people. But rushing legislation that erodes Tribal sovereignty and people’s right to have a say in what is built in their communities, abandons clean water safeguards, and wildlife protections, while further enabling a rogue president that is waging a war on clean energy and our public lands is not an acceptable outcome.

“Senate Democrats have been given a mandate to act decisively to deliver relief on energy bills and level the playing field for clean energy. They can and must fight for a better deal. Unless additional dialogue and changes can happen, the Sierra Club cannot support this legislation.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5811. São Paulo Grand Prix Proves F1 Can Still Thrill In The Rain

Rain is often called the great equalizer in Formula 1, with slippery conditions making driver ability far more crucial to success than car performance. F1 rekindled its wet-weather magic on Sunday in São Paulo, Brazil. As the dominant Mercedes team faltered, 2025 champion Lando Norris showed poise through changing conditions to take his third victory this season. The weekend at Interlagos broke the recent trend of rain halting on-track action instead of shaking up the competitive order. Actually racing in the rain had become as rare as spotting a dodo bird in the wild.

Saturday previewed both sides of the wet-weather coin. After a heavy downpour delayed Q1 by more than an hour, fans were treated to one of the season's best qualifying sessions. Wily veterans Fernando Alonso and Nico Hülkenberg dragged their subpar equipment into Q3. They were joined in the final segment by Red Bull's often-scrutinized prospects Liam Lawson and Isack Hadjar. In all, nine teams slotted into Q3's 10 spots.

Total frustration for George 😖#F1 #BrazilGP pic.twitter.com/yb6vJsOzOx

An approaching storm cut the fight for pole into a one-lap shootout. Norris put his McLaren on provisional pole, and George Russell hoped to beat the lap time. However, the Mercedes driver was impeded by Williams' Alex Albon and reassured by his team that he could squeeze in a second lap. When heavy showers proved another run was impossible, Russell lashed out over the radio. Team principal Toto Wolff had to chime in to calm him down.

Norris wasn't as fortunate at the start on Sunday. With the track still wet from earlier rain, the field took to the grid on intermediate tires. Mercedes' Kimi Antonelli actually slid off the track on his reconnaissance lap. The new world champion would have much better traction from his second-place grid box. He launched past the McLaren for the lead into Turn 1 with Lawson following him into second. More action unfolded behind the leaders. Despite starting 20th, Lewis Hamilton flew through the field and was ninth by the end of the opening lap. Max Verstappen made it to 11th from 21st.

Watch the seven-time world champ charge through the field at the start 🤩#F1 #BrazilGP pic.twitter.com/M685ysLctw

F1's phenoms didn't just leverage their wet-weather prowess, but also adapted better to the changing conditions. After prodding the Ferrari pit wall, Hamilton was the first driver to switch to slicks on lap 14 of the 71-lap contest. The seven-time champion, making his 400th career start, was immediately the fastest driver on track. With a dry line forming, the challenge was getting the tire up to the optimum temperature on the cool track. Russell spun in the pit exit after his stop. Others ran wide into the runoff areas in corners.

Here's Antonelli's spin at Turn 1 which handed Norris the lead 👀#F1 #BrazilGP pic.twitter.com/bZRcgm07h5

The biggest casualty was Antonelli, who lost the lead to Norris on lap 19 after spinning in Turn 1. He had reported a strange issue where his Mercedes was pulling one side under acceleration. The issue would also crop up on Russell's car. The rain would return with 10 laps to go, but Norris safely swapped back to inters and cruised to the finish line. The only thing that went wrong for Norris was him shattering his champagne bottle on the podium.

While Mercedes clinched the World Constructors' Championship at Interlagos, the factory team didn't have a weekend befitting a title-winning campaign. The errors behind the wheel and in the garage were impossible to ignore, even though Russell and Antonelli finished fourth and fifth, respectively. After the race, the team had no idea what went wrong with either car. Russell shared his thoughts with The Race, stating:

Maybe it's a small theory because we were the first ones onto the inters, even just on that outlap on the driest track, you damage the tires and the temps go up and you're in this negative spiral. As of now, that's the only explanation."

The unpredictability of wet races is always thrilling. It's a shame we can't see racing in the rain more often. The São Paulo Grand Prix thankfully fell within a golden window where the track could be wet without the rooster tails reducing visibility to effectively nothing. The FIA trialed wheel covers in 2024 to reduce the spray, but they had no impact. The reasoning behind them ignored that the car's floor is an aerodynamic device that sucks in water and shoots it out of the rear diffuser. The only guaranteed solution would be to somehow slow the cars down, which would be universally unpopular.

#5810. What Do Patients With Sleep Disorder Really Mean by 'Brain Fog'?

— Social media analysis finds its not just a synonym for fatigue

Brain fog among individuals with sleep disorders was primarily characterized by memory impairment, difficulty concentrating, and forgetfulness, according to a lexicon analysis of social media posts.

Among 554,312 posts and comments in online narcolepsy and idiopathic hypersomnia communities, the top five contextual words associated with brain fog -- unrelated to symptom relief -- were "forgetfulness," "memory," "fatigue," "drunkenness," and "dissociation."

Meanwhile, some of the most common terms associated with symptom alleviation were "clears," "lifted," "lifts," and "clearing," reported Anne Marie Morse, DO, of Geisinger Medical Center in Danville, Pennsylvania, and colleagues.

The findings were presented at the Neuroscience Education Institute (NEI) Fall Congress in Colorado Springs, Colorado.

"'Brain fog' is one of the most burdensome symptoms people with narcolepsy and idiopathic hypersomnia experience, but there is no agreed definition of what it means, whether patients experience the same thing, or specifically whether it varies by condition," Morse told MedPage Today. "Anything we think we know about brain fog comes from clinical or research encounters, where the conversation is already conditioned or biased by the setting."

"In a prior social listening analysis, we evaluated the term's use over time to challenge the idea that this was merely popularized by COVID-related brain fog," she continued. "We found that the term predated COVID by at least a decade, though use grew significantly thereafter alongside research across conditions like celiac disease and fibromyalgia. We wanted to understand how people living with narcolepsy type 1, narcolepsy type 2, and idiopathic hypersomnia describe it on their own, unprompted."

For the social listening analysis, researchers evaluated posts and comments from two subreddits (r/Narcolepsy and r/idiopathichypersomnia) and one Facebook group (PWN4PWN) between August 2011 and September 2025. Most data (85%) originated from r/Narcolepsy. Semantic similarity was calculated using cosine similarity.

The top five non-synonymous terms with the highest semantic similarity to brain fog were "tiredness," "difficulty concentrating," "inability to focus," "poor memory," and "memory issues." Additionally, the top five associated clinical experiences were led by memory-related symptoms and included "impaired memory," "memory dysfunction," "impaired concentration," "fatigue," and "forgetful."

To assess how fatigue and sleepiness influence the meaning of brain fog, authors mentioning either symptom were stratified into four cohorts: fatigue-dominant, sleepiness-dominant (sleepiness exceeded fatigue by <7.5%), very sleepiness-dominant (sleepiness exceeded fatigue by ≥7.5%), and fatigue/sleepiness-balanced.

When comparing the groups, the greatest discrepancy in how brain fog was characterized occurred between the fatigue/sleepiness-balanced and very sleepiness-dominant groups. Authors in the balanced cohort tended to frame brain fog around memory and focus connotations, whereas those in the very sleepiness-dominant group connected the term to energy and motivation, frequently pairing it with time and intensity qualifiers such as "constant," "intense," and "severe."

Morse emphasized that brain fog is not simply a synonym for fatigue or sleepiness. "In the community data, [brain fog] separates out as its own concept, and its meaning has been stable since 2018, which suggests a mature shared vocabulary rather than a passing phrase," she said.

Mentions of brain fog increased annually from 2011 to 2017 before stabilizing at approximately 1% of all posts through 2025. Semantic shifts were subtle, with the most notable transition occurring between 2011 to 2017 and 2018 to 2020, when descriptors shifted away from memory-focused terms toward exhaustion and tiredness. Meaning remained consistent from 2018 through 2025.

"The stability of this vocabulary may potentially be valuable for research," Morse noted. "If people with these conditions use 'brain fog' consistently with the same personal meaning across years and communities, that supports building a patient-reported outcome measure using their language instead of ours, establishing clinical endpoints that reflect what they actually experience."

For practicing clinicians, Morse recommended asking patients about brain fog in their own words. "Treat the answer as its own symptom. Don't translate it into fatigue or sleepiness, although concurrent fatigue or sleepiness may modify the severity of brain fog."

"Patients are telling us that cognition is a core part of how this condition costs them their day -- at school, at work, driving, and being present with family. We must start asking about it specifically rather than relying on a guesstimate from a sleepiness score or missing the discussion entirely," she said.

Morse also noted two key study limitations: online communities are self-selected, reflecting conversational patterns rather than true population prevalence, and clinical diagnoses cannot be independently verified from social media data.

#5809. West Yorkshire’s £229 Million Bus Decisions Will Outlast Its Operating Contracts

West Yorkshire has made two substantial commitments to its bus network. On November 2, FirstGroup announced a £109 million agreement with the West Yorkshire Combined Authority covering five depots, associated infrastructure and 330 buses, including 79 battery-electric vehicles. The authority is also spending £120 million on more than 250 new buses through a separate programme. These investments will help establish the region’s new franchised Weaver Network, but many of the assets being acquired will remain in use long after the first operating contracts have ended.

West Yorkshire’s fleet and depot commitments illustrate a problem explored in my 83-page report, The Bus Is Not the System. The accompanying TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines which decisions are becoming expensive to reverse and what authorities should establish before making their next commitments.

The Weaver Network changes the relationship between public transport planning and private bus operations. The Combined Authority will specify services and retain responsibility for passenger revenue under its initial large contracts, while private operators deliver the routes. The region is securing assets that will remain important regardless of which company operates the buses. In principle, that can make future competition easier because bidders do not each have to provide a new depot and fleet.

The challenge is that buses, operating contracts and infrastructure follow different clocks. An operating contract might last five or seven years. Vehicles can work through successive contracts, while depot buildings and electrical connections may remain useful for decades. A change in route patterns, population or employment locations can make an established depot less convenient. A bus can often be reassigned, but a multi-megawatt electricity connection cannot be transferred to another site because the network needs charging capacity elsewhere.

West Yorkshire has arranged a staged transition. The five depot sales have completed, while the buses are scheduled to transfer as new franchises begin operating between May 2027 and October 2028. First Bus will continue delivering existing services under temporary depot leases and will manage electrification at Bradford and Huddersfield, with the authority paying for that work. The arrangements allow services and infrastructure development to proceed while ownership and operating responsibilities change.

Securing strategically important depots is a defensible approach. It reduces dependence on an incumbent operator’s control of scarce infrastructure and can help new companies compete for future contracts. The authority also assumes long-term responsibilities for the assets, including maintenance, future electrical capacity and their suitability for a changing passenger network. A depot can be worth purchasing without every potential alternative having been examined, but the consequences of losing those alternatives should be understood before the agreement becomes binding.

Electrification makes this especially important. Charging equipment, substations and electricity connections involve different suppliers, construction schedules and contractual responsibilities from the buses themselves. Vehicle orders can proceed before chargers are fully commissioned when the infrastructure delivery pathway is sufficiently assured. Problems arise when a fleet commitment depends on electrical capacity that cannot be delivered in time, or when the fallback arrangements are inadequate.

The passenger network can also change in ways that alter the original investment case. Better bus priority can reduce the number of vehicles needed to maintain a timetable. New housing developments can shift demand toward different corridors. A major employment centre or hospital relocation can change the value of existing routes and depots. The infrastructure secured today should support those possibilities rather than make sensible network improvements prohibitively expensive.

None of this argues for delaying every investment until uncertainty disappears. Authorities need to secure land, procure vehicles and obtain electrical connections years before some services begin. The useful distinction is between preparation that reduces uncertainty and commitments that substantially increase the cost of changing direction. Both need to be assessed against the passenger services the authority intends to deliver.

West Yorkshire’s programme offers a concrete example of the opportunity and challenge. Securing assets and specifying passenger services across the metropolitan network may produce a more coherent system than leaving every operator to optimize its own routes and depots. The long-term measure of success will be whether the authority can continue improving that network through changing demand, successive contracts and further electrification without having to reconstruct infrastructure it has already paid to provide.

This assessment concludes my five-part series drawn from The Bus Is Not the System, an 83-page TFIE Strategy report examining four decades of British and Irish metropolitan bus governance. The full West Yorkshire Briefing connects the report’s findings to current fleet, depot, electricity infrastructure and franchising decisions.

#5808. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang Cheated On His Marriage With An Alleged Spy

Happy Monday! It's November 9, 2026, and this is The Morning Shift — your daily roundup of the top automotive headlines from around the world, in one place. This is where you'll find the most important stories that are shaping the way Americans drive and get around.

In this morning's edition we're looking at Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's affair, and rough times over at Tesla and SpaceX. We'll also look at dealerships sharing your most sensitive conversations with AI, and Toyota trying to get a break on tariffs.

If you'd like a recap of the latest auto news sent to your inbox each weekday, you can sign up for Jalopnik's free Morning Shift newsletter here.

Jensen Huang, Nvidia CEO and leather jacket enthusiast, cheated on his wife. This is a simple fact, admitted by Huang. The part of the situation that's still up for debate is whether the woman Huang cheated with is a Chinese spy sent to gather intel on Nvidia's precious advanced micro devices. If so, it could spell trouble — not just for Nvidia, not just for autonomous vehicles, but for everything wrapped up in the AI bubble. From Bloomberg:

Nvidia Corp.'s investigation into Chief Executive Officer Jensen Huang's affair with an alleged Chinese spy raises a series of once-unthinkable questions, including whether the man at the center of the AI boom is at risk of losing his job.

Nvidia shares slid about 5% in premarket trading after Huang disclosed the romantic relationship in a memo to employees, a decline that would mean a roughly $225 billion loss in market capitalization. The company's board is assembling a committee of independent directors who will work with outside counsel to investigate the matter.

With the stunning revelation, investors need to consider a range of possibilities that could affect not just Huang and Nvidia, but the entire artificial intelligence industry. Nvidia, the leading maker of AI chips, has used its financial strength to back dozens of major players, from frontier labs like OpenAI to cloud computing players like CoreWeave Inc. Huang has also been among the most forceful advocates for the benefits of AI, from Washington to Wall Street.

If there's one thing that rich guys love more than acting incredibly divorced, it's cheating on their spouses in order to get divorced in the first place. There's a possible trajectory here where Jensen Huang's inability to keep it in his pants crashes the global economy — the AI bubble, its circular financing, and everything invested in it all comes tumbling down.

Tesla and SpaceX have been teasing a merger for a while now, bringing CEO Elon Musk's biggest companies — Tesla, SpaceX, Twitter, xAI — under one cult of personality stock ticker. Shares have been climbing in anticipation of such a merger, but the midterm blue wave likely took that off the table. Now, investors are balking. From Reuters:

Nov 5 (Reuters) – Shares of Elon Musk's companies ‌SpaceX and Tesla slumped on Thursday, extending losses from the previous session, as investors worried that Democratic wins in the US midterm elections could slow data center build-outs.

SpaceX, which has leaned into the ​AI data center boom, is headquartered in Texas alongside Tesla, where Democrats won ​the races for Senate, governor and attorney general on Tuesday. Musk ⁠had spent heavily to back Republicans in the midterms.

The selloff also reflects worries that ​Democratic gains could bring tougher scrutiny of Musk's businesses.

Tesla has recovered some since this Reuters piece — it's up a bit over 5% today as of this writing — but it's still down from pre-midterm highs. SpaceX is on a near-identical trajectory, but slightly higher. Don't worry, Elon still has money.

Are you concerned that AI might not be invading enough of your private life? Well, now you can rest easy with the knowledge that dealerships are bringing it in — not just on the sales floor, but in the F&I box. Some dealers are recording those conversations, and running them through AI in order to upsell you more effectively. From Automotive News:

Until this year, Len Stoler Automotive Group's finance offices generally sold one product per vehicle.

Now, that number is closer to two. The group says employees — coached in part by artificial intelligence — deserve some of the credit.

Len Stoler Automotive uses AI-powered software to record sales interactions with customers. Part training tool and part compliance coach, the technology provides feedback to the group's sales teams to help them highlight the value of a product, rather than push a customer to buy more.

Auto News frames this as a positive, a way for dealers to make more money, while to me it's horrifying. Yeah, why not share all my most private financial information with an AI in real time — an AI with the explicit intent of upselling me on stuff I don't need? Even if it doesn't work, which is likely, that AI now has my financial data to lose in some data breach down the line. Cut it out, dealers.

When Audi promised to build a factory in the U.S., it got a tariff discount from the Trump administration in exchange. Now Toyota, like a middle child feeling left out, is asking if that means it can have extra dessert too. From Automotive News:

When the Trump administration reportedly floated some level of tariff relief to Audi before the election in exchange for a commitment to build an assembly plant in Georgia, no one was more interested than Toyota Motor Corp.

The Japanese automaker paid more than $8 billion in tariffs during its last fiscal year, which ended March 31, dwarfing the bill from every other automaker struggling with the duties President Donald Trump imposed in early 2025.

Now, as Toyota works feverishly to lower the tariff bill, it is asking the Commerce Department whether the preelection offer floated to Audi for its $4 billion plant in Georgia also applies to Toyota because of the $3.6 billion factory it said in July it would build in Texas.

Toyota doesn't have the best bargaining position here — it already agreed to build a Texas factory, without a tariff deal in exchange — but it can't hurt the company to try. The Trump administration is on the back foot now, and it might be more willing to make deals in exchange for good press.

We're nearly back below $4 per gallon, which seemed impossible just months ago. Is this the power of Woke?
