#5807. Senate advances federal energy permitting bill

The Senate is fast tracking legislation that would reduce permitting times for energy projects, but possibly to the detriment of longstanding policies protecting the environment.

The Associated Press reported that the Senate voted 73-25 to advance the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026 (BAAJA). The bill was drafted in response to growing electricity costs from sources like grid-connected hyperscale data centers. By reducing permitting times, energy projects, like solar energy or onshore oil drilling, could address rising energy costs.

However, environmental groups consider BAAJA a reckless response to an immediate need for more power. The bill would amend sections of the Natural Environmental Policy Act, Clean Water Act and Endangered Species Act of 1973 to shorten review periods for new projects, and reduce which projects actually require reviews under these acts.

“We shouldn’t and don’t need to endanger our wildlife just to save a few dollars on gas. We shouldn’t and don’t need to weaken the rules that protect our water from pollution in order to build more clean energy. We shouldn’t facilitate more global-warming air pollution. And we definitely shouldn’t be taking away the public’s right to weigh in on projects that can ruin their environment, make them sick and lessen their quality of life,” said Lisa Frank, executive director of Environment America.

Despite the bill’s threat to environmental measures, the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA) has voiced its support for BAAJA, as have hundreds of companies in the solar and energy storage industry. More than 300 groups co-signed a letter addressed to Congress yesterday imploring the House pass this bill.

“Americans urgently need lower energy costs, a better electric grid and more nergy made in the U.S.A. This bipartisan legislation delivers all three,” said Tim Pawlenty, CEO of SEIA.

SEIA conducted an analysis that claims passing BAAJA could spur 167 GW of new solar energy on the grid between 2028 and 2035, while curbing the country’s CO2 emissions by 20%.

“Updating and expediting transmission and clean energy permitting is essential to delivering lower costs, reduced air, water and climate pollution, protecting communities and expanding the grid for the American people,” said Loren Blackford, executive director of Sierra Club, in a press statement on Sunday. “But rushing legislation that erodes Tribal sovereignty and people’s right to have a say in what is built in their communities, abandons clean water safeguards and wildlife protections, while further enabling a rogue president that is waging a war on clean energy and our public lands is not an acceptable outcome.”

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

“Environmental racism harms all communities and it is important to hold corporations and utilities accountable for expanding poisonous methane gas into our communities and exposing us to environmental harms that exacerbate health, housing, education and economic barriers,” said Tenaya Taylor, Nonprofit Accountability Group.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. #5805. These Are The Worst Road Trips You've Ever Taken

Not every road trip is an adventure that you'll remember for the rest of your life. We wish every day on the road could be sunshine and rainbows, but sometimes your vacation is just nonstop thunderstorms. God forbid you decide January is a good time to visit a national park, but the highway to get there is unplowed and has turned into a stranded-car sanctuary.

We asked our readers last week about the worst road trip they've ever taken. I honestly couldn't conceive how bad things could get when stuck inside a car with other people for hours on end. Commenters submitted some truly absurd anecdotes, ranging from being locked inside a scorching, aluminum-topped pickup bed with his brother as kids to someone's beloved Border Collie defecating in his girlfriend's lap. I love driving, but have road trips been a secret form of torture all along? Without further ado, here are the long-distance drives you'd love to forget:

Right around the turn of the century, my wife and I had to pick up our stepdaughter at Chicago Midway Airport so she could come home after spending Christmas with her bio-dad.

Nothing majorly bad happened to us, but it was a very white-knuckle drive.

A huge blizzard hit in the morning when we were supposed to leave. Bio-dad said her flight still hadn't been canceled, so we set off on our way (early, of course).

What normally would have been a 2-hour trip turned into a 4-hour one. Literally, when we got off the highway at our exit to go to Midway, he calls and says her flight was canceled.

So we turned around and headed back home. That was a 6-hour trip going about 30 MPH the whole way. That was the worst because I was really tired by then. It was dark by that point, so you really couldn't see anything but the snow coming down. My mind kept seeing cars spin out in front of me, when they weren't.

We made it almost all the way home but got stuck in the middle of the road about 3 miles from home. A nice couple was there in a 4X4 when it happened, so they gave us a push and that got us home.

We did get stuck in the driveway, but that was something to deal with the next day.

I never want to drive 10+ hours in a blizzard ever again!

In the days of paper maps, was skiing with my girlfriend in Vermont, and we planned to drive to a town about 10 miles away for a nice dinner (jacket and tie, candlelight, etc.). Driving along, some roads plowed, some not, but no biggie. Start driving up a hill, encouraged by the fact that, although there was snow on the road, there were tracks, and I had 4WD. As the snow started getting deeper, my brain clicked: those are snowmobile tracks. Stopped the car, did a 34-point turn to make sure I stayed on the road and didn't go into a ditch or a gully, and coasted back down the hill. Ended up grabbing pizza right near the place we were staying.

Around 2017, driving my wife's new-to-us BMW with runflats to Blacksburg, VA from CT. Car was CPO and had bout 20K miles. We hit a mega hole on I-78 in NJ near the PA line. Two passenger-side tires blew. Torrential rain, 5 a.m. could not see a thing. Pulled over and found a BMW dealer 20 miles away in PA that would open in a couple of hours. Went there and waited. The service manager was great, he looked the car over and told us that the remaining tires were new and that changing just two was not a problem. Three months later, the front diff fails. $6700 estimate, a small battle with BMW corporate, but once we produced the service doc with the dealer's recommendation of only two tires they fixed the car under the warranty. Thing is, they did a repair, not a replace and sure enough the thing fails again just out of warranty. Traded it in for an Accord. A word of caution: NEVER buy an AWD, especially German, if you don't know the tire replacement history. Essentially, tires need to be replaced in fours unless the others are very fresh. My trusted indy tells me that as little as 9mm can make the difference!!!!

We drove from Charleston, SC to the Outer Banks of NC in August in a 1989 Jeep Wrangler with no AC in the rain. The windshield kept fogging up and the road conditions were awful. We had to make time to meet the ferry schedule and it was sweaty, back pain-filled, bouncy 9 hours...

Amarillo to Dallas for Christmas break in school. My Mazda3 hatch was loaded to the gills with everything we needed to bring home during the break, plus our snowboard gear. A small spot was carved out for our Border Collie to sit. Unbeknownst to me, my girlfriend (now wife) had fed him 6 egg yolks from her egg white omelet that morning and had given him terrible gas. We stopped often to get him to poop, but nothing. Then, in the final 5 miles to her parents' home, he panicked in the backseat. We were on an under-construction part of highway with no exits in sight and no shoulder when he jumped from the back seat into the front seat (he is now properly secured while in the car). I'm trying my best not to crash while looking for an exit while she grabs him and bear hugs him to herself. I then hear her say, "he's sh*tting, he's sh*tting," as she holds him tight and has diarrhea all into her lap. I pull into a Taco Bueno parking lot and she takes him out. I go in and get cleaning supplies and we clean him up, throw away her snuggy-like robe, and put him in a trash bag with his head sticking out for the 10-minute drive to her parents' house. The only casualty was the passenger seat belt, which she accidentally let fully retract and required a full deep clean/disassemble and a 48-hr air out.

Houston to Chicago and back in a brand new BEV. Having spent 15 years in PHEVs, expected refueling to be a minor issue. It was a major issue; chargers were often inaccessible, broken, vandalized, few and far between. Yes, one can spend a tremendous amount of time gaming out a plan for slightly better results, but lotza time and only slightly better results. The stalling out of the plans to make electric cars viable, by the Republicans, cost manufacturers TONS of money and great frustration for me personally.

"Of all the sad words of tongue or pen, the saddest of these: it might have been." (if not for the lingering orange fart in the White House)

May 2019. 7 friends, 4 guys and 3 girls on a 2-week road trip from New Orleans to Yosemite and back. At least that was the plan. Our last hurrah trip before graduating college didn't go right from the start. Imagine 7 adults in a rented Escalade PLUS 2 weeks' worth of gear! We overpacked from the start. We had to leave all our tents behind except for one, which was my friend's dad's old Boy Scout tent that smelled like toe cheese and had old wax coating peeling off. We left late because of the packing, so we were constantly behind schedule as a result. We also overplanned things to do so this meant we'd see something for 5 minutes and then have to keep driving so we wouldn't miss something else! Texas went on forever. The big crater in Arizona? Didn't get to see it, had to drive right by. The Grand Canyon? We got there late in the day instead of the morning, so instead of hiking and seeing it we got to look at it for 30 minutes, 15 of which were obscured by clouds. Then we got sleeted on and had to leave. Vegas? Giant waste of time where we all secretly confessed to looking at flights home and tried to salvage the trip... and friendships. Drove through Death Valley in the middle of the night. Tensions were high. Some people were controlling. There were tons of fights and crying. It was too cold to camp so we diverted to San Diego for a few days which saved us. Then Santa Fe. Then 20 hours through storms in Texas and then home. Too many people and too much time. Never again.

The past two summers, I've taken my 1979 El Camino from northern Wisconsin to be a part of Elcofest in Indiana the final weekend in July with no A/C. Both times it was 95ºF, muggy, and rainy, so we had to keep the windows up. For me, those were among the best road trips I've ever taken. But if you ask the wife, she'd probably say they were among the worst.

In the late '70s, I drove from Saint Paul up to northern Minnesota in -10 degrees to go cross-country skiing. Unfortunately, the heat in my rusted-out VW Squareback was not up for the trip. My passenger rode in a down sleeping bag. We had to constantly scrape the ice off the inside of the windows and windshield. My passenger refused to ride back with me, instead opting for the bus from Bemidji.

Ah, Yes. The old air-cooled VW winter experience, all bundled up with one gloved hand on the wheel, and the other with an ice scraper!

Indiana to John Pennecamp State Park in the Florida Keys. In summer. My brother and I rode in the bed of my dad's truck (a single-cab F-100) under a beercan-thin aluminum topper for ALL the miles. Occasionally one of us would be allowed to go through the rear sliding window and sit in the cab with mom and dad. We could open the windows to the topper, but then it got REALLY loud, so we had to use that sparingly. We mostly just baked.

Bonus points: we were sitting on a plywood platform covered in that 1970s green outdoor grass carpet, and under the platform were several scuba tanks which we had been told were full and super dangerous and could explode. Because 1970s parents thought that sort of thing was hilarious.

It was hot, loud, uncomfortable and we spent the whole time going down afraid for our lives. It was years later that our parents explained that the tanks were empty; they just wanted us not to move around too much.

#5804. Trial Finds Tangible Results From Yoga as Cardiac Rehab

— Indian heart failure trial shows better quality of life, but not hard clinical outcomes per se

Yoga-based cardiac rehabilitation did not change cardiac outcomes for people with heart failure and reduced ejection fraction (HFrEF), though there were big boosts in exercise capacity and quality of life in an Indian randomized study.

In the Yoga-CaRe HF trial of nearly 3,500 patients, there was technically no significant difference in the risk of 15-month cardiac events (death, repeat hospitalization, or stroke) whether people underwent yoga-based cardiac rehab or were assigned enhanced standard care (21% vs 22.8%; adjusted HR 0.90, 95% CI 0.79-1.05), according to Dorairaj Prabhakaran, MD, MSc, of the Centre for Chronic Disease Control in New Delhi.

On the upside, per-protocol analyses suggested reductions in the first cardiac event among the most active yoga practitioners, namely those HFrEF patients practicing yoga 6 or 7 days a week (HR 0.72, 95% CI 0.56-0.91) or more than 255 minutes per week (HR 0.73, 95% CI 0.57-0.92), Prabhakaran told the audience at the American Heart Association (AHA) annual meeting in Chicago.

Moreover, the yoga program led to improved quality of life given "a huge difference" of 8.1 points (P<0.001) in Minnesota Living with Heart Failure Questionnaire scores at 3 months, along with better exercise capacity (improvement of 25.5 m for 6-minute walking distance [6MWD], P<0.001) and enhanced health status (improvement of 0.05 points on the European quality of life questionnaire quality-adjusted utility value, P<0.001), Prabhakaran said.

Among the other secondary findings of the trial were improved frailty and smoking cessation in the yoga arm.

All in all, Prabhakaran called the yoga program a "scalable" model for cardiac rehabilitation in India that has additional "potential to be adopted in other settings with necessary validation."

In heart failure, exercise-based cardiac rehabilitation is established for reducing the risk of all‐cause hospital admissions and heart failure‐related hospital admissions and possibly improving quality of life. These programs are underutilized, however, as they may be time-consuming and financially costly.

Hence, there is much interest in expanding the different modalities of cardiac rehab to make it more accessible and palatable options for each patient.

The yoga arm in Yoga-CaRe HF was provided a multi-prong intervention including patient education, goal-setting for lifestyle improvement, and yoga practice. Yoga sessions were supervised by a board-certified yoga practitioner over 3 months, initially in-person then virtually, with one follow-up session every 3 months until the end of the study. Participants were expected to perform home practice daily.

"The most conservative approach to evaluating ... this is that it was a neutral trial from a statistical perspective. But there does seem to be some impact of doing yoga for a high-risk population," commented Justin Ezekowitz, MBBCh, MSc, of University of Alberta in Edmonton, Canada, during a press conference.

"What should we do with our patients who want to attend and consider other options for cardiac rehabilitation? Should we consider yoga as one of those avenues? Based on this trial, if the goals are quality of life and for better functional status, the answer should be 'yes,'" he said.

"I think these are important patient-centered endpoints that are still meritorious," agreed session moderator Amit Khera, MD, of UT Southwestern in Dallas. "Yoga has been practiced around the world by millions of people, so to be able to assess it in this way in a very rigorous fashion was incredibly important."

Ezekowitz highlighted the improvement in 6MWD in particular. "For people that want to walk from their front door out to their mailbox, or front door out to the local store, a 25-meter gain for patients with HFrEF is a substantial gain," he said, on par with expectations for "medications in pulmonary hypertension, for example, so this is an important marker."

Yoga-CaRe HF was an open-label randomized trial conducted at 16 tertiary care hospitals in India. Participants were adult patients with HFrEF, with New York Heart Association (NYHA) class II or III symptoms, who were recruited in hospital and outpatient settings.

Participants were randomized to yoga rehab (n=1,737) or enhanced standard care (n=1,755). In contrast to the yoga intervention, the controls got standard care consisting of an educational booklet and guideline-directed medical therapy (GDMT).

The cohort had a mean age of 57, with women accounting for 27% of the group. Roughly half were in NYHA functional class III, and the heart failure was of an ischemic etiology in 65% of cases. Medications at baseline largely included beta-blockers (approximately 84%), renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system inhibitors (77%), mineralocorticoid receptor antagonists (70%), and SGLT-2 inhibitors (60%). Patients were on quadruple GDMT in 38%, and triple GDMT in about 50%, at baseline.

Over the 15-month follow-up, individual clinical endpoints (e.g., all-cause death, repeat hospitalization, cardiac death, heart failure hospitalization) tended to numerically favor the yoga intervention group without reaching statistical significance.

Patient improvement in 6MWD and health status persisted to 24 months.

Additionally, Prabhakaran reported that the yoga intervention was safe, with no adverse events logged during yoga practice. Composite safety events, including all deaths and emergency/unplanned hospitalizations, reached 25.6% in the Yoga-CaRe group and 29.6% in the enhanced standard care group.

As for the caregivers involved in the trial, their own quality of life and depression were ostensibly improved with the yoga intervention.

#5802. Sierra Club Welcomes CalPERS’ $71 Billion Climate-Solutions Investment Milestone, Urges Fund to End Financing of Fossil Fuel Expansion

SACRAMENTO, CA — On Monday, the California Public Employees’ Retirement System (CalPERS) reported that the value of investments it classifies as climate solutions reached $71 billion as of June 30, 2026, up from $59.7 billion a year earlier. The increase brings the nation’s largest public pension fund closer to its goal of $100 billion in climate investments by 2030.

Sierra Club welcomed CalPERS’ continued commitment to climate-solutions investing and called on the fund to adopt stronger standards and transparency around what counts toward its $100 billion goal. Sierra Club also urged CalPERS to prioritize investments that support real-world emissions reductions and address climate risks across its broader portfolio to protect the retirement security of the workers and retirees it serves.

In response, Ben Cushing, Director of Sierra Club’s Sustainable Finance Campaign, released the following statement:

“It’s encouraging to see CalPERS make progress toward its climate-solutions goal, but a growing investment total doesn’t tell us how much new money is helping build clean energy and cut emissions. Reducing climate risk also means looking at where CalPERS invests across the whole portfolio. CalPERS has already taken the important step of using its votes to hold company directors accountable for how they handle climate risk. It can build on that work by adopting a policy to stop financing fossil fuel expansion and backing climate projects that need new investment to get built or expand.”

CalPERS said the $11.3 billion increase came from both new investments and gains on assets it already held. CalPERS also reported that financed emissions across the asset classes covered by its analysis rose by approximately 6 percent over the past year, largely reflecting investments in higher-emitting assets that it expects to decarbonize over time. For its global public equity and U.S. corporate credit holdings, CalPERS reported an implied temperature rise of 2.9°C, up from 2.7°C a year earlier.

Last year, Sierra Club and the California Common Good coalition urged CalPERS to publish a full list of the investments it counts toward its $100 billion goal, adopt science-based standards for what qualifies as a climate solution, and exclude fossil fuel investments from that total. Sierra Club has raised these concerns at CalPERS board meetings, and elaborated on them in a December 2025 CalMatters op-ed questioning the credibility and real-world impact of CalPERS’ reported climate investments.

Sierra Club’s 2025 Principles for Climate Solutions Investments outline standards for credible investing, including prioritizing real-world emissions reductions and establishing safeguards for transition financing. Its January 2026 Climate Solutions Gap report likewise highlighted CalPERS as an example of why ambitious climate-solutions targets must be backed by credible definitions, clear eligibility criteria, and transparent reporting.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5800. FBI Investigates Doc's Death; Measles Cases Top 5,000; Can Flu Shot Fight Cancer?

— Health news and commentary gathered by MedPage Today staff

Note that some links may require registration or subscription.

The FBI is aiding in an investigation over the death of Heather Kovich, MD, a prominent family medicine physician in New Mexico who died last month on the Navajo Nation. (Santa Fe New Mexican)

Sen. Chris Murphy (D-Conn.) said he was discharged from the hospital after suffering a stroke and will miss "several weeks" of votes in the Senate as he recovers in a rehabilitation facility. (AP)

Experts said the Trump administration's new global health partnerships could undermine pandemic preparedness. (New York Times)

#5799. New BYD Seal 07 Illustrates Chinese EV Evolution

Last week, BYD introduced the next generation Seal 07 EV. This model replaces the original BYD Seal, which entered the market in late 2022 and put the brand on the map in the minds of many people around the world. While the new vehicle is important to BYD, it also illustrates many of the changes that have happened to the EVs and the EV market in China over the past few years.

The new Seal 07 grows to 5080 mm long by 1960 mm wide by 1495 mm tall. That compares to 4,800 mm long, 1,875 mm wide and 1,460 mm high for the previous Seal. That also makes it slightly larger than the late Tesla Model S.

Like many new BYD cars, the interior has become more premium and technologically advanced.  Interiors offer front zero gravity seats with heating, ventilation and massage. An onboard refrigerator is offered in the center console and a power sunshade in the roof. Speakers have proliferated. The screens have improved,  programmable buttons can be added, and voice interaction has improved. Basic versions come with BYD’s God’s Eye C with BYD covering autonomous parking, while higher trims offer LiDAR-based God’s Eye B with BYD adding coverage for city navigation.

Seal 07 models now come with DiSus-C adaptive damping on lower trim models and DiSus-M magnetorheological active dampers on higher trims. Suspension is double wishbones up front, multilink at the rear.

Of course, the new car offers BYD’s flash charging, capable of charging from 10-70% in 5 minutes and 10-97% in 9 minutes, while taking 12 minutes to go from 10-97% at -30 C with no preconditioning. 20,000 BYD flash charging stations are planned to be in operation by the end of the year, which will be more than all DC charging stations in the US combined. Meanwhile, the previous Seal took 26 minutes to go from 30-80%.

Looking at the closest version of the original Seal from 2022, battery capacity has dropped from 82.5 kW to 76.4 kW for the new model. Meanwhile, range increased to 775 km CLTC from 700 km in the old model due to greater efficiency. While efficiency may have once been a criticism of the Seal, it is now a highlight. Power is up from 230 kW to 245 kW (329 hp). Meanwhile, a curb weight of 1975 kg is lighter than the previous 700 km model (2055 kg), with increased use of UHSS in the structure.

Overall, it is a larger and more luxurious car than the vehicle it replaces, while losing a little weight. Performance, charging and efficiency have improved. Equipment levels are higher. Technology is more advanced. And then we get to affordability.

In 2022, the original Seal offered a 550 km range trim for 209,800 RMB ($31,424 USD at current exchange rates), but the 700 km range version, which is closest to the new model, started at 259,800 RMB ($38,913).

A little over 4 years later, the new Seal 07 EV starts at 149,900 RMB ($22,437 USD) with 775 km of range, more power and much faster charging. It costs 10,000 RMB ($1498) more for a version with God’s Eye C intelligent driving. For the top-of-the-line flagship with LiDAR-based God’s Eye B, the price is 184,900 RMB ($27,694 USD). In addition, there are launch discounts and perks. Of note, there is no AWD version of the new Seal 07, with that role filled by other BYD models in China.

For the default comparison, the standard RWD Model 3 is smaller (4720 mm long) slower, comes with less equipment and only 634 km of CLTC range (612 km with optional 19” wheels). Price starts at 225,500 RMB, although paint colors and interior options increase the price (included with the Seal). To get Enhanced Driver Assistance, which is roughly equivalent to God’s Eye C, you need to add on 32,000 RMB, while Tesla Driver Assist (similar to God’s Eye B) adds on 64,000 RMB ($9,586 USD). To get as close to a top spec Seal 07 in a standard Model 3, would take you to 307,500 ($46,057), but you would still be under-equipped with less range in a smaller, less powerful vehicle. The Long Range RWD would take you to a bit above the Seal 07 with 800 km of range on 19” wheels, even though that ternary  battery is recommended not to charge to 100% on a regular basis, unlike the LFP-based second generation Blade Battery in the BYD. The Long Range RWD Model 3 costs 331,500 RMB ($49,652 USD) with paint, 19” wheels and Driver Assist, but still lacks in equipment.

However, the Seal 06 (4870 mm) is a better match for the standard Model 3 but comes in at 119,900 to 159,900 RMB ($17,958 to $23,950 USD). There is also a no-compromise, larger (5150 mm) Seal 08 with more available range, power and amenities from 196,900 to 239,900 RMB ($29,492-$35,932 USD). And if you want something sportier, there is now the Fang Cheng Bao Formula S (5060 mm) at 189,900 to 229,900 RMB ($28,443-$34,434 USD). For something more luxurious, there is the Denza Z9S at 255,800 to 325,800 RMB ($38,313-$48,798 USD). None of those BYD models existed when the original Seal was launched. In addition, there are also Dynasty Han and Qin sedans from BYD for a little different flavor. And those are just the BEV models. There are also PHEV versions.

Looking beyond Tesla and BYD, the Seal 07 fits in the price gap between the Mona M03 and Next P7 at XPENG. It slots below the SU7 from Xiaomi. Below a Zeekr 7X or Nio ET5. At its current price, size and capability, the Seal 07 is in a good competitive niche between entry level sedans and more premium vehicles. If the Seal 07 had kept the original Seal’s higher price point, the competition would be much more challenging.

Compared to 2022, the new Seal 07 does not need to cover as many bases. This lets it better meet the needs of specific customers, while related models can be tailored to different customer needs. Undoubtedly, these different models will divide sales numbers, which may not be good for bragging rights. Analysis will become more challenging and may require combining sales of related models for relevant comparisons. But the increased choice is better for customers.

It may be a while before we have a clear picture of what impact this all has on sales. Chinese regulations that began earlier this year prevent manufacturers or dealers from selling below cost. This essentially prohibits the model-year-end clearance sales that are typical throughout much of the West. In addition, Chinese regulations stopped the practice of registering a vehicle and then exporting it as a zero-mile used car. In combination, it becomes much more difficult to manage inventory. As such, many models that are about to be replaced have their deliveries cut well in advance of the new model launch and have sales essentially evaporate. Then, it can take a while for the new models to ramp up production and deliveries to take hold. We may not have a good picture of BYD’s sedan sales in China until next year.

It also may be a while before we have global distribution plans or pricing for the new models. The three new Seal models are not yet offered outside of China. BYD has taken the approach of waiting to bring their latest models to export markets, often introducing models overseas as new that have already been discontinued in China. When they do offer current models, they tend to be at a significant premium (see Denza Z9GT). Meanwhile, companies like XPENG are introducing their latest models globally.

However, with intensifying competition from Chinese automakers in some export markets, BYD’s approach may change. Some flash charging cars are starting to reach export markets. The Seagull (ATTO 1) has recently been selling in Australia for $19,990 AUD ($13,752 USD), representing a significant reduction in export markup. We might see more advanced models at lower markups in markets where protectionism has not taken hold.

For comparison to the US, the least expensive car of any powertrain is the gasoline Kia K4 at $23,535, which is more than the base Seal 07 ($22,437). The least expensive EV is the soon-to-be-discontinued Chevy Bolt at $28,995, which is more than the most expensive Seal 07 ($27,694). Meanwhile, the least expensive EV sedan in the US is once again the Tesla Model 3 at $38,630 (including destination and fees, which are included in Chinese prices). A number of other EV models were canceled.

In China, EV choices have expanded, cars have become more capable, and prices have come down over the past four years. Meanwhile, in the US, cars remain unaffordable and EV choices have diminished with aging products.

Of note, the original BYD Seal had already attained regulatory approval from Transport Canada for import prior to Biden and Trudeau implementing prohibitive 100%+ tariffs on Chinese cars. Due to regulatory harmonization between the two countries, that meant it would have also been approved to enter the US. The US EV market could have looked dramatically different now, even with the rollback of consumer subsidies. With free trade, we could be looking at an EV growth in market share, expansion of options and improvement in affordability similar to China, even if not to the same extent.

Overall, the new Seal 07 is a strong entrant into a competitive segment in a very competitive market. It might be a challenge to choose it over one of the many other excellent options from BYD and its competitors in China. However, having too many excellent, affordable EV options to choose from is a good challenge for customers to have.

Larry lives in Queens, NY, with his wife and cats. While he has spent much of his professional career leading global marketing efforts, his passions focus on clean technology and the automotive industry. He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5798. 5 Years In, Mustang Mach-E Battery Data Shows What Real-World Electric Vehicle Durability Looks Like

Five years ago, the first Mustang Mach-E customers began adding something important to the Ford electric vehicle battery validation work: real-world ownership at scale.

As part of our quality process, design and validation engineering starts long before a vehicle reaches a customer. We model battery performance, run physical tests, expose packs to heat, cold, and charging profiles, and build in margin for demanding real-world conditions.

These rigorous quality tests are designed to help us understand how battery systems perform across a wide range of climates, charging behaviors, and driving conditions before customers get behind the wheel.

Then David Blenkle drove past 400,000 miles on the original battery in his 2022 Mustang Mach-E. He gave us a real-world example of what is possible — and begged the question of whether he’s an outlier or not.

His battery health has remained strong through unusually high-mileage use. His experience gave our team a chance to compare one exceptional ownership case with what we are seeing across the broader fleet.

The bigger question was if David’s experience reflected a broader pattern. So, we decided to look back at the Mustang Mach-E owners who started it all: The Class of 2021.

Over five years ago, the first Mustang Mach-E vehicles — model year 2021 — reached customers. Those electric SUVs have been on the road, in customers’ hands, ever since.

We analyzed the battery state-of-health data for the entire fleet.

Here is what the Class of 2021 looks like at the five-year mark:

More than 36,000 vehicles. Two billion all-electric miles, across all 50 states and across Canada.

Our Class of 2021 has almost reached Neptune — not a practical comparison, but a useful way to understand the scale and distance.

The number I look at closely is the spread. Ninety-five percent of those vehicles fall between 87% and 97% health. Averages can hide variation, so that range matters.

In some cases, David has driven 300,000 miles more than many in our 2021 cohort. Then there are also the people who are road-warriors, like David.

More than 2,000 Class of 2021 owners have crossed 100,000 miles on their original battery, averaging better than 91% battery health. A smaller group of high-mileage drivers — more than 30 drivers past 200,000 miles — is averaging 88%.1

David’s mileage remains exceptional, but the broader Class of 2021 data shows he is part of a larger pattern of strong battery health across real-world use.

Long-term battery health remains one of the biggest questions for many people considering an electric vehicle, and real-world data like this helps make that conversation more concrete.

Two billion customer-driven miles is meaningful because it reflects how people use their vehicles — commuting, road-tripping, and running their everyday lives.

And it’s not just the 2021 Mustang Mach-E.

Aggregated across the full battery-electric lineup released over the last five years in the U.S. and Canada, the pattern holds: our packs are designed to last at least 10 years, and in the wild they’re maintaining, on average, greater than 90% health at 100,000 miles.2

The Class of 2021 is the cohort old enough, right now, to tell the most detailed version of that story.

While this story focuses on the 2021 Mustang Mach-E, the learning doesn’t stop there.

Ford uses field data from vehicles on the road to inform current and future electric vehicle programs such as the Universal Electric Vehicle platform and Fathom. This includes how we think about battery management, charging guidance, and long-term ownership confidence.

Battery chemistry is complicated, but a few simple everyday habits can help support long-term battery health:

Owners should not have to become battery chemistry experts to take good care of their electric vehicles.

The 2021 Mustang Mach-E vehicles in this data set use NCM battery chemistry, where frequent full charges are generally not needed for daily use. Some newer Ford EV batteries use LFP chemistry, which benefits from an occasional charge to 100% to help the battery management system stay calibrated.

Ford over time has implemented new features, like guidance in the Ford App, to help owners look after their batteries, specific to the vehicle and battery system they drive.

I have spent my career on the battery side of this business — cell chemistry, pack design, thermal management, and the systems that help a battery perform over time.

For an owner, the battery is central to the EV experience. It shapes confidence in every commute, road trip, and charging stop.

This is one of the most valuable feedback loops we have had at this scale: more than 36,000 vehicles, five-plus years, and billions of real-world miles.

The data is encouraging, and the learnings continue to inform how Ford approaches battery systems, software guidance, and future electric vehicle programs.

The Class of 2021 has added a real-world chapter to what our validation work was designed to understand. And we have many more electric miles ahead of us.

By Kevin Vander Laan, the Technical Leader of High-Voltage Battery Cells and Systems at Ford.

1. Battery state-of-health data is as of August 2026 for all 21MY Mustang Mach-E vehicles sold to U.S. and Canada retail and fleet customers, covering approximately 36,000 owners with an average ownership period of 5.2 years. Figures reflect usable battery capacity.

2. Based on a 2026 manufacturer field data study of high-voltage battery state of health on Ford battery electric vehicles.

#5797. Nissan Brings The Patrol Name Back To The U.S. After Nearly 60 Years ... Kind Of

In a move that's either pure nostalgia-bait or an appeal to hardcore off-road nerds, Nissan is bringing the Patrol nameplate back to the U.S. for the first time since 1969. No, Nissan's Armada isn't getting a name change. Don't freak out, Armada faithful. Instead, as a celebration for the Patrol's 75th anniversary, Nissan is bringing the name back as a trim level on its full-size SUV brute.

Called the Armada Patrol Pro-4X, the body-on-frame SUV adds all sorts of neat off-road extras while also not being terribly different from the normal Pro-4X it replaces, save for new Patrol badging on the tailgate, center console, door sills and floor mats. That's what it's all about, baby. There are also a handful of optional extras, like some very tough-looking Nismo-branded rock sliders and a roof rack with an incorporated light bar, that are new for 2027. They can be had along with Nismo's existing 18-inch Axis wheels, which are wrapped in  BF Goodrich All-Terrain T/A K03 rubber.

Under the hood, you'll find the same twin-turbocharged 3.5-liter V6 as the cool normal Armada, pumping out 425 horsepower and 516 pound-feet of torque. It's paired to a nine-speed automatic transmission that sends power to all four wheels courtesy of a full-time 4WD system and a locking rear differential. To help keep you out of trouble when you're off-road, the Patrol Pro-4X comes with height-adjustable air suspension, underbody armor, and tow hooks.

If you're the type of person who is a real nasty Nissan nerd, or you just love off-roading, perhaps the 2027 Armada Patrol Pro-4X is for you. If that's the case, you've just got to be ready to fork over $81,265, including $2,275 destination. That's no small chunk of change, but it actually undercuts this car's closest rival, the Toyota Sequoia TRD Pro, by $6,855.

I won't lie to ya, I don't completely understand the decision here for Nissan. I mean, Patrol is hardly an established nameplate in the U.S. It only existed here from 1962 to 1969, and it's been gone for a hell of a long time, so I can't imagine there's a ton of nostalgia behind it. I suppose you could point to an appeal to off-roading nerds as a reason for the name addition or perhaps Nissan is getting ready to switch the Armada's name over to Patrol in the U.S., though I sort of doubt that.

Patrol has been a part of Nissan since 1951, so there's obviously a lot of brand heritage behind it. I'm just not sure there's a lot of brand heritage behind it in the U.S. Better late than never, though, and Patrol Pro-4X does sound a bit cooler than just Pro-4X, so who am I to complain?

#5796. FDA Slaps New Safety Warning on GLP-1 Drugs

— Required labeling will warn of potential risk of NAION, a potentially blinding eye condition

The FDA is requiring manufacturers of all GLP-1 drugs to update their prescribing information to include a warning about the potential risk of non-arteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy (NAION), a rare but potentially blinding eye condition.

Cases of NAION have been documented across all currently marketed GLP-1 products, according to the agency.

Healthcare providers should "instruct patients to immediately report sudden onset of acute, painless, decreased vision; visual field deficits; or other visual disturbances in one eye," the FDA stated in a safety communication on Monday. "If NAION is suspected, promptly refer the patient to an ophthalmologist for evaluation. Consider discontinuing the GLP-1 [receptor agonist] in patients who develop NAION, unless the benefits of treatment clearly outweigh the risk."

NAION occurs when blood flow to the optic disc is reduced. Beyond the painless vision loss -- often noticed upon waking -- symptoms can include blurred or missing visual fields, color distortion, and reduced central visual acuity. Its exact triggering mechanism remains poorly understood and animal data on GLP-1 drugs did not further elucidate a potential mechanism for the higher risk observed with the drugs, the FDA said.

The agency's action follows a wave of studies suggesting a potential link between GLP-1 drugs and NAION, including a 2024 study in JAMA Ophthalmology involving semaglutide (Ozempic, Wegovy) that FDA said prompted its review. Europe's drug regulator warned about the risk 17 months ago.

In a meta-analysis of randomized trials performed by the FDA, the agency identified 15 probable NAION events among more than 134,000 patients. Of those, 10 cases occurred among the roughly 78,000 treated with GLP-1 drugs, none were in those randomized to other glucose-lowering medications, and five occurred in placebo recipients. Incidence was 6.4 cases per 100,000 person-years among the GLP-1 drug recipients compared with 3.8 cases per 100,000 person-years among patients receiving a non-GLP-1 medication or placebo.

A retrospective cohort study performed by the FDA involving type 2 diabetes patients showed no significantly increased risk for NAION with GLP-1 agonists versus SGLT2 inhibitors (HR 1.19, 95% CI 0.91-1.54) but an increased risk was seen compared with DPP-4 inhibitors (HR 1.43, 95% CI 1.02-2.01).

Common risk factors for NAION include smoking, diabetes, hypertension, obstructive sleep apnea, anemia, and chronic kidney disease. Patients with a history of NAION carry an increased risk for recurrence.

Patients and clinicians can report suspected adverse reactions associated with GLP-1 medications to the FDA's MedWatch reporting program.

#5795. More than 300 Solar & Storage Companies Urge Congress to Pass Bipartisan Permitting Reform to Accelerate Clean Energy Deployment, Lower Costs

WASHINGTON D.C. — More than 300 solar and storage companies are urging Congress to pass the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAAJA), S. 5653, warning that outdated federal permitting processes and grid infrastructure bottlenecks are delaying critical energy projects and driving up electricity costs.

In a letter to congressional leaders, the companies call on lawmakers to quickly send the bipartisan legislation to the President’s desk. The signatories note that solar and storage account for 70% of new electricity capacity added to the grid thus far in 2026, while $120 billion in clean energy investment remains caught in permitting delays.

“You can’t lower energy costs if you can’t build the energy projects, and these companies know that this bipartisan legislation will help get America building again,” said Tim Pawlenty, CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA).

BAAJA would modernize the federal permitting system by ensuring fair treatment for all technologies, protecting lawfully issued permits from politically motivated delays, establishing predictable timelines for federal reviews and judicial proceedings, modernizing transmission planning, and expanding distributed energy resources’ access to wholesale electricity markets.

New SEIA analysis shows that BAAJA could unlock 167 gigawatts of additional solar capacity between 2028 and 2035, bringing solar to over 18% of U.S. electricity generation by 2035. The analysis finds that the bill’s reforms could reduce cumulative power sector CO2 emissions by approximately 20% and avoid $54 billion in public health damages.

The message from more than 300 companies comes as voters made clear in the midterm elections that lowering energy costs is a top concern. Permitting reform is urgently needed to meet rising demand and get more reliable, affordable solar and storage projects built.

About SEIA®: The Solar Energy Industries Association® (SEIA) is leading the transformation to a clean energy economy. SEIA works with its 1,200 member companies and other strategic partners to fight for policies that create jobs in every community and shape fair market rules that promote competition and the growth of reliable, low-cost solar power. Founded in 1974, SEIA is the national trade association for the solar and solar + storage industries, building a comprehensive vision for the Solar+ Decade through research, education and advocacy. Visit SEIA online at www.seia.org and follow @SEIA on Twitter, LinkedIn, and Instagram.

#5794. I've Solved The 72-Hour EV Test Drive Conundrum, And Every Automaker Is Free To Steal My Idea (Except Tesla)

Because Republicans have fought so hard to spread pro-oil propaganda about electric vehicles, the general public largely has no idea whether an EV would work for them or not. Batteries easily last well over a decade and are recyclable, fast chargers are actually pretty common, and lifecycle emissions are way lower for EVs even if you account for mining, but somehow, objectively true statements are controversial now. New EV sales in the U.S. are also struggling, and automakers are struggling to find buyers. The good news is, I have an actual, viable solution that automakers are free steal borrow.

OK, so we've seen that spending time living with an EV, even temporarily, goes a long way to debunking pro-oil propaganda and can change people's minds about whether or not an EV is right for them. Personally, I think the easiest way to make that happen is for owners who want to see EVs get more popular to lend their cars to as many people as they can, for at least 3 days at a time. Ideally, automakers would offer 120-hour test drives to anyone who wants one, but that would be wildly expensive, so I understand why it's hard to even get a 72-hour loan.

Automakers can't sit back here, though. And I'm not just saying that because EVs are the future (they really are), or because I want my nieces and nephews to grow up breathing clean air (I really do). No, it's because they're already shipping EVs to the U.S., and selling more of them would help both sales and profits, while keeping excess inventory from piling up on dealer lots. So here's the idea I'm offering every automaker, free and clear: introduce incentives that encourage dealers to use EVs as service loaners.

When an auto journalist gives business advice, usually, the best thing any automaker can do is the opposite. And that goes double when that advice is on Jalopnik, a site that still loves a good s—post. I'm also well aware I'm not a serious journalist. But tell me the service loaner isn't the most cost-effective way to get customers into an EV long enough for them to realize how great it is to have a gas station in your garage.

I know we all love conquest sales, but current owners are your most likely future customers. The real key to a future EV sale, though, is the low-pressure, no-commitment, just-try-it-for-fun nature of the service loaner. Even people who actively hate EVs would probably be willing to play with one for three or four days while their car's in the shop if the loaner was free. Customers are less likely to think it's some sort of sales trick, too, if the offer comes from a service manager instead of a salesperson.

Considering we're talking about people who already own a car, I don't know if we'd see large numbers of owners picking up their cars, handing over the keys to the loaner EV, and demanding to trade their car in. But as long as you make a good car, there are no downsides to making a positive impression on your customers. And I bet that program would end up being the most cost-effective sales tool in any automaker's arsenal. Not because they're forcing people to go electric but because EVs do such a great job of selling themselves when they fit a prospective owner's lifestyle.

Not everyone can justify an EV. It's pricier and less convenient if you can't charge at home, and some people do crazy amounts of driving or frequent towing. But if you work for an automaker, you know your EVs make better daily drivers for regular homeowners than your most popular daily drivers, and you know they'd sell better if you could get more butts in seats. Now you have a way to get your most likely customers' butts in those seats for far less than the cost of launching a 120-hour test drive program.

Even better, the anti-EV crowd should be all over this one. If EVs are still as bad as they're convinced they are, then a few days driving an EV should be all anyone needs to disabuse them of the notion that they should go electric. Give the skeptics at your company the opportunity to rack up a win and prove this whole EV thing is a waste of everyone's time and money. I mean, they aren't afraid people will like EVs better if they just spend some time with one, are they? Let's put our money where our mouth is and find out.

#5793. Flock Cameras Can Still Record License Plates Using Infrared, Your Trash Bags And Canceled Contracts Be Damned

After a Missouri town voted unanimously to cancel its contract with Flock in October, the town, like many others who have recently cancelled contracts, went ahead and covered its flock cameras in black trash bags. Not two weeks later, the town's police department found not only were the cameras still on, but they continued to record vehicles, using infrared technology for the dark trash-bag induced conditions.

Excelsior Springs Police Department took to Facebook on November 5 to inform its residents of the issue. The department's Lieutenant Ryan Dowdy told Local 12 that the city initially placed bags over the cameras when they voted to end its contract with Flock and remove the cameras. However, not only are the cameras still on, the black garbage bags do not seem to block Flock's automated license plate reader's infrared technology. The company touts that tech on its own website as "nighttime performance that doesn't quit."

Also problematic is that with a cancelled contract the police department cannot access or use the data but other law enforcement agencies might be able to.

The 15 cameras installed in the town have recorded some vehicles, Dowdy said that most of these images have been headlights and taillights, with a few license plates. The screenshot image shared to Excelsior Springs Police Department's Facebook page showed a few of the vehicles captured through the garbage bags with the infrared. The third of the semi-hazy images showed a red box over the license plate it captured.

About a month ago, the police department held an informative session with the people of Excelsior Springs to explain Flock's ALPR technology to citizens. Community concerns still prevailed in taking down the cameras, as the council voted unanimously a couple of weeks later to cancel the contract. Although the police department said not using the 15 cameras posted in town will affect its police work, Dowdy said it continues efforts to be honest with area residents.

"You know, we'd rather tell people, be upfront about it," Dowdy said. "We're not trying to hide anything. To be honest, we haven't looked at it until yesterday to even find that it was occurring."

The city's solution for now is to double-bag the cameras until they are removed, hopefully some time around the contract's end at the end of this month. This latest folly is another warning to towns, cities and communities that have gone about covering their cameras with trash bags to double up. Who knows what kind of trash can seep through just the one layer.

#5792. Was Superglue to Blame for His Lung Nodule?

— Benign nodule discovered during CT scan for abdominal pain

A CT scan turned up an incidental nodule in the lung of a patient being evaluated for kidney stones, and clinicians' best guess is that it had something to do with superglue.

The patient, a man in his 40s, presented to an emergency department in Belgium with severe abdominal pain, and a CT scan revealed kidney stones, Pauline Vermoesen, MD, of Ghent University in Belgium, wrote in BMJ Case Reports.

However, there was also an incidental finding of an air-filled pulmonary nodule, about 16 mm in diameter, they wrote. They followed the patient via CT scan for about 9 years.

The nodule turned out to be a pneumatocele, a gas-filled, thin-walled cavity more commonly seen in children, and typically found in the context of infection, trauma, or hydrocarbon aspiration. In this case, the patient did report that a few weeks before turning up at the ED with abdominal pain, he had "accidentally inhaled superglue."

"In the absence of infection or trauma, glue aspiration is considered the most likely cause, although without histopathological confirmation a definitive causal relationship cannot be established with certainty," Vermoesen and colleagues wrote.

The patient was a non-smoker, with no history of a respiratory infection or thoracic trauma. The nodule hadn't been there on a CT scan done 7 years prior to the kidney stone finding, the authors noted.

Positron emission tomography-CT scanning showed minimal fluorodeoxyglucose uptake, suggesting it was a benign lesion. Serial CT imaging over a total of 9 years showed, "progression from a solid nodule to a thin-walled cystic lesion consistent with a pneumatocele," they wrote.

The pathophysiology of pneumatocele likely involves parenchymal necrosis and a check-valve mechanism, Vermoesen and colleagues wrote, whereby partial bronchiolar obstruction leads to air trapping and distal cystic dilatation.

This rare case in an adult demonstrates the benign and uncomplicated evolution of a pneumatocele. In most cases, pneumatoceles regress and resolve spontaneously, they wrote.

"In our patient, however, the lesion persisted throughout the 9-year observation period, a pattern also observed in a case series of two pneumatoceles arising during sorafenib therapy, which likewise failed to fully resolve during follow-up," they wrote.

The aspirated glue was identified as a neoprene-based contact adhesive containing hydrocarbon solvents.

Serial imaging allowed differentiation from other cystic lung lesions such as bullae, blebs, or true cysts, "enabling us to forgo invasive diagnostic work-up and supporting the conservative management approach adopted in this patient, given the absence of complications," the authors concluded.

#5791. IIHS Founder Warned Drivers About The Dangers Of Speed Almost A Decade Before He Started The Organization: What's Streaming

Not long before the Insurance Institute for Highway Safety was informing drivers of how dangerous or safe a car was, James S. Kemper was on a mission to improve road safety. Kemper worked in the insurance world in his first job out of high school. He was an "office boy" for Central Mutual Insurance according to his New York Times obituary, but he went on to produce his own driver safety materials: educational films.

The films were made by Kemper's personal insurance company, Lumbermen Mutual Casualty, and in partnership with the LAPD and Traffic Safety Films Inc. The LAPD connection helped bring "Sergeant Bruce" to the films, who starred on-camera several times as the presenter of important safety lessons — each video with its own specific theme. Maybe it's Sgt. Bruce's personality, or maybe it's a cop-thing, but he also delivers a pseudo 1950s-version of scared straight energy with every specific dangerous driving situation.

Join me as I introduce a new(er), sometimes nostalgic video worth adding to your Watch Later lists. Leave a note in the comments if you find an older or interesting film you'd like to see featured.

In "Speed, The Accident Multiplier," a film restored and posted by 16mm Time Machine on YouTube, the on-camera narrator, Sergeant Bruce of the LAPD, explains to viewers that speed is dangerous. He uses charts and figures to show how faster speed increases stopping distances. The biggest takeaway is that the faster you go, the longer it takes to brake, and the more likely the damage to whatever the car hits will be catastrophic. That same messaging carried over into the IIHS organization decades later.

Sgt. Bruce ended the film in a familiar way that you'll find with most of these films: A message about how engaging with these dangers will lead to death. In "Speed," he tells viewers, "Speeding is a two-edged sword. It makes an accident more probable, and at the same time, it makes death more certain." He went on to explain that one out of every three fatal accidents involved a speeding driver, "which means in one out of three cases, a driver took a chance with death and lost."

There are a couple more films Kemper and his organizations had made in 1950 that are also available on YouTube. The next two, or rather four, were uploaded by PeriscopeFilm — specifically, they're two short double-features. The first pairs "Following Too Close" and "Driver Irritations," with the second pairing including "Child Safety" and "Driver or Pedestrian" — with the latter admittedly sounding more like a dangerous game, and that was likely the point. The opening to "Driver or Pedestrian" doesn't mess around either. Within the first minute, a camera follows a man driving a car as Sgt. Bruce tells viewers, "This is a driver..." before cutting to a tombstone, where Sgt. Bruce adds, "...who was a pedestrian. This is how it happened." Quite a way to set the scene.

Nine years later, Kemper's company joined forces with another two insurance companies to form the IIHS. Another decade after that, the IIHS would become its own scientific research organization, conducting studies to better understand road safety and finding ways to help improve it. By that point, IIHS had begun to do its own vehicle safety testing, as well. Those tests — although they have changed some as vehicles have evolved quite a bit since the 1960s — still provide consumers with important information regarding the safety of their vehicles today, like its latest look into the safety of heavy-duty trucks and cargo vans.

Kemper's work in insurance also helped push mutual insurance to the forefront in the U.S., which, if you're unfamiliar, it means the insurance company is owned by policyholders. There are no stock shareholders to appease. He was adamant about ensuring people were paid for their claims. In his New York Times obituary, he's quoted saying, "I made up my mind that this company was going to settle claims for cash in the shortest time possible. I did not see then and do not see now the value of contesting claims you know you will settle." Anyone feel like a few insurance companies could take some notes? Kemper's legacy endures with his insurance company over a century later, which now exists as Kemper Insurance, and the place where he left the biggest mark — in his role establishing the IIHS.

#5790. Australian Plugin Vehicle Market Steady in October — Update

… with a spotlight on the Great Wall Motor ORA 5 rising star.

CleanTechnica readers were probably hoping for the surge to keep surging Down Under. I was. However, it appears to have remained steady. Not the flash in the pan petrolheads were hoping for at least. In September 2026, 108,778 passenger vehicles were sold, including all drivetrains. See the charts here.

Battery electric vehicles (BEVs) achieved a market share of 24.2% (26,287 units), outselling petrol-only and diesel-only vehicles. Add in 10,072 plugin hybrid (PHEV) sales and you get a plugin penetration rate of approximately 34%. PHEV sales are the fastest growing segment in Australia — increasing 122% year on year. For those who care, 17,843 HEVs sold in October (a YoY increase of 20%). I am looking forward to peak HEV, followed by peak PHEV, as we have seen in more mature Scandinavian markets.

Australia’s EV penetration rate (including BEV and PHEV) has hovered in the low to mid 30% for the last few months. Not sure what the future holds, but even just maintaining this rate for a few months will make a difference. Nothing beats lived experience. My neighbours are now asking about how much it costs to fill up. One came for coffee yesterday and stayed to go for a drive in our new Model Y. He was very impressed with the tech, the finish, and above all the running costs. He confided that he had just filled up his wife’s Honda Civic — AU$82 — and his Isuzu D Max — AU$121.

On a recent shopping expedition, we counted 10 electric cars in the car park, out of 50 vehicles. Not just Tesla and BYD, but also Zeekr, Geely, the GWM ORA 5, and a Deepal. Battery electric vehicles are making their presence felt. But not all my friends can see them. They can spot Tesla and BYD vehicles, but they tell me they don’t see any other EVs on the road. Yes, we have tried to educate them, but it is not working just yet. “See if it has an exhaust pipe. Does it have a sealed front?”

One car that I am seeing in ever increasing numbers is the refreshed Great Wall Motors ORA — now in an SUV body and a much cheaper price. When it launched back in 2023, I expected great things (see our review here). Sadly, the Funky Cat (as it is known in China) did not live up to expectations. It was the cheapest EV in Australia at the time (AU$46,000) but couldn’t compete with the BYD Dolphin and the MG 4. Now, relaunched as an SUV and with a more appropriate price (AU$34,000 — scratch that, just got an email that knocks another grand off the price), I am seeing them everywhere — 6 on the highway just yesterday.

So, on to the best-selling BEVs on the Australian market in October 2026:

Pretty much the same as last month. Obviously, BYD was the best-selling brand, closely followed by Tesla. BYD has six cars among the top twenty BEVs. There are no cars from Korean, Japanese, or European carmakers in the top ten BEV numbers. When all powertrains are considered, BYD is the second highest selling brand behind Toyota in the Australian market. There seems to be some talk in the auto news about Tesla and BYD being considered “legacy” brands — who would have thought?

Expect big things from Geely as it aggressively expands throughout Australia. Geely recently announced that it has opened 100 dealerships over the past 18 months. Its EX5 sales figures are putting pressure on the competition in the medium-SUV-segment Mazda CX-5, Hyundai Tucson, Mitsubishi Outlander, and Nissan X-Trail.

Utes still dominate the Australian market. When a compelling BEV offering becomes available, the top ten may change again. I was excited to see the KGM Musso BEV ute, but it has failed to gain traction. Only 269 have sold in the past few months. The Toyota BEV Hilux only has 14 units in the country — likely to be demonstrators. PHEV utes seem to be doing much better, led by the BYD Shark. Over 15,000 have sold so far this year.

Now for some newsy titbits: Dealers for established brands are calling for a temporary ban on new car brands (read Chinese made cars) entering Australia, as they cannot compete. The boss of the Australian Automotive Dealer Association says the industry has reached a crisis point, with the last quarter delivering the first loss since the global financial crisis. Chinese brands now own 36% of new car sales.

“This is a clear warning that the commercial settings underpinning our industry are becoming unsustainable,” Mr Voortman said. He speculates on the possible risks to consumers: “… the influx of new brands also puts consumers at risk, as it can lead to rapid depreciation of vehicles, a lag in parts supply, longer repair times, and orphaned vehicles should those companies choose to leave the country.”

Though, what we are seeing in Australia is that legacy brands, mainly European, are the ones leaving the country.

The FUD continues as misconceptions and outdated information are rolled out as facts. If you want to revisit these arguments, check this out. Trigger warning: some of you may wish to yell at the screen. The author twists himself in knots trying to make the argument that the wrong Australians are buying electric vehicles. I would contend that the right people are buying EVs — those commuting from the outer suburbs of our sprawling cities. Data shows that it is these people who are behind the EV sales boom we are currently experiencing, and these are the ones benefitting most from not paying inflated petrol costs caused by Iran’s unwillingness to admit defeat and grovel at the feet of the orange one. Please excuse my sarcasm. All of this is documented in the 10th annual Electric Vehicle Council Report. We reviewed this report here.

Despite the desperate attempt to promulgate fear of “these here new-fangled doodads” — the creation of doubt and uncertainly — every month shows how intelligent the average consumer is as they seek a better, electrified, and brighter future.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5788. A Tribute to Bunker Roy: The Man Who Turned Village Grandmothers into Solar Engineers

Sanjit “Bunker” Roy, who taught illiterate grandmothers to become solar engineers, passed away peacefully this morning at his home in Tilonia, Rajasthan, aged 81.[1] I first encountered his work not in India, but in the rain forests of Borneo.

It was October 2016, and as night fell, the surrounding Borneo tropical rain forest had come alive with noise, dominated by the trill of frogs and the rhythmic pulse of cicadas. I was about to embark on the Sabah study tour of the Emerging Leaders Dialogue Asia (ELDA). Over the next two weeks, we would visit timber plantations, mountain communities recovering from a deadly earthquake, and marine parks.

But undoubtedly the highlight for me was discovering the inspiring story of Barefoot College, which trains village grandmothers to be solar engineers. On our first evening, while enjoying a mini feast of banana fritters, pomelo, and langsat (a bit like lychees but more delicious), we were told about the college’s remarkable partnership with the Sabah Women Entrepreneurs and Professionals Association (SWEPA) in Borneo.

Barefoot College was the brainchild of Bunker Roy, and he was an unlikely revolutionary. Born into privilege in Bengal in 1945, he was educated at the Doon School and St Stephen’s College, Delhi—two of India’s most elite institutions—and was a national squash champion. A conventional career beckoned. Then, during the Bihar famine of the mid-1960s, he saw rural poverty firsthand, and his life changed course.[2] When he told his mother he wanted to live and work in a village, he later joked, she “went into a coma” and didn’t speak to him for a long time.[3]

In 1972, Roy settled in Tilonia, a dusty village in Rajasthan, and founded the Social Work and Research Centre (SWRC), which later became known as Barefoot College. Its rules turned conventional education on its head. Anyone with a university degree was disqualified from studying there, and no degrees were awarded. In 2011, Roy said that nobody at the college earned more than $100 a month. The campus itself was built in 1986 by a dozen local “barefoot architects” who could not read or write, and it went on to win an Aga Khan Award for Architecture. At the college’s night schools, children aged 6 to 14 elect their own prime minister and cabinet.[4]

Roy’s guiding conviction was simple: “There is no urban solution to a rural problem.”[5] He was awarded the Padma Shri, one of India’s highest civilian honors, in 1986, and named one of Time magazine’s 100 most influential people in 2010.[6] He is survived by his wife, Aruna Roy, the former civil servant who led the grassroots movement behind India’s Right to Information Act.[7]

The positive impact of his work has been immense. By the college’s own count at its 50th anniversary in 2022, it had trained 1,708 illiterate and semi-literate rural women from 96 countries as solar engineers, bringing light to more than a million people in over 75,000 households and saving about 45 million liters of kerosene. Its rainwater harvesting had captured some 70 billion liters and brought safe drinking water to more than 1,650 schools. More than 90,000 working children had studied in its night schools, and its barefoot health workers had treated over 300,000 patients.[8]

During our study tour, SWEPA arranged for us to meet one of Barefoot College’s beneficiaries, a so-called solar mama. She was part of the program that takes illiterate grandmothers from poor rural villages and, within six months, trains them at the college in India for their new job. They return to their villages to install and maintain full solar lighting systems. Roy deliberately chose grandmothers because they are rooted in their communities; men and younger people, he found, were more likely to take their new skills and leave for the city.[9] Because the women shared no common language, much of the training was done through sign language and hands-on practice.[10]

Malaysia’s first solar mama—very likely the woman I met that evening—was Tarihing Masanim, from Kampung Sonsogon Magandai, near Kota Marudu in Sabah. She completed her training in India in 2015 and returned to bring electricity to around 100 homes in her village.[11] Roy loved to tell stories like hers. One of his favorites was about a 55-year-old Afghan grandmother who electrified 200 houses and set an Afghan engineering department head straight on the difference between AC and DC.[12]

The story of the solar mamas captures many elements of what it means to harness the power of responsibility to create a more inclusive society and economy. So many barriers are being broken through, as these amazing women challenge stereotypes about age, gender, and rural capacity. Barefoot College shows that sustainable development—including adopting sustainable technologies—is an option for people living with low incomes in remote villages. The Barefoot model empowers people to be agents of their own destiny.

Bunker Roy stands out as an inspiring example of inclusive leadership. His impact, which is so strongly evidenced by the solar engineers program, is as much about restoring dignity to those who are traditionally excluded as it is about developing or transferring practical skills. Roy, who said he had “a very elitist, snobbish, expensive education in India” that “almost destroyed” him, explained:

“So we started the Barefoot College, and we redefined professionalism. Who is a professional? A professional is someone who has a combination of competence, confidence and belief. A water diviner is a professional. A traditional midwife is a professional. A traditional bone setter is a professional. These are professionals all over the world. You find them in any inaccessible village around the world. And we thought that these people should come into the mainstream and show that the knowledge and skills that they have is universal.”[13]

Roy ended that talk with a line often attributed to Mahatma Gandhi, whose principles guided his life’s work: “First they ignore you, then they laugh at you, then they fight you, and then you win.”[14] For the solar mamas of Barefoot College, and for the more than a million people whose homes they have lit, Bunker Roy won.

This article draws from content in Wayne Visser’s book Thriving: The Breakthrough Movement to Regenerate Nature, Society, and the Economy (New York: Fast Company Press). For more information, see waynevisser.com.

#5787. 1961 Chevy Impala Bubble Top For Sale With 1,111 Miles Shown Is A Wild LS V8 Restomod

When GM styling head Bill Mitchell set about redesigning the Chevrolet look for the 1961 model year, taking over from the iconic Harley Earl, he wanted to make a big shift away from the upright aesthetic with dramatic tail fins that defined the brand's 1950s. Moving into a smoother and cleaner jet age look, the 1961 Impala looked lower, longer, wider, and more streamlined. With wire-thin pillars and wraparound windows front and rear, the unique Sport Coupe model came to be known as a "bubbletop" in enthusiast circles. Decades on from its production date, this Impala design looks as beautiful as the day it was born. This particular example, currently for sale with a bubble-bursting $259,900 price tag, hides some modern improvements to make it an even better Impala.

Many car fanatics drive vintage cars because they're stylistically impressive, and it doesn't get much more impressive than a bubble top Impala scraping the ground on bags. There are downsides to driving a classic every day, though. Old cars typically don't have the modern amenities that American drivers have come to expect, like the unstoppable power of a fuel-injected small-block Chevrolet V8, a modern overdrive transmission, the stopping power of disc brakes, or modern air suspension. Thankfully, this Impala is fitted with all of these. This is a showstopper that was built to handle the rigors of modern traffic. What more could you want?

Take one part old Impala, one part modern Corvette, and one part creative hot-rod ingenuity, and you get a magnificent piece of automotive art. Albeit automotive art with the price tag of a long-lost Gertrude Abercrombie surrealist masterpiece. I want to know everything there is to know about this machine, and I bet you do, too.

While the car still carries all of the beautiful bodywork of a '61 Impala, it appears that just about everything under the skin has been replaced with modern alternatives. With new drivetrain components, suspension, steering, and brakes, this looks like a truly incredible driving experience waiting to happen. With the seller claiming just 1,111 miles on the odometer, it's just barely been broken in and should be taken on a nice long road trip with expediency. Imagine next summer cruising back and forth across the country on Route 66 in a black-over-red Impala and barely breaking a sweat because you have a nice fuel-efficient LS engine, graceful power steering, comfortable suspension, and ice-cold Vintage Air A/C blowing. Yeah, that's the American dream.

Back in 1961, Impala buyers would have been given three different engines to choose from, including a Blue Flame six-cylinder, a 283ci V8, and a 348ci V8. The most powerful Impala of its day featured 350 horsepower from a "Special Super Turbo-Thrust" V8 with three two-barrel carburetors. A modern LS2, cribbed from a C6 Corvette, makes an uncomplicated 400 ponies from its six-liter displacement. Presumably for the sake of simplicity and availability, the shop decided to pair this engine with a 4L60 four-speed automatic. This was an extremely common transmission, used in practically every rear-wheel drive General Motors product built from the mid-1990s to about 2010. This car should have no trouble delivering the giddyup, or laying some rubber if you want it to.

The seller describes the suspension setup thusly:

Equipped with a complete Ridetech Air Ride Suspension System, this Impala can transform from a pavement-hugging showpiece to a comfortable highway cruiser at the touch of a button!

Inside the Impala, there's an interesting juxtaposition of old and new, with gorgeous period-correct red interior upholstery wrapped around custom bucket seats, a unique center console, and plenty of retro-modern touches, like the full Dakota Digital gauge cluster. In addition to the modern air conditioning, this car is fitted with a modern (unspecified) multi-speaker sound system. I'm not sure how much music you'll need, considering the LS2's soundtrack is bolstered with a stainless-steel Borla exhaust.

I don't like the Billet Specialties wheels, and I think they're much too large for this car, but a nice set of 18s wouldn't go amiss here. Likewise, the steering wheel is a touch too far. Beyond that, I'm not sure there is much I would amend here. It's a nice car that appears to be nicely done. I'd want a few thousand more miles of testing on a completely custom car before taking it on a big trip, but the parts to fix it will be available at whatever parts counter you find along Route 66.

I'm not going to try to justify the price tag, because this is an absolutely absurd number, and I don't think they'll get anyone to pay it. If you have the spare quarter mil, though, there are a lot worse cars you could buy. Anything new in that price range is going to be faster, but will it be as effortlessly stylish? What's the point in buying a new Porsche 911 GT3 when a sea of them parks at your local cars and coffee? You could instead arrive in something that has show-worthy style, and you'd still have enough comfort to cross the country.
