#5786. The Flu Shots Are Here! The Flu Shots Are Here!

Every year, we wait on the arrival of this season's annual flu vaccine, patiently biding our time and explaining to our patients that it's coming, any day now, while the manufacturers finish their processes of selecting the candidate strains, making the vaccine, packaging it up, distributing it, and getting it into our hands at the practice through a complex system of logistics.

Every year, it seems to arrive later and later, and often in shorter supplies, than we would hope.

Many of our patient visits in late summer include specific questions: when is the flu vaccine going to be here, when can I get mine?

We usually tell them we're hoping for September but it might be October, but we've had some even later years for delivery, we're hoping to send out an announcement through the patient portal, so as a patient, please be patient.

Sometimes folks can't wait, they are really anxious to get that protection; the minute their pharmacy announces they have it in stock, they go on in and get it.

We're often deluged with messages: please update my chart to show I got the flu vaccine on September 3, at my workplace. And usually within a few days the statewide registry will catch up and dump this into their vaccine immunization history in our electronic medical record.

There always are lots of patients who don't want a flu vaccine, who've never gotten one and never will. No matter how much we try to address their hesitancy and answer their questions, some have a real reason -- some have fears that have grown from social media or friends and family, and some have had a prior severe reaction or side effect. Guillain-Barré. Optic neuritis. Anaphylaxis.

Try as we might, we never get everybody vaccinated, but I'm incredibly grateful that we do such a good job of getting it to everyone we can before flu season is really barreling down in full force.

When I first started out in practice, I never really bothered getting the flu shot.

While I'd had the flu a few times in my life (once a miserable few days spent in bed feeling like death-warmed over), I always figured it wouldn't be that bad and I've always been healthy and I didn't really need the vaccine.

As I matured into my practice, I became more convinced by the arguments of my colleagues and our infectious disease friends and epidemiologists and public health experts, showing that I was actually putting patients at risk, including during that subclinical phase when I would be carrying and shedding the virus and potentially spreading it to my patients, who could fare far worse with a case of influenza than I might.

Now that I've matured even further (and by "matured" I mean "gotten older"), I suddenly find myself qualifying for the "senior flu shot," a milestone I once looked at as being far off in the distance, now suddenly upon me.

Since we got the flu vaccine in our practice this season, it has been incredibly busy, and I haven't had time to get mine. I keep making excuses: I'm going out to dinner or playing squash tonight, big presentation in the morning, I'll get it later.

Suddenly this week, I realized the deadline for all of us at our institution getting vaccinated was upon us, and it was time to get my jab.

In the old days, one of my partners used to give me the flu shot, and I would give him his, but now we want to make sure it gets accurately registered in the system so that Workforce Health and Safety can close our files, mark us as compliant with our mandatory annual influenza vaccination.

So, I dutifully scheduled an appointment at a local pharmacy, and stopped in to check off that box.

Overall, everything went smoothly. One of the folks working behind the pharmacy counter checked me in after confirming my name and date of birth, and I was the only one waiting so the nurse called me right in.

She went through the same process of confirming my date of birth, asked which arm I wanted it in (my right deltoid, since I'm left-handed), a quick pinch, and I was done.

Think how amazing this is: I'm able to get this done, to protect myself and my patients and my family and my friends and community from what could be a potentially deadly illness.

Science and public health and our amazing healthcare system have so much to offer to keep people healthy, to get them healthy, to prevent and treat and cure.

I know some people will read this and scoff, have their arguments about why vaccines are terrible, say we've all been deluded by some secret cabal of people trying to make money and causing some disease or changing my DNA, but for the most part it would be wonderful if everyone would just believe us, let us do right by providing the best care with the best evidence.

Someone can say no, but we should never deny any group of people, any community, any individual, the opportunity to seek the best healthcare they can get.

I'm hopeful as we've seen the results of the midterm elections last week that opportunities to improve healthcare, to bring calmer and saner voices to the table, will revitalize and transform our healthcare system, have it live up to the potential it holds, and take us down a series of brilliant pathways that will allow us to serve our patients as best we can.

#5785. Court blocks CMS' moratorium on new ACA broker registrations

Court blocks CMS' moratorium on new ACA broker registrations" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

A federal court has granted a preliminary injunction to two key groups representing insurance brokers over the Trump administration's moratorium on new broker enrollments.

The District Court of Maryland issued the injunction after determining that the National Association of Benefits and Insurance Professionals and Health Agents for America "have shown that they are likely to succeed on the merits of their claims under the Administrative Procedure Act."

The court also said that the organizations made clear they would likely suffer significant harm if the moratorium stays in place.

NABIP said that the ruling means that it will restart training for new brokers beginning on Nov. 6.

"Today’s ruling is a significant victory for licensed agents and brokers and the consumers who rely on their expertise," said NABIP President Mychal Walker in an announcement (PDF). "With Open Enrollment already underway, every day matters."

"Lifting the moratorium immediately and beginning training tomorrow will help affected professionals get to work assisting individuals and families in finding coverage that fits their needs and budgets," said Walker.

NABIP initially sued on Oct. 15 to challenge the moratorium. The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services instituted the moratorium in late September as part of its broader push to combat fraud on the Affordable Care Act's exchanges.

The National Association of Benefits and Insurance Professionals is suing the Trump administration over its moratorium on new brokers entering the Affordable Care Act marketplaces.

The organization, the leading trade group for insurance brokers and agents, said the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services' moratorium impacts close to 19,000 brokers and agents who will be barred from registering ahead of open enrollment.

CMS unveiled the moratorium in late September as part of its ongoing push to root out fraud, waste and abuse. In addition, the agency said it would terminate more than 200 agents and brokers that failed to comply with marketplace standards.

The agency said new brokers and agents were disproportionately responsible for submitting inappropriate enrollments and engaging in behaviors at high risk for fraud.

In an announcement (PDF), NABIP says that it supports the broader effort to combat fraud, but that CMS' moratorium "threatens the livelihoods of thousands of legitimate, licensed professionals who have not been accused of fraud and limits consumers’ access to professional assistance as they navigate Open Enrollment."

The organization argues in the lawsuit that the rule instituting the moratorium violates both the Administrative Procedure Act and the Affordable Care Act. The regulation is unlawful as it is "arbitrary, capricious, and unsupported by sufficient evidence," NABIP said.

The 19,000 agents who would be barred from participating under the rule are part of a critical network of professionals who help consumers find coverage that meets their needs, the organization said.

NABIP President Mychal Walker said that CMS should look elsewhere in its fraud efforts.

"The existence of fraud is not in dispute. NABIP has raised these concerns with CMS repeatedly and has offered concrete solutions to address them," Walker said in the announcement. "What is in dispute is whether the appropriate response is to exclude thousands of legitimate agents based solely on whether they registered last year, without any individualized evidence about them."

"There is a better approach: target fraud where it occurs, strengthen the system’s security and hold bad actors accountable while preserving consumers’ access to trusted professionals," Walker continued.

CMS has taken steps to crack down on brokers as the agency views improper ACA enrollments as a key target in its fight against fraud. CMS officials have repeatedly raised concerns that people are enrolled in ACA plans without their knowledge, or despite being deceased.

Earlier this week, the agency revealed it will purge 189,000 enrollees who are currently listed as enrolled in both ACA plans and other coverage, such as Medicaid or Medicare.

#5784. Oh, So That’s Who’s Buying All Those New Subaru Trailseeker EVs…

After EV sales in the US crashed into a brick wall last year, the Subaru Trailseeker has been one of the few points of light. Newly introduced this year, Trailseeker has racked up a relatively decent sales track record in the crowded SUV field. As for whether or not Subaru can continue to grow its EV sales in the US, the automaker’s elaborate, multi-faceted “Subaru Loves Pets” campaign has emerged as the secret weapon…

The Subaru Loves Pets campaign first appeared 18 years ago, back in 2008. A lot of demographic and cultural water has passed under the bridge since then. More so than in past generations, pets have taken on the role of family members equal to, if not superior to, anyone else in the household. And if that’s the case, shouldn’t pets have their own seatbelts, too?

Now Subaru’s longtime relationship with the pet-centered population is beginning to muscle into EV territory. One indication of a connection between pet and EV preferences surfaced in October, when Subaru released a new report indicating that pets wield a noticeable influence on purchasing  preferences in the US auto market overall.

Commissioned from the firm Talker Research, the report is based on interviews with 2,000 and pet owners in the US. Of that cohort, a resounding majority of 73% said they are more likely to drive for the holidays, specifically in order to travel with their pet.

“As the holidays approach, more than two-thirds (68%) said their pet will affect their travel plans this year, and nearly half (45%) have turned down travel opportunities that couldn’t accommodate their pet,” Subaru adds.

Households with pets also have distinct priorities for a vehicle. “Plenty of room between pets, passengers, and luggage tops the list of features pet owners say matter when traveling with an animal,” Suburu reports.

“More than one in four (26%) prioritize cargo space for crates, carriers, beds, or gear. Among hybrid and EV drivers, that climbs to 35%,” the automaker further notes.

If you missed the money quote, it’s that thing about 35% of hybrid and EV drivers prioritizing space for pet gear, compared to just 26% of drivers overall. In addition to the traveling-with-pets angle, that generational difference also reflects a sharp generational divergence in attitudes towards EVs.

“Electric vehicles have become a political flashpoint in Washington. But new national polling finds the biggest divide among potential EV buyers isn’t political. It’s generational,” reports the advocacy organization ZETA (the Zero Emission Transportation Association) in September.

“Seventy-five percent of adults ages 18–34 and 72% of adults ages 35–49 say they are likely to purchase or lease a new EV within the next five years,” ZETA elaborates. “Among adults ages 50–64, that number falls to 50%. Among those 65 and older, it drops to just 23%, a striking 52-point gap between the youngest and oldest Americans.”

Now let’s take another look at the Subaru survey. Talker Research found that 75% of Gen Z and 70% of millennials traveled with a pet within the past five years, compared with just 58% of Gen X and only 40% of baby boomers (looking at you, Mitt Romney).

A similar generational difference surfaced in other areas of the survey:

Younger pet owners are also more likely to say they plan travel around their pet, with 42% of Gen Z and millennials doing so compared with 27% of Gen X and boomers.

Among pet owners who haven’t yet road-tripped with their pet, 74% of Gen Z and millennials would consider it, compared with 54% of Gen X and boomers.

Subaru pulled back on its overall electrification plan for the US market after last year’s EV sales crash, but it did follow through on the new Trailseeker, offered for sale on a limited basis in January of this year. Volume deliveries to dealers began in April of this year, and in May the Trailseeker racked up 1,074 in sales. The number is vanishingly small compared to industry leader Tesla but a promising start in the challenging US market. By August, Trailseeker had a total of almost 5,000 in sales for the year to its credit, rising to 6,006 sold by the end of October.

All else being equal, recognition from Wards Auto may help keep the momentum going. On October 13, Subaru announced that the Trailseeker earned a slot in the 2026 Wards 10 Best Engines & Propulsion Systems list.

“For 2026, Wards evaluated 26 powertrains from returning winners as well as all-new or significantly updated cars, trucks, or SUVs. They were evaluated on several criteria, including power, torque, refinement, efficiency, and innovation,” Subaru notes.

“Its 375 horsepower combined with immediate electric powertrain response always satisfies. Its range estimates also hold up well in real-world driving, interstate and all. Yet Subaru keeps the price completely reasonable given such capability,” said Robin Warner, who manages the 10 Best Engines & Propulsion Systems program.

By “completely reasonable,” Warner means the Trailseeker’s starting MSRP of $39,995 and its estimated range of 281 miles. According to Subaru, the Trailseeker can go from 10% to 80% charged in as little as 28 minutes, with up to 150 kW on a DC-fast charger.

“With an onboard battery preconditioning system, enabled either by navigating to a charging destination or by the driver via touchscreen controls, the Trailseeker can charge its battery in cold climates — as low as 14 degrees Fahrenheit — at speeds comparable to those in normal weather conditions,” Subaru also advises.

In addition to the seal of approval from Wards, on October 29 Subaru announced that Trailseeker earned the “Car of the Year” award in the 2027 TopGear.com US Car Awards program

The automaker cites Top Gear US Correspondent Alex Kalogiannis, who was particularly enthused about the vehicle’s off-road performance. ““Off the beaten path, the set-and-forget X-MODE figures out all the tricky terrain stuff as you trundle through the bush, and with about 8.5 inches of ground clearance, it can scale all but the scariest obstacles in your path,” Kalogiannis remarked, somwhat colorfully.

“People still carry a torch for the WRX and its many rally-inspired incarnations, but the heart of Subaru fandom now is its dutiful, rugged, useful wagon – the Trailseeker,” he added.

That remains to be seen. Trailseeker will have to compete for the hearts and minds of Subaru fans against other models in the automaker’s lineup, including gasmobiles. The SUV-styled Outback wagon, for example, clocked 118,470 in sales from January to the end of October this year, and that was not the only Subaru model to hit six figures.

Still, US President Donald Trump has provided Subaru and other automakers with a new opportunity grow their EV sales profile this year. On February 28 the President launched his war against Iran, sending global oil prices on an upwards trajectory and improving the affordability case for EVs regardless of the loss of the $7,500 federal EV tax credit, which Trump and his Republican majority in Congress axed last fall.

Ever since dropping the first bombs on Iran, Trump has repeatedly claimed that the war is over, or almost over, or over any minute. In the latest pronouncement, on  Monday Trump said that multilateral negotiations over a peace settlement in Iran are in a state of “positive momentum toward an end.” And if you believe that…

Photo: Subaru has racked up a fairly respectable EV sales record so far this year for its new Trailseeker SUV (2026 Trailseeker cropped, courtesy of Subaru).

#5783. How A Salesman Accidentally Convinced Shaquille O'Neal To Buy 3 Ridiculously Expensive Cars

Shaquille O'Neal doesn't do small or understated. The 7-foot-1 four-time NBA champion has an instantly recognizable brand. Oh, he's worth around $500 million, too. Naturally, as with many other well-to-do star athletes, he has a penchant for flashy cars. Problem is, he seldom fits in them without some contortions and modification. It's what drove him to have a Lamborghini lengthened into a Franken-Gallardo. That stretched supercar isn't the only time Shaq did some silly stuff with expensive cars.

In what was almost certainly a prideful purchase, O'Neal bought three outrageously expensive cars on the spot. Why? All it took was one condescending question from a less-than-respectful Rolls-Royce dealership salesman, and Shaq bought the lot. Just like that, the champ and business mogul had three extra opulent cars he says "I never drive."

Don't believe it? One of them, a 2004 Rolls-Royce Phantom, just sold on Bring-a-Trailer. If that wasn't enough, the once-undisputed heavyweight champion of the world, Mike Tyson, may have had a hand in nudging the Diesel to buy three ultraluxe cars instead of a solitary sedan. Of course, it's not difficult to get carried away when shopping for Rolls-Royces; just look at the one-off Phantom inspired by "Goldfinger."

Some 30 years ago, Shaquille O'Neal signed a groundbreaking $120 million deal to don Los Angeles Lakers colors and put away his Orlando Magic jersey. Several years later, he wandered into a Rolls-Royce showroom with his friend Alex and started browsing the hand-built grandeur.

Now, O'Neal had money to spare, but he wasn't quite up for indiscriminate spending. "I like to spend money, but I don't like to waste money. So I don't want to spend $400,000 for a car," O'Neal recalled in a Revolt interview. He went about asking a salesperson about the prices of the showroom's many Roll-Royces. That's when the salesperson said the wrong thing. Or the right thing, considering the commission on three hilariously expensive cars.

In O'Neal's book, "Shaq Uncut: My Story,"  he recalls the question as especially disrespectful, be it accidental or deliberate. The salesperson gave the Lakers sweatpants-clad O'Neal a glance and, per Basketball Network, asked, "Are you sure you can afford it, sonny boy?" That did it. Shaq lost it. "I said, 'What the f*** did you just say to me?'" So, he pointed around the showroom. "I want that silver hardtop one, and I want the green one, and give that red one over there, too."

One of those cars, the silver hardtop O'Neal selected, was a 2004 Rolls-Royce Phantom. That car sold in November 2026 on Bring-a-Trailer for $62,000. Not exactly the $320,000 or so O'Neal likely paid for it when it was new in 2004. Although he was shopping in a Rolls-Royce dealer, Shaq's book mentions a handful of Bentleys as part of the splurge. Whichever British luxury marque made O'Neal's list of picks, it was quite the spendy visit to a dealership.

Shaq's financial flexing and what is likely the most impressive commission that Rolls-Royce salesperson has ever made makes for a pretty crazy story. Believe it or not, it gets even more wild from there. According to O'Neal's book, "Iron" Mike himself, Mike Tyson, was in the mood for Bentley shopping just as the Laker found himself at the dealership.

Shaq recalled seeing Tyson picking out a couple of Bentleys and the need to out-do the champion pugilist. "Next thing I know Tyson picked out two. He's buying two of these cars, so you know what I've got to do. I've got to buy three!" Though it's not clear what ratio of Rolls-Royce to Bentleys made up his impulse shopping spree.

Be it Iron Mike or the mouthy salesperson who drove Shaq to pull the trigger, the outlandishly expensive price tag for the purchase hit him after. "A million dollars gone in 10 minutes," the massive athlete once lamented in an interview with Vanity Fair. Frankly, that's not the only time O'Neal has had the rotten luck with luxury cars. After spending the money to have it customized to fit his considerable frame, his Lucid Air Coupe ended up in a salvage yard less than two years later. Bummer.

#5782. Humana pacing ahead of expectations during Medicare enrollment window, CEO says

Humana pacing ahead of expectations during Medicare enrollment window, CEO says" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Humana offered a look at early results from Medicare's annual enrollment period during its earnings call on Friday morning.

CEO Jim Rechtin told investors that based on signups so far, the insurer expects its membership to grow yet again in 2027. This includes both strong performance in recapturing members who were impacted by the company's market exits as well as new sales that are "ahead of plan."

"So far, AEP performance is ahead of our initial expectations," Rechtin said. "While it remains very early in AEP, we currently anticipate that our membership will be up for 2027."

Humana saw a significant enrollment increase for 2026, which has impacted the acuity of its membership and led to an uptick in its medical loss ratio. The company's 2026 star ratings performance has also proven a drag on the performance this year.

However, Humana's performance in the latest round of star ratings made for a significant boon, with it earning "the highest ratings Humana has ever achieved," per Rechtin.

The team has also been hard at work in identifying health risks and pushing to better outcomes, he said. For example, 534,000 more members completed an annual preventative care visit. In addition, about 28,000 members received an overdue mammogram, which found 600 previously unidentified cases of breast cancer.

With these results, Rechtin said that the company expects a one-time tailwind in 2028 that it can use to continue investing in improvements that support stars performance.

He also said Humana expects to return some of that value to shareholders as "we do not believe we can productively invest the full tailwind."

"Once we have more information on 2028, which includes competitive dynamics, membership and product dynamics, and regulatory environment, we will quantify the level of and the expected use of the tailwind," Rechtin said. "We will size how much we will invest, and we will explain roughly how much we will pass through to earnings."

Humana beat the Street for both earnings and revenue in the third quarter.

The company reported $162 million in profit and $41.2 billion in revenue for Q3, both of which surpassed analysts' predictions, per Zacks Investment Research. By comparison, Humana posted $195 million in profit and $32.6 billion in revenue for the third quarter of 2025.

Across the first three quarters of the year, the company has earned $2 billion in profit and brought in $121.7 billion in revenue. Revenue is up significantly year-over-year, as the company reported $97.1 billion in revenue across the first nine months of 2025.

Earnings are on par with the previous year, as the company also had $2 billion in profit through three quarters in 2025.

"Humana is delivering positive results for our members, our patients and their health," said Humana CEO Jim Rechtin in the press release. "We are also increasingly driving financial and operating performance toward being 'ahead' of our plan."

Humana posted a medical loss ratio of 93.9% in the quarter, up from a 91.1% ratio in Q3 2025. MLR through three quarters is also elevated compared to 2025, at 91.5% compared to 89.93%.

The insurer attributed this increase in part to its significant jump in Medicare enrollment for this year, as those newer members tend to be higher acuity. It also said the ratio was impacted by its 2026 star ratings performance, which was a consistent headwind.

The company's pricing strategy for 2026 and planned footprint contraction both helped to dull the impact of those headwinds, Humana said.

Revenues at Humana's core insurance business were $39.1 billion, up from $31.2 billion, per the report. Much of that revenue growth was driven by its membership increases, though the star ratings scores did offset that to some degree.

At CenterWell, revenues were $7.2 billion, growing from $5.9 billion. The company said it saw higher revenue across its CenterWell units that was linked to membership growth in 2026.

On the back of the performance, Humana boosted its guidance to $9.50 in earnings per share for the year. It expects at least $160 billion in revenue for 2026.

#5781. Bulevirtide Linked to Less Decompensation in Chronic Hep D With Cirrhosis

— However, recently approved drug did not appear to reduce liver cancer risk in retrospective study

In patients with chronic hepatitis delta (CHD) with cirrhosis, treatment with bulevirtide (Hepcludex) was associated with lower risk of liver decompensation, according to results from a European retrospective study.

Among more than 700 CHD patients with cirrhosis, those treated with bulevirtide had significantly lower rates of de-novo decompensation compared with untreated patients, with 3-year cumulative incidence rates of 3% versus 11.3%, respectively (P=0.001), reported Elisabetta Degasperi, MD, PhD, of the Foundation IRCCS Ca' Granda Ospedale Maggiore Policlinico in Milan.

On the other hand, there was no significant difference in 3-year de-novo hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) rates between the two cohorts (7.3% for treated patients vs 3.6% for untreated patients, P=0.86).

"In chronic hepatitis delta patients with cirrhosis, a 36-month course of bulevirtide monotherapy reduces decompensation, but not HCC, compared to historical pooled untreated cohorts," Degasperi said at the annual Liver Meeting in Denver, sponsored by the American Association for the Study of Liver Diseases.

"Chronic hepatitis delta is the most severe form of viral hepatitis, resulting in fast progression to cirrhosis and its complications, such as hepatocellular carcinoma and decompensation," Degasperi said, in explaining the rationale behind the study.

She pointed out that bulevirtide has been approved by the FDA for treatment of CHD in adults without cirrhosis or with compensated cirrhosis, and that in phase II and III clinical trials and real-world studies, bulevirtide monotherapy has achieved higher rates of virological and biochemical responses in patients with cirrhosis.

"However, the impact of bulevirtide treatment on hard clinical outcomes remains scarcely defined," she added.

Here, CHD patients with cirrhosis receiving bulevirtide monotherapy for up to 3 years in a retrospective European cohort study were compared with pooled data from two cohorts not treated with bulevirtide. The study endpoints were the incidence of liver-related events (decompensation and HCC), death, or liver transplantation.

The study included a total of 729 patients (431 treated with bulevirtide and 298 untreated controls). There were some significant differences in baseline characteristics between the cohorts (including age and sex), but inverse probability treatment weighing (IPTW) was used to balance the groups.

Among patients treated with bulevirtide monotherapy, 72% achieved virological response, 70% biochemical response, and 54% a combined response at 3 years.

During the course of the study, there were a total of 36 de novo liver-related events in bulevirtide-treated patients (12 decompensation and 24 HCC) and 32 in untreated patients (24 decompensation and eight HCC).

In the IPTW-adjusted Cox regression analysis, bulevirtide-treated patients had significantly lower risk of developing liver-related events compared with untreated controls (HR 0.69, 95% CI 0.49-0.99, P=0.04) or decompensation (HR 0.66, 95% CI 0.44-0.98, P=0.03). These results were confirmed by the IPTW-adjusted competing risks regression model.

Conversely, the risk of HCC was similar between the two groups (HR 0.83, 95% CI 0.50-1.36, P=0.46).

Among patients treated with bulevirtide, lower baseline albumin values independently predicted liver decompensation (HR 0.17, 95% CI 0.07-0.39, P=0.001), while independent predictors of HCC in this group also included lower albumin (HR 0.22, 95% CI 0.11-0.44, P<0.001), as well as older age (HR 1.05, 95% CI 1.01-1.09, P=0.01) and male sex (HR 3.72, 95% CI 1.39-9.91, P=0.01).

Regarding overall survival (OS), "we did not see any significant difference between bulevirtide-treated and untreated patients, and this was also confirmed in the adjusted analysis," Degasperi observed. The 3-year OS rate was 95.2% with bulevirtide and 96.2% among untreated patients (P=0.63).

However, "if you concentrate on the features of events, we can see that most events leading to liver transplant, and also death, in the bulevirtide-treated cohort were HCC related, while in untreated patients, end-stage liver disease was the most frequent liver-related event leading to death and liver transplant," she pointed out.

Specifically, there were a total of 16 deaths or liver transplantations in the bulevirtide arm, 12 of which were HCC related, while seven of the nine deaths or liver transplantations in the untreated cohort were related to end-stage liver disease.

"Of course, additional studies in cohorts with extended follow-up are needed in order to confirm these data," she concluded.

#5780. Experience The Gales Of November With This Real-Time Great Lakes Simulator

The wreck of the SS Edmund Fitzgerald became a bit of a meme last year during the 50th anniversary of the disaster. Gen Zers rediscovered the great Gordon Lightfoot song and it was all over the interwebz. Those youths can now experience the thrill, terror and wonder of the Great Lakes firsthand with my new favorite obsession, the website In The Lakes; a free site which shows you the real-time conditions on any one of the great lakes without tracking your data.

Using buoys located on Lake Superior, Lake Michigan, Lake Huron, Lake Erie, and Lake Ontario and honorable mention Lake St. Claire, In The Lake users can view a digital simulation of conditions. Different colors on the map represent real-time wave heights. As of the writing of this story, both Lake Superior and Lake Michigan are mostly red, indicative of choppy waters and waves reaching 10 feet or more. My first "Float" sent me just north of Sault Ste. Marie in Lake Superior where it was, predictably, cold, grey, and extra choppy — just the sort of thing the Edmund Fitz faced on that cold day in November, 1975.

But it's not all rough seas and wind. Pick a calmer section of lake and spend a few hours of your work day sipping a beverage, listening to a radio that features local radio stations as well as multiple genre stations, or meditate. The meditate feature is fairly entertaining, with inspirational messages meant to resonant with folks from the Great Lakes. The last time I pressed it, a message popped up reading "Somewhere, someone's shoveling a driveway. Not you. Not today." Damn right.

But it's not just floaties. You can find the option to "Take the helm in these conditions." And the familiar lake scene will load with a kayak, schooner, or even a Coast Guard ice breaking ship. I tried out the kayak to see what it was like for my husband when he puttered around Lake Superior on his own kayak, a feat that nearly killed him. You can also dive the depths of the lakes, or write a message and pop it in a bottle, then set it into the lake and see where the real forecast takes it.

In the Experiences tab you can take on several adventures from repairing the Cort for sailing duties to Cruising the Soo Locks. There's also a plethora of adventures to go on. I opted for the Brigade to the Rendezvous, an 1815 canoe trip to deliver beaver furs to Fort William, a midway trading point between the Midwest and Montreal. Teachers can also find a dedicated hub with tons of resources to go over critical things related to the Great Lakes, appropriate for certain grade levels.

Or keep it simple and continue to enjoy the occasional float on one of the Great Lakes. You can use it to escape your kids as easily as educate them, after all. An extra click will allow your setting to reset (change) every five minutes without your interference. So relax, ride the waves, and beware the gales of November.

#5779. CMS proposes rollbacks of 'excessively burdensome' Conditions of Participation

CMS proposes rollbacks of 'excessively burdensome' Conditions of Participation" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services plans to trim several “unnecessary” requirements for hospitals and other providers participating in Medicare, including emergency services readiness requirements and staff training for obstetrical services that had been brought during the tail end of the Biden administration.

The proposed rule’s Conditions of Participation (CoP) rollbacks, shared late Thursday (PDF), followed a “comprehensive review” CMS conducted to identify and eliminate “obsolete, outdated and excessively burdensome regulations.”

Removing these, the agency estimated, would save $320 million and free up 3.26 million hours of staff time for provider organizations.

Among the chief changes is a repeal of requirements finalized in the 2025 Outpatient Prospective Payment System (OPPS) and ASC Payment Systems rule that tasked hospital and critical access hospital emergency departments to be “continually prepared” to provide emergency screening or stabilization regardless of whether they provide relevant specialty services, which were made effective in July 2025.

The 2025 rule paired those with baseline staffing and care delivery standards, annual staff training and maternal quality assessment and performance improvement (QAPI) requirements around obstetric care, which are currently set to take effect on Jan. 1, 2027. CMS also proposed eliminating those requirements this week.

CMS under the prior administration had said these requirements would improve safety, quality and the timeliness of care delivery for pregnant women.

On the other hand, CMS' new proposed rule looks to add a new obstetrics requirement for hospitals and critical access hospitals tied to the collection of birth tissue, “including standards for informed consent and policies and procedures to support families requesting their birth tissues for cultural, religious or personal reasons,” per a fact sheet.

Other proposed changes to the CoPs include clarification revisions to policies regarding distance-related eligibility for critical access hospital designation and to implement an exemption from the distance eligibility requirements for some critical access hospitals.

Long-term care facilities would also see changes around COVID-19 vaccination education for staff and uptake for residents, as well as exemptions for existing facilities from newer room occupancy and bathroom facility standards. Among other changes are removals or slim downs of respiratory illness data reporting requirements for multiple types of provider facilities.

The proposed rule will be open for public comment for 60 days. Among these, CMS said it is interested in hearing from stakeholders on additional requirements for participating providers, including flexibilities for physician supervision of certified registered nurse anesthetists, expanding the scope of practice of nurse practitioners and physician assistants in long-term care facilities, and reducing barriers for new transplant programs to achieve initial certification.

#5778. At $14,000, Is This 1993 Toyota MR2 A Red-Hot Deal?

According to its seller, today's Nice Price or No Dice MR2 is a highly optioned model and was once sold on Bring a Trailer. It's been demoted to a Facebook Marketplace ad, and it's up to us to decide whether its price matches that lower-tier venue.

Rumored federal rollover safety standards effectively killed off convertible production in the U.S. from 1976 to the early '80s. The convertibles that boldly remained on the market during this time were all older cars like the MGB and Mercedes-Benz SL.

It was that latter convertible, the two-seater Benz, that served as the role model for the 1977 Cadillac Seville Milan we looked at on Monday. A custom-bodied two-seater convertible, the Milan attempted to fill that obvious gap in North America's car lineup. Unfortunately, the car's odd proportions gave the impression that upon arrival, its occupants would be revealed to be a congress of clowns rather than a chic couple with wind-tousled hair. That factor, plus questions about the car's condition, made us all question the seller's set $8,000 asking price. In short order, that dropped in an 82% 'No Dice' loss.

When it comes to styling and proportions, it's safe to say that Toyota hit the mark with the second-generation MR2. Larger, more cohesive, and roomier, the SW20 edition traded the first MR2's odd mini-Ferrari Mondial look for one more reverent to the better-looking Ferrari 328 GTB. It still checked all the boxes that made the first generation popular: a mid-engine layout, pop-up headlamps, and an optional T-top roof. Where the second-generation car strayed from the first was in size (it's substantially larger in almost all dimensions), and in the breathing of its top-models as the earlier cars' supercharger was traded for a turbo. One other distinction of the SW20 (perhaps unfairly) is that its handling at the limit can bite unwary drivers on the butt.

This 1993 MR2 comes in the officially named Super Red, making it a perfect accompaniment to the Man of Steel's cape and booties. This is a non-turbo car, so horsepower is limited to 135 ponies from the 5S-FE DOHC four. Fortunately, that engine is mated to a five-speed manual to make the most of that output. Even better, the seller says a new clutch was installed 46k miles ago, and the timing belt and water pump were refreshed just 26,000 miles back. New motor mounts, pads and rotors, and T-top seals are additional milestones in the car's service history.

This MR2 looks the part of a well-cared-for car, too. According to the seller, it was bought in Florida and spent much of its life in the South before being brought to New Jersey in 2018. When the present owner made that move, the car had 118,000 miles on the clock. It now has 136,788 miles, which averages about 2,400 miles a year under the current owner's care.

Overall, the car looks to be in great condition. The paint still pops, set off against factory alloys that show no evidence of injury. The engine looks cramped under its cover, and the sweeping buttresses make maintenance even more of a back-breaker, but at least it all looks intact.

The cabin doesn't appear cramped at all and looks quite handsome in its black-and-biscuit color scheme, with swanky leather upholstery on the seating surfaces. It all appears stock in here, save for a TRD-branded leather shift knob, although that does keep it in the family.

On the downside, the seller warns that the A/C compressor is grinding and hence they've removed the belt to keep it from turning and potentially doing more damage. The ad also makes no mention of the tires' age or condition. With so few miles driven each year, it's easy to let tires hang on longer than they should. The MR2 features a staggered setup, with larger, wider wheels in the back, meaning that rotating front to rear is out of the question, making the meats even more out of sight, out of mind.

On the plus side, the ad notes that all of the car's glass has been VIN-etched as an anti-theft measure. In addition, the car comes with a clean title, not one but two options for T-top storage, and three keys. Oddly, though the seller says this was a heavily optioned car, it does not seem to have a remote-entry key fob. Weird. There are also two manila folders full of maintenance records going back to the previous owner. Those will accompany the car in the sale.

On the face of it, this appears to be a solid example of a stock, mostly complete second-gen non-turbo MR2 with all the right specs and the promise of a well-managed service history. What's not to like, right? Well, there's that sweaty-Betty non-working A/C. That's going to cost a pretty penny to fix. We should also probably consider a new set of tires unless, upon inspection, they still seem to be in the game. Other than that, there don't seem to be any major red flags on this car. Plus, who doesn't dig an old MR2?

That only leaves us with the car's $14,000 asking price to either rejoice or bemoan. Let's talk about that. What's your opinion on this MR2 at that $14,000 asking? Does that sound fair given the car's presentation and specs? Or, at that price, is this Mister a miss?

Facebook Marketplace out of Tenafly, New Jersey, or go here if the ad disappears.

#5777. Why Not Sirolimus for Active Lupus?

— Trial shows sirolimus very effective as second-line treatment, but safety issues simmer

ORLANDO -- Patients with systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) that is uncontrolled despite standard therapy did well with a daily oral dose of sirolimus in a trial from China, although safety concerns weren't entirely relieved.

In a 145-patient randomized trial, 54.3% of those assigned to 1.5 mg/day of the ultrapotent immunosuppressant achieved so-called SRI-4 responses within 24 weeks, indicating clinically significant improvement, compared with 22.7% of patients receiving placebo (difference 31.6 percentage points, 95% CI 16.6-46.7), according to Liying Peng, MD, of Peking Union Medical Hospital in Beijing.

Secondary endpoints all favored sirolimus, including changes in SLE Disease Activity Index 2000 (SLEDAI-2K) score and achievement of low lupus disease activity state (LLDAS), she told attendees at the American College of Rheumatology's annual meeting.

Of course, safety is a major concern with sirolimus, insofar as the drug inhibits a broad range of immune activity and thus brings substantial risk for infections. But if these 24-week data can be relied on, the risks may prove to be acceptable for patients in need of an easily accessed therapy for refractory SLE. Of the 70 patients receiving the drug, only two serious infections were recorded, and just four stopped sirolimus because of adverse events, Peng said. One patient in each arm died, but these were considered unrelated to treatment. The most frequent adverse clinical event was menstrual irregularity (14%, versus none in the placebo group).

Adverse effects were common with sirolimus but were primarily laboratory abnormalities. These included a 50% rate of elevated triglycerides, 37% for increased cholesterol, and 27% and 21% for leukopenia and neutropenia, respectively. If the trial had continued for longer, it's likely that related clinical problems would have arisen; an ongoing 24-week, open-label extension phase should shed more light. But Peng made no suggestion that, in ordinary clinical use, sirolimus would need to be continued indefinitely for patients who respond adequately.

Peng's is not the first study to examine sirolimus as a lupus treatment. A Lancet report in 2018 described the immunosuppressant's use in 43 patients with refractory SLE, yielding a 50% reduction in SLEDAI-2K scores without major safety issues among the 29 who completed a full year of therapy. That was a single-arm, unblinded trial, however, prompting Peng and colleagues to mount the current double-blind, placebo-controlled trial.

To be eligible, patients needed a SLEDAI-2K score of at least 4 after having received standard antimalarials or immunosuppressants such as mycophenolate mofetil for 12 weeks or longer. Those with substantial renal impairment, other comorbid autoimmune conditions, or a recent history of more potent immunosuppressive therapy (intravenous cyclophosphamide, for example) were excluded. All patients continued on standard medications during the trial.

Mean age of the trial participants was about 35, and over 90% were women. Disease duration averaged some 9 years. Baseline SLEDAI-2K scores stood at roughly 10, and Physician Global Assessment (PGA) scores averaged just over 1.4. About 40% showed moderate renal involvement.

SRI-4 served as the primary outcome. It combines three separate endpoints: at least 4-point reduction in SLEDAI-2K score, at least 0.3-point improvement in PGA score, and no more than one organ with new moderate involvement plus no new serious involvement. Secondary endpoints, in addition to those noted above, included clinical response by British Isles Composite Lupus Assessment (BICLA) criteria, achievement of SLEDAI-2K of zero ("clinical remission"), and change from baseline in PGA score.

BICLA responses were achieved by 60% as opposed to 31% with placebo at 24 weeks. SLEDAI-2K fell by an average 4.7 points with sirolimus versus 1.0 point with placebo. Biomarkers such as anti-double stranded DNA antibody titers all improved markedly.

More stringent outcomes, however, were difficult to achieve. Only 14% of the sirolimus group got to full clinical remission, and 24% to LLDAS, which is similar to SRI-4 but stricter, with no activity in major organs.

Overall, the outcomes with sirolimus were similar to those typically seen with biologic drugs for lupus. But as Peng explained, "cost and access remain barriers" to their use. This is not just an issue for less developed nations -- while biologics such as belimumab (Benlysta) are usually covered under Medicaid in the U.S., prior authorization and quantity limits apply in many states. As a shelf-stable oral tablet, sirolimus may also be more practical for certain patients.

#5776. What Car Looks Best On Aftermarket Wheels?

Let's face it: Most stock wheels are, at best, fine. Occasionally one will stand out for its looks, usually because it's been made by Mercedes, but most OEM wheels are just some basic metal that you can wrap a tire around. That's good enough for getting from point A to point B, but we car enthusiasts often want better. Enter the entire world of aftermarket wheels, in any style you could dream up — rally turbofans, classic five-spokes, even teddy bears.

Today, though, we're not here to talk about the wheels themselves. We're here to talk about the cars they go on, and that brings us to a very broad question: What car looks best on aftermarket wheels? I'll accept answers to this in two forms, either cars that most need aftermarket wheels or cars that look best on the most different designs of aftermarket wheel. Are those criteria wide-open enough for you?

For both versions of the question, I think there's a single answer: The Toyobaru, most specifically the first-generation Scion FR-S/Subaru BRZ. It's a car that looks great on lightweight track wheels like Volk TE37s or Titan 7 T-S5s, but its style is just as well-suited to a set of bags and a BBS mesh design. Even a vintage wheel like the RS Watanabe, when scaled up to modern fitment, looks great on the Toyobaru chassis.

I think the car that looks best on aftermarket wheels is the first-generation Scion FR-S/Subaru BRZ, but what do you think? Do you think the Civic most needs a new set of shoes, or that the Lexus LS400 can do no wrong with any style of wheel face? Let me know in the comments below, and I'll collect my favorite answers later in the week. Bonus points if you've tried aftermarket wheels on a chassis yourself.

#5775. Industry Voices—New majority, new targets: Where healthcare fraud enforcement goes next

Industry Voices—New majority, new targets: Where healthcare fraud enforcement goes next" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Control of Congress has changed. The bipartisan appetite for scrutinizing practices across the healthcare industry has not. A new Democratic majority will arrive with no shortage of investigative priorities and only so much time to pursue them, but recent history suggests that healthcare is likely to be among them.

Affordability dominated this election cycle, and healthcare sits at the center of that debate. Combating fraud in Medicare and Medicaid has endured as a rare point of consensus precisely because it allows policymakers of both parties to say they are protecting patients and taxpayers at once. The open question is not whether that work continues, but where it is directed.

Congressional oversight largely reflects the priorities of the majority, which controls committee agendas, hearings and subpoenas. If recent history is a guide, a Democratic majority will gravitate toward a familiar set of concerns: the practices of large insurers including those involved in Medicare Advantage, the stewardship of hospital systems, the role of private equity, vertical integration across the industry, drug pricing, and the hyper-growth sectors attracting new investment.

The administration has elevated healthcare fraud to the top of its enforcement agenda. The Justice Department’s 2026 National Health Care Fraud Takedown charged 455 defendants in schemes involving more than $6.5 billion in alleged losses, with particular emphasis on Medicaid, while CMS suspended or revoked the billing privileges of more than 2,400 providers. In an October 1 directive, DOJ’s newly created National Fraud Enforcement Division went further, designating healthcare fraud its foremost corporate enforcement priority.

That model has been defined largely by scale: the number of defendants charged and the dollar value of alleged fraud identified. It will persist regardless of which party holds Congress, carried forward by entrepreneurial prosecutors and agents. What a Democratic majority could add is a complementary lens, one that weighs not only how much fraud is charged but how the industry’s largest and fastest-growing participants are organized, owned and paid.

The best predictor of the next two years may be the last several. While Democrats controlled the Senate Permanent Subcommittee on Investigations, its October 2024 majority staff report examined post-acute care denials by the largest Medicare Advantage insurers. That inquiry did not end with the change in Senate control. In July 2026, Sen. Richard Blumenthal (D-Connecticut) and Sen. Josh Hawley (R-Missouri) sought updated data from one of those insurers on its prior authorization practices and its use of algorithms in coverage decisions. In the House, Democrats have called for closer scrutiny of insurer practices at nursing homes serving Medicare Advantage members.

Hospitals have drawn bipartisan attention as well. A January 2025 Senate Budget Committee report on private equity ownership of hospitals, led by Sen. Sheldon Whitehouse (D-Rhode Island) and Sen. Chuck Grassley (R-Iowa), raised concerns about staffing, hospital closures and distributions to investors. Months earlier, the Senate HELP Committee, then chaired by Sen. Bernie Sanders (I-Vermont), subpoenaed the chief executive of a bankrupt, private equity-backed hospital system, and the full Senate voted unanimously to hold him in contempt after he declined to testify.

Concern extends to the structure of the industry itself. In May 2026, Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-Massachusetts) and Sen. Josh Hawley, joined by House members from both parties, reintroduced legislation that would require companies that own pharmacy benefit managers to divest their pharmacies, a measure that would force significant restructuring at some of the largest vertically integrated healthcare companies. Drug pricing has drawn similar scrutiny. In September 2024, the HELP Committee called the chief executive of a leading manufacturer of GLP-1 drugs to testify about U.S. prices for its diabetes and weight-loss treatments.

To the extent healthcare commands the new majority’s attention, the tools at its disposal are considerable. Committee chairs can compel documents and testimony by subpoena, require executives to testify under oath and publish staff reports that place internal records in the public domain.

Committees’ influence extends well beyond the hearing room. They can call on the Government Accountability Office and the HHS Office of Inspector General to examine particular programs, press CMS for data and refer matters to the Justice Department. Through legislation and appropriations, they can push for reforms in areas such as prior authorization, Medicare Advantage payment and transparency in private equity ownership. Oversight does not generate cases on its own, but it shapes where agencies look, which documents surface, and what insiders conclude is worth reporting. It shapes public opinion as well. Under the glare of hearings and headlines, a misstep that might once have been resolved quietly can escalate into a reputational and regulatory crisis.

Scrutiny of private equity tends to follow the sectors where investment has moved fastest. Applied behavior analysis (ABA) therapy for children with autism illustrates the dynamic. The underlying demand is genuine: the CDC estimates that one in 31 children has autism spectrum disorder. Private equity firms have acquired more than 500 autism therapy centers over the past decade, and CMS reported in August that Medicaid and CHIP spending on ABA rose 421% between 2021 and 2025, far outpacing the 67% growth in the number of children served.

Whatever the causes of that gap, regulators have begun to respond. CMS issued a toolkit to help states strengthen oversight of ABA services, New York halved Medicaid reimbursement for certain ABA services in October, citing program integrity concerns, and Indiana paused new ABA provider enrollment. Sectors that combine rapid growth, substantial public funding and concentrated investor ownership are natural subjects of congressional interest, whichever party is in the majority.

Should the emphasis shift, Medicare Advantage is the likeliest starting point, given the sustained attention already paid to prior authorization, post-acute care denials, algorithm-driven coverage decisions and risk adjustment. Beyond it, the record points to three broader fronts that track how the industry is organized, owned, and paid. The first is integration, as lawmakers ask whether combining insurers, pharmacy benefit managers, pharmacies and providers under one corporate roof serves patients. The second is ownership, as committees examine how private equity affects staffing, billing and quality at hospitals and in hyper-growth sectors in healthcare. The third is pricing, particularly for prescription drugs.

For companies in these areas, the practical consequence is that exposure can arrive before any agency acts, and congressional and Justice Department scrutiny tend to reinforce each other. A single congressional letter can place internal records and decision-making in public view, and the attention that follows tends to encourage potential whistleblowers to come forward. Those insiders have strong incentives to take their concerns to the Justice Department as well. Under the False Claims Act, a whistleblower who files a qui tam suit may receive between 15% and 30% of the government’s recovery, and DOJ’s Corporate Whistleblower Awards Pilot Program offers awards for original information about criminal healthcare fraud, including fraud against private insurers.

The overlap in subject matter is considerable. The joint DOJ-HHS False Claims Act Working Group has named Medicare Advantage and drug pricing among its enforcement priorities, the same areas that have drawn sustained congressional interest. Testimony and documents made public through oversight can become a road map for relators’ counsel and prosecutors alike, and many healthcare fraud cases still originate with an insider.

For companies and investors, the prudent response does not depend on who holds the gavel. It begins with a clear-eyed understanding of where the business’s risks reside, followed by rigorous testing of controls in the areas of fastest growth and highest reimbursement, and a willingness to address problems early, with the benefit of counsel where the facts are uncertain. Enforcement priorities shift with each election. The expectation that companies will identify and remedy their own problems does not.

Kevin Lowell is a member at Miller & Chevalier and previously served as deputy chief of the National Fraud Enforcement Division at the U.S. Department of Justice.

#5774. Intensive Blood Pressure Control Gets a Win in Stroke Survivors

— But no significant benefit of a systolic BP target below 120 mm Hg observed in diabetes trial

Two large trials conducted in Brazil in different patient populations came to somewhat divergent conclusions on the cardiovascular benefits of intensive blood pressure (BP) lowering.

Over roughly 4 years of median follow-up, intensive treatment targeting a systolic BP below 120 mm Hg in patients with a previous stroke or transient ischemic attack (TIA) significantly reduced the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events compared with treatment targeting a BP below 140 mm Hg (10.9% vs 13.5%, P=0.007), reported Otavio Berwanger, MD, PhD, of Imperial College London, at the American Heart Association (AHA) annual meeting in Chicago.

"We had one price to pay here that offset the benefits," he said, noting a potential increase in the composite renal endpoint, primarily driven by a sustained drop in renal function, making it likely that this was a hemodynamic effect. There was no increase in the more serious renal endpoints of renal mortality, end-stage renal disease, or need for dialysis.

Conversely, in a trial of patients with type 2 diabetes and high cardiovascular risk also reported by Berwanger, there was no effect of the same intensive BP lowering strategy over a similar timeframe on the same primary endpoint -- a composite of death from cardiovascular causes, nonfatal myocardial infarction, nonfatal stroke, or hospitalization for unstable angina or heart failure -- with an incidence of 6% versus 6.7% with the standard systolic BP target of below 140 mm Hg (P=0.20).

The results of the studies -- OPTIMAL-STROKE and OPTIMAL-DIABETES -- were published simultaneously in the New England Journal of Medicine.

Commenting on the results of the stroke trial, Cheryl Bushnell, MD, of Wake Forest University in Winston-Salem, North Carolina, called this a landmark trial, and applauded what the researchers were able to accomplish in terms of intensive BP lowering in this population.

"A lot of times when patients are discharged from the hospital, they have carotid stenosis, and middle cerebral artery stenosis, and everybody's nervous about lowering their blood pressure too much," she said. "So this kind of abolishes some of the concerns that a lot of neurologists and primary care physicians have had about cerebral perfusion."

Current guidelines recommend a target BP of 130/80 mm Hg in this population, Bushnell noted, but OPTIMAL-STROKE now suggests that shooting for a target systolic BP of below 120 mm Hg can improve outcomes. "We know what we need to do, but it's not the what, it's the how," she said. "Managing blood pressure in stroke patients is incredibly difficult, and somebody has to own that as a clinician."

AHA discussant Daichi Shimbo, MD, from Columbia University Irving Medical Center in New York City, agreed that implementation of these findings will be a challenge.

"If you look at these data, it's likely that a substantial portion of individuals did not achieve a systolic blood pressure less than 120 in the intensive arm," Shimbo said. "What I conclude from this is, even in a beautifully conducted trial, it is really hard to get systolic blood pressure less than 120, and I would argue, it's probably even more difficult to do it in clinical practice. So, my takeaway point is, if a target systolic blood pressure less than 120 is recommended in a future guideline, which I suspect it will be, guidance should be provided in how best to achieve this target in clinical practice."

He also pointed to the renal outcomes, noting that the number needed to treat suggested that for every 1,000 patients treated intensively for about 4 years, 26 will benefit and 17 will be harmed. "So the question I have is, will clinicians feel comfortable with this risk-benefit ratio? Of course, the benefit is [major adverse cardiovascular events] reduction, and the risk is just a creatinine bump, but one wonders what clinicians will feel about this trial."

On the other hand, Berwanger noted, the results of the diabetes trial "should be interpreted as a neutral trial, of course, but it's not the only trial."

In a pre-specified meta-analysis of these findings with previous trials looking at the same question (BPROAD, conducted in China, and ACCORD BP in North America), "there was a 16% relative reduction in the risk of major cardiovascular events," he said. "What this is saying is that potentially, there is a reduction in cardiovascular events in this population, but it's attenuated."

"No trial in isolation will be able to detect a reduction of between 10% and 15%, more or less," Berwanger added, "so you need to consider the totality of the evidence."

AHA commenter Shawna Nesbitt, MD, from the University of Texas Southwestern Medical Center in Dallas, pointed out that in previous trials, including BPROAD, women had less significant BP reduction compared with men. The larger population of women in this trial (62%), "may have skewed the trial a little bit to having a little less significance earlier on," Nesbitt said. "My opinion would be that if you follow them out longer, you more than likely see the same results."

"I think it very strongly suggests that this trial supports, rather than refutes, what we already have seen in other trials of treating hypertension more aggressively in patients with diabetes," she added.

OPTIMAL-STROKE was a multicenter, open-label trial carried out at 30 sites in Brazil. A total of 4,368 patients with elevated BP and previous ischemic stroke or TIA were randomly assigned 1:1 to receive intensive treatment with a target systolic BP of less than 120 mm Hg or standard treatment with a target of less than 140 mm Hg.

After randomization, investigators adjusted antihypertensive therapy according to treatment assignment and prespecified algorithms. Additional monthly visits were arranged as needed in the intensive-treatment group to achieve the target BP, according to the study investigators.

During follow-up, excluding the first 3 months, the mean systolic BP was 122.0 mm Hg in the intensive group and 135.2 mm Hg in the standard group.

The composite renal outcome -- sustained reduction of ≥50% in estimated glomerular filtration rate or progression to end-stage renal disease -- occurred in 3.9% of patients in the intensive-therapy group and 2.2% of patients in the standard group (HR 1.76, 95% CI 1.24-2.50). The overall incidence of serious adverse events was similar between groups, with rates of 15.1% and 15.3%, respectively.

OPTIMAL-DIABETES, an open-label trial also conducted in Brazil, included 9,476 patients with elevated BP, type 2 diabetes, and high cardiovascular risk. They were randomly assigned in a 1:1 ratio to either the intensive strategy or the standard treatment strategy.

The mean systolic BP during follow-up, again excluding the first 3 months, was 121.8 mm Hg in the intensive group and 135.4 mm Hg with standard therapy. Serious adverse events occurred in 14.9% of patients with intensive therapy and 14.6% of patients with standard therapy.

#5773. Mayo Clinic Platform launches AI-driven clinical decision support tool

Mayo Clinic Platform launches AI-driven clinical decision support tool" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Mayo Clinic launched an AI-enabled clinical decision support solution embedded directly in electronic health record workflows. The CDS solution will put Mayo’s clinical knowledge and evidence-informed guidance at the point of care, rather than requiring clinicians to search across separate resources.

Mayo Clinic Platform, a strategic initiative from the Rochester, Minn.-based health system, developed the tool, and it will be expanded and updated over time, executives said. The solution draws on multiple clinical tools, reducing the need for clinicians to navigate independent resources to find trusted clinical information.

Mayo Clinic is making the CDS tool available to Mayo Clinic clinicians as well as healthcare organizations participating in Mayo Clinic Platform's provider programs. Mayo Clinic is essentially turning its clinical expertise into scalable AI infrastructure available beyond its own health system.

Beginning in November, eligible clinicians will have access to two key capabilities: a specialty-specific chat that covers more than 35 specialties and 22 clinical calculators available via chat or voice dictation.

Powered by a large language model, the specialty-specific AI chat tool surfaces relevant information to help clinicians reason through complex cases and cites responses from peer-reviewed literature. The tool is updated daily, giving clinicians access to relevant emerging evidence alongside established medical knowledge, executives said. The tool helps clinicians quickly answer patient-specific clinical questions, explore differential diagnoses and evaluate care options. And the specialty-specific AI chat mirrors Mayo Clinic's subspecialty model of care.

The clinical calculators, spanning cardiology, oncology, hepatology, endocrinology and other specialty use cases, provide industry-standard medical information and evidence-based decision support on clinical questions and patient care context. Proprietary Mayo Clinic-developed calculators are included in this tool, according to the health system.

"Clinicians have access to a vast amount of medical knowledge, but finding relevant information for individualized patient care takes valuable time," said Maneesh Goyal, Mayo Clinic Platform chief operating officer, in a statement. "With this new solution, we are bringing trusted Mayo Clinic knowledge directly into the clinical workflow so clinicians can spend less time searching for information across multiple resources and more time focused on patient care."

The platform is "designed to evolve" as new tools are added to Mayo Clinic Platform, executives say.

David C. and Flora C. Pratt Distinguished Chief Medical Officer for Mayo Clinic Platform Gelareh Zadeh, M.D., Ph.D., said in a statement the solution "does not replace clinical judgment."

"Mayo Clinic physicians have provided feedback on the different tools to ensure the solution supports clinicians and reflects Mayo Clinic's approach to patient care," Zadeh said.

#5772. What Is a Great Google Quality Real Estate Score: A Comprehensive Guide

Navigating the world of Google Ads can be challenging, especially in an industry as competitive as real estate. One term you've probably heard in relation to Google Ads is "Quality Score." This score is crucial to how your ads perform and how much they cost. But what does it mean for realtors, and what constitutes a great Google Quality Real Estate Score? Let's dive in.

Google Ads Quality Score is essentially a measurement of the quality and relevance of both your keywords and PPC ads. It is a dynamic metric, assigned on a scale of 1 to 10, with 10 being the best possible score. It is used by Google to determine your cost per click (CPC) and your ad rank in the ad auction process.

Quality Score is calculated based on three primary components: click-through rate (CTR), ad relevance, and landing page experience.

For any industry, including real estate, a good Quality Score is generally around 7 or above. However, in the competitive world of real estate, striving for an even higher Quality Score could give you an advantage. So, for realtors, a great Quality Score might be considered 8 or above.

That said, keep in mind that a high-Quality Score is not an end in itself. It's a sign that your ads are relevant, and your landing pages provide a good user experience, but it's still just one factor in the overall success of your Google Ads campaigns.

A high-Quality Score is beneficial for several reasons:

Improving your Quality Score is all about relevance and user experience. Here are some strategies:

A high Google Ads Quality Score can be a strong asset for realtors. It not only helps you rank higher and pay less for clicks but also ensures your ads are shown to the most relevant audiences. However, remember that Quality Score is just one piece of the puzzle. You should also consider other factors like your bidding strategy and overall account performance to create a successful Google Ads campaign.

With continuous effort and strategic planning, you can optimize your Quality Score and ultimately generate more leads for your real estate business.
